UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
NORTHERN DISTRICT OF ILLINOIS 
Civil Action No. 95 C 4006 
Jury Trial Demanded 
PAUL FELZEN, TRUSTEE OF 
Louise LASKIN TRUsT—1991, 
Plaintiff, 
Vv. 
DwayYNe O. ANDREAS, RALPH Bruce, JOHN H. DANIELS, 
Howarp G. BUFFETT, LOWELL W. ANDREAS, JAMES 
R. RANDALL, MICHAEL D. ANDREAS, H.D. HALE, O. 
GLEN WEBB and MARTIN ANDREAS, 
Defendants, 
-and- 
ARCHER DANIELS MIDLAND COMPANY, 
a Delaware corporation, 
Nominal Defendant. 
COMPLAINT 
Plaintiff, 
for its Complaint brought derivatively on 
behalf of nominal defendant Archer Daniels Midland 
Company (“ADM” or “the Company”), alleges upon in- 
formation and belief, inter alia, based upon an investiga- 
tion conducted by and through its attorneys except as to 
paragraph 6, which is alleged upon knowledge: 
35 
NATURE OF THE ACTION 
1. This is a stockholder’s derivative action brought 
on behalf of ADM against certain of the Company’s 
present and former officers, 
directors 
and employees. 
Plaintiff complains of a course of conduct by defendants 
which caused ADM to systematically engage in price- 
fixing activities in violation of federal antitrust laws, and 
to fail to disclose information concerning such conduct, 
in violation of federal securities law. 
2. As set forth in greater detail below, this Complaint 
alleges that the Company, through its officers and direc- 
tors, has grossly mismanaged and failed to properly 
supervise its various divisions, employees, and agents and 
has made decisions relative to the business, operations 
and corporate governance of the Company itself and its 
affiliates which have caused and/or threatened to cause 
substantial losses to the Company and other damages. 
3. The entire ADM Board of Directors knew or 
recklessly disregarded the fact that these violations were 
taking place. ADM’s Board of Directors failed to ade- 
quately oversee ADM’s officers and employees, failed to 
prevent their wrongdoing despite ample opportunity to 
do so, and failed to create a compliance or auditing pro- 
gram to prevent illegal activities or to detect them after 
they occurred. 
4. Because defendants’ illegal conduct was discovered 
by federal law enforcement officials, ADM and certain of 
its executives have been subjected to an extensive grand 
jury investigation, and the Company has seen its stock 
price decline and suffered significant damage to its repu- 
tation and goodwill. 
JURISDICTION AND VENUE 
5. This Court has jurisdiction over this action pur- 
suant to 15 U.S.C. §§ 1, 4, 15, 26, 78n(a) and 78aa, 

36 
and 28 U.S.C. § 1331. Venue is proper in this District 
pursuant to 28 U.S.C. § 1391(b) because a substantial 
part of the events giving rise to the claims occurred in 
this District. This action is not a collusive one to confer 
jurisdiction on a court of the United States which it 
would not otherwise have. 
PARTIES 
6. Paul Felzen is a trustee of plaintiff Louise Laskin 
Trust—1991 (the “Trust”). The Trust owns and at all 
relevant times herein has continuously owned shares of 
common 
stock of ADM. 
Plaintiff 
brings 
this action 
derivatively on behalf of nominal defendant ADM. 
7. Nominal defendant ADM is a public corporation 
organized under the laws of Delaware with its principal 
place of business at 4666 Faries Parkway, Decatur, Illi- 
nois. ADM is engaged in the business, among other 
things, of processing and merchandizing agricultural com- 
modities and products. ADM’s products include high fruc- 
tose corn syrup (which is used in soft drinks and other 
food products), amino acid lysine (which is used as an 
animal feed supplement), and citric acid (which is used 
in the pharmaceutical and food and beverage industries). 
ADM’s common shares are registered pursuant to Section 
12 of the Securities and Exchange Act of 1934, 15 U.S.C. 
§ 781, and trade on the New York Stock Exchange. 
8. For its fiscal year ended June 30, 1994, ADM re- 
ported net earnings of more than $484 million on sales of 
over $11.3 billion. ADM is named as a nominal defend- 
ant herein solely in its derivative capacity and this action 
is brought on its behalf and no claims are asserted against 
it. 
9. Defendant Dwayne O. Andreas is Chairman of the 
Board and Chief Executive Officer of ADM, and has been 
a member of ADM’s Board of Directors since 1966. 
In 
1994, Dwayne Andreas received from ADM a salary of 
37 
$2,972,630 and other annual compensation of 
at least 
$198,000.

sation of at least $75,000 from ADM. 
11. Defendant John H. Daniels has been a director 
of ADM since 1957, and is a former Chairman of the

least $100,000 from ADM. 
r

ch of the foregoing individual defendants, be- 
ae “% 
his position - an officer and/or director of 
ADM, owes fiduciary duties to the Company and its 
stockholders in connection with the operations, manage- 
ment and supervision of the Company. As officers and/or 
directors of a publicly held company whose stock is reg- 
istered with the Securities and Exchange Commission 
under the Securities and Exchange Act of 1934, the in- 
dividual defendants had a duty to disseminate accurate 
and truthful information promptly with respect to the 
Company’s operations and management, so that the mar- 
ket price of ADM securities would be based on truthful 
and accurate information and to insure that the Com- 
pany’s business was conducted in accordance with federal 
and state law. 
20. The individual 
defendants 
participated 
in _the 
wrongdoing complained of herein in order to (i) continue 
and prolong the illusion of ADM’s ongoing growth and 
profitability and its success as a market leader in the 
agricultural products business, 
(ii) inflate the price of 
ADM’s common stock, (iii) conceal the adverse facts 
concerning ADM’s illegal conduct, lack of adequate inter- 
nal controls, and management and future prospects, and 
(iv) protect and perpetuate their directoral or executive 
positions and the substantial compensation and prestige 
they obtained thereby. 
CAUSE OF ACTION 
21. Plaintiff repeats and reallages [sic] each of the 
foregoing allegations as if set forth fully herein. 
39 
22. ADM is currently the target of a Chicago-based 
federal grand jury investigation into what the Company 
has publicly termed “possible violations of federal anti- 
trust laws and related crimes in the food additives in- 
dustry.” 
23. The criminal probe, which is focusing on ADM’s 
pricing activities with respect to high fructose corn syrup, 
amino acid lysine, and citric acid, has been substantially 
aided by the efforts of an inside “whistle-blower”—-ADM’s 
BioProducts Division president, Mark E. Whitacre, who, 
armed with FBI-supplied tape equipment, has helped fed- 
eral investigators secretly record conversations and meet- 
ings between ADM’s representatives and its competitors 
reflecting price fixing activities violative of federal anti- 
trust law. 
24. Federal investigators have also issued subpoenas, 
and obtained a ses"ch warrant, to obtain from the Com- 
pany additional information concerning its illegal pricing 
practices. 
Additional subpoenas have been served upon 
ADM executives and managers as well as certain of the 
Company’s competitors. 
25. Neither these pricing practices nor any informa- 
tion concerning the practices was disclosed by ADM in 
its Proxy Statement dated September 14, 1994, which the 
Company’s directors, including the individual defendants 
herein, caused ADM to publicly disseminate to ADM’s 
shareholders in order to solicit votes for the election of 
ADM 
directors, 
including 
the 
individual 
defendants 
herein. The absence of such disclosure rendered the state- 
ments contained in the Proxy Statement materially mis- 
leading, in violation of Section 14( a) of the Securities 
and Exchange Act of 1934, 15 U.S.C. § 14n(a), and the 
rules promulgated thereunder by the Securities and Ex- 
change Commission. 
26. Each of the individual defendants owed ADM and 
its stockholders fiduciary duties of care, candor and loy- 

40 
alty and the duty to exercise, in the performance of their 
responsibilities as directors, reasonable care in the over- 
sight and supervision of ADM’s corporate affairs and 
management. 
The individual defendants violated those 
duties through their conflicting interests, lack of independ- 
ence and intentional or reckless acts which caused or 
contributed to the commission of the conduct complained 
of herein in violation of Section 1 of the Sherman Anti- 
trust Act, 15 U.S.C. § 1, and Section 14(a) of the Se- 
curities and Exchange Act of 1934, 15 U.S.C. § 78n(a), 
and the rules promulgated thereunder by the Securities 
and Exchange Commission. 
27. Based on the foregoing, the individual defendants 
have violated their fiduciary duties of care, loyalty, rea- 
sonable inquiry, oversight, good faith and supervision. 
28. The individual defendants knowingly or recklessly 
caused ADM to engage in the wrongful conduct described 
herein, or were reckless or grossly negligent in failing to 
prevent ADM from engaging in such conduct and to as- 
certain and avert such misconduct. 
29. The individual defendants’ breaches of fiduciary 
duty have proximately caused ADM to suffer damages in 
an amount yet to be determined. 
30. Plaintiff has no adequate remedy at law. 
31. 
Plaintiff has not made a demand on ADM’s board 
of directors to pursue the claims herein, because such a 
demand would have been a futile act for the reasons set 
forth below: 
(a) The board itself is accused of a breach of its fidu- 
ciary duties and its members are implicated in and liable 
for abdicating their responsibility to the Company and its 
shareholders and for failing to put into place adequate 
internal controls and means of supervision to prevent the 
wrongful conduct referred to herein. 
41 
(b) A reasonable doubt exists that ADM’s directors 
exercised valid business judgment because the wrongful 
actions and/or inactions by the individual defendants 
alleged herein amounted to breaches of their fiduciary 
duties of good faith, disclosure, due care and loyalty to 
ADM and its stockholders, and an abdication of the 
directors’ responsibilities giving rise to liability 
to ADM. 
(c) The ADM directors’ actions and omissions, which 
resulted in ADM’s improper activities, cannot have been 
the product of valid business judgment. 
(d) At least a majority of the present ADM directors 
has a disabling conflict by virtue of their personal liability 
as defendants for the damages caused to ADM by their 
alleged wrongdoing and, therefore, could not be relied 
upon to reach a truly independent decision as to whether 
to commence an action against themselves and other per- 
sons responsible for the wrongdoing complained of herein. 
(e) Each of the directors receives 
ts, benefits 
and other emoluments by virtue of his 
position 
with the 
Company. The directors are interested parties and cannot 
in good faith exercise independent business judgment in 
determining whether to bring this action against them- 
__(f) As a general matter in recent years, insurance 
l- 
icles covering the liability of an insured’s officers and di- 
rectors purport to exclude legal claims asserted directly 
of Ge tanases cqpinst cach persons. To the extent the 
ompany mainta’s insurance coverage for the liability 
of any officers or directors and the policy or policies con- 
tain the foregoing exclusion, the insurance policy or poli- 
cies would not protect the officers and directors if the 
Company asserted a claim directly against such individ- 
uals. Thus, there is a substantial disincentive for the di- 
py acing 
ap 
es 
| 
aad 

42 
WHEREFORE, 
plaintiff demands judgment as follows: 
A. Against each individual defendant and in favor of

sustained by ADM as a result of the wrongful conduct 
described herein; 
B. 
uiring the individual

and its shareholders, to implk 
me it and enforce policies, 
practices and procedures, including an appropriate anti-

wrongful conduct of the type described herein; 
C. Awarding plaintiff the costs and disbursements of

fees; and 
D. Such other or further relief as the Court deems

Litigation, Consolidated Case No. 95 C 3979, generally 
involves a class action securities action, brought on behalf 
of plaintiffs who purchased ADM common stock and 
who allege violations 
of Sections 10(b) and 20 of the 
Securities Exchange Act, 15 U.S.C. §§ 78j(b) and 78t(a), 
and Rule 10b-5 of the Securities and Exchange Commis- 
sion (“SEC”), promulgated thereunder. This consolidated 
complaint essentially alleges that during the alleged class 
period of September 16, 1992, through July 10, 1995, 
ADM'’s “earnings and success were driven in large part 
by the growth of its high fructose corn syrup (“HFCS”), 
citric acid and lysine production businesses, and also by 
increased prices for these products.” 
(Cons. Compl. ¢ 7). 
Plaintiffs specifically allege that, “While the defendants 
publicly attributed this success to [ADM}]'s efficiency and 
to favorable market conditions, 
in reality, defendants 
knew that this was not the case. 
Rather, [ADM]’s suc- 
cess in the lysine, HFCS and citric acid businesses was pos- 
sible only because, since at least 1992, ADM had been 
colluding with its competitors in these markets to fix the 
worldwide production volume and prices for these prod- 
ucts in order to reap illegal and undeserved profits. The 
defendants concealed this material information from the 
public in the Company's SEC filings and public announce- 
ments in order to inflate artificially the market price of 
ADM common stock.” (/d.) 
Consolidated Case No. 95 C 4006 makes similar deriv- 
ative allegations on behalf of plaintiff ADM’s sharehold- 
ers. 
This complaint specifically alleges that the named 
director defendants “caused ADM systematically to en- 
gage in illegal price-fixing activities involving several of 
its major products in violation of the federal antitrust 
laws.” (Cons. Compl. § 1). 
At this initial point in this securities litigation, this 
Court has been called upon to determine the proper venue 
for all future proceedings in both consolidated cases. The 
ANALYSIS 
ADM seeks transfer of this action to the Central Dis- 
—— Illinois pursuant to 28 U.S.C. § 1404(a), which 
For the convenience of parties and witnesses, in the 
located. See 15 U.S.C. § 78; 28 U.S.C. § 1391(b Be 
(c). Tank Gb whi tees ak o> Oat Gee 
whether a transfer will serve the convenience of the par- 
ties, the convenience of the witnesses, and the interest 
of justice. 
In evaluating the convenience and fairness of transfer 
under § 1404(a), the Court must consider both the pri- 
the 
vate interests of the parties and the public interest of 
Court. 
Private interests include: 
(1) 
intiff’s choice 

46 
of forum, (2) the situs of material events, (3) the rela- 
tive ease of access to sources of proof in each forum 
including the court’s power to compel the appearance of 
unwilling witnesses at trial and the costs of obtaining 
the attendance of witnesses, (4) convenience to the par- 
ties—specifically, their respective residences and abilities 
to bear the expense of trial in a particular forum. 
See 
Van Holdt v. Husky Injection Molding Systems, Ltd., 
887 F. Supp. 185, 288 (N.D. Ill. 1995); G. H. Miller 
& Co. v. Hanes, 566 F. Supp. 305, 307 (N.D.IIl. 1983); 
15 C. Wright, A. Miller & E. Cooper, Federal Practice 
$§ 3849-53 (1986). 
Public interest factors include the 
court’s familiarity with applicable law, the desirability of 
resolving controversies in their locale and the principle 
that “related litigation should be transferred to a forum 
where consolidation 
is feasible.” 
Coffey v. Van Dorn 
Iron Works, 796 F.2d 217, 221 (7th Cir. 1986). 
See 
Van Gelder V. Taylor, 621 F. Supp. 613, 619 (N.D. Til. 
1985). A party seeking a § 1404(a) transfer bears the 
burden of showing that “the transferee forum is clearly 
more convenient” than the transferor forum. 
Heller Fi- 
nancial, Inc. v. Midwhey Powder Co., Inc., 883 F.2d 
1286, 1293 (7th Cir. 1989). 
There is no dispute in this case that defendant ADM 
is a public corporation organized under the laws of Dela- 
ware with its principal place of business at 4666 Faries 
Parkway, Decatur, Illinois. ADM is engaged in the busi- 
ness of, among other things, processing and merchandis- 
ing agricultural commodities and products. ADM’s three 
main products are high fructose corn syrup (which is used 
in soft drinks and other food products), amino acid lysine 
(which is used as an animal feed supplement), and citric 
acid (which is used in the pharmaceutical and food and 
beverage industries). 
All of ADM’s executive officers 
have their offices in Decatur. (Schmalz Dec., 
43). Three 
of the four meetings per year of ADM’s Board of Direc- 
tors take place in Decatur. 
(/d., 
§4). ADM’s central 
47 
recordkeeping, its corporate records, and its central com- 
puter operations are located in Decatur. 
(Jd., q 7). 
A. 
Plaintiff's Choice of Forum 
This Court has already noted on numerous occasi 
that plaintiff's choice of forum has diminished in signifi 
Pa 
since the enactment of § 1404(a). 
Van Holdt y. 
usky Injection Molding Systems, Inc., 887 F. Supp. at 
188. Moreover, the weight to be given to plaintiff's 
choice of forum is lessened if the chosen forum has rela- 
tively weak connections with the operative facts giving 
rise to the claim. Id. In this case, the parties have little 
or no real connection to this district. The plaintiffs only 
real connection to this district is that their attorneys 
happen to have offices in Chicago. 
However, the con- 
venience to plaintiffs’ counsel is not a proper considera- 
tion. 
Id. at 190; Sypert vy. Miner, 266 F.2d 196, 
199 
(7th Cir.), cert. denied, 361 US. 832 (1959): Koos, 
Inc. 
v. 
a v. Performance Industries, Inc., 747 F. Supp. 487, 

48 
Consolidated Case No. 3979 lists twenty-one different 
plaintiffs. 
(Cons. Compl. 4 11). However, there is no 
indication that these plaintiffs are residents of this district. 
Consolidated Case No. 95 C 4006 lists two plaintiffs. 
Plaintiff Paul Felzen is a trustee of plaintiff Louise Laskin 
Trust—1991, which is located in the State of Florida. 
(Cons. Compl. 4 12). The other plaintiff, Sandra Esner, 
is a resident of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania. Cons. 
Compl. ¢ 13). 
The Court’s review of the plaintiffs’ choice of forum 
leads it to conclude that this factor favors the transfer 
of this case to the defendants’ home district which has a 
more substantive relationship to this cause of action. 
B. 
Situs Of Material Events 
This Court especially reviews the situs of material 
events in deciding a motion to transfer venue to determine 
whether a lawsuit has any material relationship to this 
district. The Court’s review of this factor strongly favors 
the transfer of this case. The consolidated securities com- 
plaint principally alleges omissions of material informa- 
tion related to alleged criminal antitrust which should have 
been made by ADM. 
The consolidated derivative com- 
plaint alleges that ADM’s directors breached duties to 
ADM by failing to supervise its employees in a manner 
which would have prevented their alleged criminal anti- 
trust violations. 
Because ADM’s headquarters are in Decatur, ADM’s 
Board of Directors meets in Decatur for three of its four 
yearly meetings; and all of ADM’s executive offices are 
located in Decatur. This Court, therefore, concludes that 
the Central District of Mlinois is the situs of events giving 
rise to the two consolidated complaints currently pending 
before this Court. Moreover, ADM’s divisions that pro- 
duce the three products which are the subject of the three 
pending grand jury investigations are headquartered in 
49 
Decatur. The Court’s careful review of the two consoli- 
dated complaints gives this Court no reason to believe 
that any material events related to this litigation occurred 
in the Northern District of Illinois. At best, the consoli- 
aa derivative complaint obliquely states that: “[mJany 
acts and transactions giving rise to the violations 
complained of herein, including the preparation and dis- 
semination to the investing public of false and misleading 
si ae occurred in the District.” 
(Cons. 
- 
However, this broad allegation would 
to the Central Distsat of Milcit a6 
stall as any ce 
federal districts where the alleged international criminal 
antitrust conspiracy occurred. Given the undisputed facts 
of this case, which include the unassailable fact that 
ADM'’s offices and operations are headquartered in De- 
catur, this Court concludes that its consideration of the 
material situs of these consolidated complaints strongly 
favors a transfer of thi 
* 
eet 
Illinois. 
is case to the Central District of 
C. Convenience of the Witnesses and Parties 
The Court next examines the convenience of 
i 
nesses and parties. The Court must determine he ee 
ease of access to sources of proof in each forum. ADM 
submits that since all of its offices and relevant employees 
are in Decatur, this factor weighs heavily in their favor 
The plaintiffs have not and can not rebut this fact. In. 
Stead, plaintiffs merely allege that Chicago is a more con- 
venient transportation point than the Central District of 
Illinois. 
The plaintiffs have not specified any witnesses 
who may be unavailable in the Central District of Illinois 
This Court therefore concludes that the convenience of 
witness factor favors the transf. 
i 
ard auger cs tee: 
ransfer of this case to the Cen- 
The plaintiffs strongly rely on the fact 
tha the gra 
. 
. 
. 
. 
ct 
jury which is investigating possible potrne rd eo 
activities in the lysine market is located in Chicago. The 

50 
Court specifically finds that this fact does not justify 
venue of these two consolidated securities cases in this 
district. 
First, this Court recognizes a clear distinction 
between the legal issues involved in the criminal antitrust 
investigation and the legal issues in these two consolidated 
civil securities cases. 
While there may be some factual 
overlap between the factual investigation of this criminal 
grand jury and the litigants’ efforts in these consolidated 
securities cases, this overlap does not translate into any 
conveniences for the witnesses in this litigation because it 
is highly unlikely that there will be any workable coordi- 
nation between the criminal and civil investigations. 
In 
fact, it is highly likely that no effective civil discovery can 
proceed until the Department of Justice concludes 
its 
criminal investigations. 
Furthermore, the fact that the 
Chicago grand jury is only investigating one of ADM’s 
three major product lines further diminishes the likelihood 
that any effective coordination can occur between the 
two consolidated cases and the criminal grand jury sitting 
in Chicago. 
Finally, in plaintiffs’ last-ditch efforts to convince this 
Court that venue is appropirate in this district they have 
noted that: 
(1) one of the key witnesses in this litigation 
who formerly worked for ADM, Mark Whitacre, is pres- 
ently working in the Northern District of Illinois and 
(2) ADM has filed a brief with the Multi-District Litiga- 
tion (“MDL”) Panel which concedes that the civil anti- 
trust MDL cases involving the lysine product should be 
transferred to the Northern District of Illinois. 
Neither 
of these two arguments. when closely examined are per- 
suasive to the Court. 
With respect to Mar’ Whitacre, the plaintiffs have not 
asserted that his testimony will not be available to them 
in the Central District of Ilinois. In fact, the probability 
is that Mr. Whitacre will never testify in any of the civil 
cases and that the parties will instead rely on any testi- 
mony offered by Mr. Whitacre in any public criminal 
51 
proceedings 
that may occur in the future. Moreover, there 
is nO guarantee 
that Mr. Whitacre 
will be a resident of 
this district if and when these two consolidated civil 
actions reach the trial stage. 
The Court also believes that ADM’s recent MDL plead- 
ing, which relates to numerous related civil antitrust cases, 
does not change its analysis under the venue factors which 
must be considered by the Court. The Court’s close re- 
view of this MDL pleading indicates that ADM has essen- 
tially never wavered from its position that most of its 
civil litigation should proceed in the Central District of 
Illinois. In this MDL pleading, ADM merely has decided 
that it should follow the same strategic decision of the 
J ustice Department and similarly urge a separate consoli- 
dation of all civil antitrust cases along its three separate 
product lines. Given this strategy, it is not surprising at 
all that ADM in its MDL filing seeks to have the citric 
acid and/or HFCS antitrust actions consolidated in the 
Central District of Illinois. In view of this request and to 
preserve the separate integrity of the HFCS and lysine 
cases, ADM reluctantly supported the transfer of all 
lysine civil antitrust to this district after initially request- 
ing that these lysine cases be sent to the Central District 
of Illinois. 
Thus, this MDL filing, when viewed in its 
Proper context, does not suggest or concede that this dis- 
trict is the proper venue for the consolidated litigation 
pending before this Court. 
In fact, factual information 
contained in this MDL pleading merely reiterates that the 
three Products at issue in the related criminal and civil 
litigation are principally manufactured 
in the Central 
District of Illinois. 
Thus, the Court’s own objective re- 
view of ADM’s MDL pleading leads the Court to con- 
clude that this MDL pleading supports, rather than weak- 
ens, ADM’s motion to transfer venue of these two con- 
solidated cases to the Central District of Illinois. 

52 
C. The Overall Interests of Justice 
Finally, the Court has independently reviewed whether 
the overall interests of justice would be served by a trans- 
fer of venue. 
All of the factors reviewed to this point 
compel the transfer of the venue to the Central District 
of Illinois, the district having the most meaningful rela- 
tionship to these two consolidated actions. Here, the over- 
all interests of justice certainly favor a transfer to the 
Central District of Illinois to facilitate further consolida- 
tion with similar cases now pending there.* 
Coffey v. 
Van Dorn Iron Works, 796 F.2d 217, 221 
(7th Cir. 
1986). 
The plaintiffs’ main argument against the transfer of 
th. 
two consolidated cases is that ADM will secure some 
sort of “home-court” advantage. 
This argument asserts 
too much. 
If the Court was to accept the logic of this 
argument our American system of jurisprudence would 
completely breakdown. 
The essence of the plaintiffs’ as- 
sertion is that they will not be able to receive a fair trial, 
if these consolidated cases ever reach that stage, in ADM’s 
home district. 
The fallacy of this assertion has been 
proven false over and over again by jury verdicts returned 
against large corporation [sic] in their own home districts 
throughout the history of our jurisprudence. This Court 
flatly rejects the assertion that its esteemed colleague in 
the Central District of Illinois will not be able to properly 
monitor jury selection in a fashion that will ensure that 
all parties in this case receive a fair trial. 
Plaintiffs’ allegations of prejudice in the Central Dis- 
trict of Illinois are also ill founded. 
The Danville Divi- 
sion of the Central District of Illinois, in which Decatur 
2 While plaintiffs’ counsel asserts that the current plaintiffs in 
the Central District of Illinois would be willing to accept a transfer 
to this Court, this factor carries little, if any, weight with this 
Court because this simply represents an accommodation between 
plaintiffs’ counsel rather than an acknowledgment that venue is 
improper in the Central District of Illinois. 
53 
is located, comprises 12 counties with a population 
of 
679,000. Even if all of ADM’s 1,800 employees and 
their friends, relatives and acquaintances were automati- 
cally excluded from the relevant jury pool, it defies reality 
to believe that eight fair and impartial persons, a typical 
civil jury of six persons and two alternates, could not be 
found in Danville to render a decision in this case. 
CONCLUSION 
This Court finds that these two consolidated actions 
bear no meaningful relationship to this district and there- 
fore should be transferred 
to the Danville Division 
of the 
Central District of Illinois for further consolidation with 
the related pending cases in Danville. Defendants’ motion 
for transfer of consolidated cases No. 95 C 3939 and 
No. 95 C 4006 is granted. 
All further litigation matt 
should be brought before the transferee Cont

THE DUTIES OF THE ADM DIRECTORS 
AND OFFICERS 
16. Each of the Director Defendants owed the Com- 
pany and its shareholders the duty to exercise a high 
degree of care, loyalty and diligence in the performance 
of his or her individual responsibilities with respect to the 
management and administration of the affairs of ADM, 
as well as in the use and preservation of its property and 
assets. Each of the Director Defendants approved and/or 
ratified anti-competitive activities and illegal foreign pay- 
ments. 
The conduct of the defendants complained of 
herein involved, inter alia, the following: 
(a) knowing 
and culpable violation of their obligations as directors 
and/or officers of ADM; (b) the absence of good faith 
on their part; and (c) a reckless disregard for their duties 
to the Company and its shareholders of which the defend- 
ants were aware, or should have been aware, that caused 
serious injury to the Company, allowed these defendants 
to obtain personal benefits, and subjected the Company 
to substantial legal exposure. 
17. To discharge their legal duties, the Director De- 
fendants were required to exercise reasonable and prudent 
supervision over the Company’s management, 
policies, 
practices, controls, and financial affairs. Pursuant to their 
fiduciary obligations, the Director Defendants are required 
to use the same care and diligence as would an ordinary 
prudent person in a similar position.

B. 
Other Reasons Why The Directors Are Not Disin- 
terested And Cannot Be Objective In Evaluating 
The Merits Of A Demand 
29. Additional reasons for each individual director's 
lack of independence in considering a demand is by vir- 
tue of his or her participation in, approval of, and/or 
reckless disregard of the alleged wrongdoing. 
Therefore, 
each defendant is fatally interested in the outcome of any 
litigation brought against the ADM Board as a whole. 
Such profound interest is manifest as follows: 
(a) the entire ADM Board of Directors participated 
in and/or were fully aware of the deliberative process 
its shareholders, and an abdication of their responsibilities 
as directors, which breaches and abdication gave rise to 
ADM'’s liability for the illegal wrongdoing; 
(c) the members of the ADM Board of Directors 
receiving payments, 
benefits, stock options and an 
emoluments by virtue of their positions and control of 
ADM, have benefited from the wrongdoing and have 
engaged, authorized and/or covered up such conduct to 
Preserve their positions of control. The Board members 
also have close personal and business ties with each other 
as well ( as set forth above) and, consequently are inter- 
ested parties and cannot in good faith exercise independ- 
ent business judgment to determine 
bring 
thi 
action against themselves. 
soa 
(d) ADM'’s officers and directors 
ar 
personal liability for their acts of vod merce g 
i 
breach of fiduciary duty by directors’ and officers’ liability 
insurance. The Director Defendants caused the Compan 
to purchase that insurance for their protection with ~ 
Porate funds, i.e., monies belonging to the stockholders 
of ADM. 
However, the directors’ and officers’ liability 
insurance policies covering the defendants in this case 
contain an “insured versus insured exclusion,” a provision 
which eliminates coverage for any action brought directly 

76 
by ADM against the Director Defendants. As a result, if 
these directors were to sue themselves or certain of the 
officers of ADM, or to take over this suit and prosecute 
it, there would be no directors’ and officers’ insurance 
protection. The Director Defendants would subject them- 
selves, their relatives and their friends and colleagues to 
a judgment in the millions of dollars that would be pay- 
able from their individual assets alone. This is a further 
reason why they will not bring such a suit, or would not 
prosecute any such action vigorously. On the other hand, 
if the suit is brought derivatively, as this action is brought, 
such insurance coverage exists and will provide a basis 
for the Company to recover for the Director Defendants’ 
wrongdoing. 
30. 
Plaintiffs have made no demand on stockholders 
of ADM to institute this action because such demand 
would be a futile and useless act for the following reasons: 
(a) ADM is a publicly traded company with many 
thousands of stockholders; 
(b) making demand on such a large number of stock- 
holders would be impossible for plaintiffs who have no 
way of finding out the names, addresses or phone numbers 
of stockholders; and 
(c) assuming 
all stockholders could be individually 
identified, making demand on all stockholders would force 
plaintiffs to incur huge expenses. 
THE SPECIAL COMMITTEE AND CORPORATE 
GOVERNANCE COMMITTEE OF THE 
BOARD OF DIRECTORS 
The Special Committee 
31. The Director Defendants’ failure to take any action 
on behalf of ADM prior to the filing of this derivative 
action constitutes further evidence that demand is futile. 
In response to the investigation by the Department of 
Justice (“DOJ”), 
the filing of the derivative lawsuits and 
77 
the class action lawsuits for violations of the federal secu- 
rities and antitrust laws, on or about July 19, 1995, 
ADM's Board of Directors announced the formation of a 
Special Committee consisting of nine directors—Archer, 
Bruce, Goldberg, Johnson, Vanier, Webb and Rockefeller, 
with defendants Mulroney and Daniels as co-chairs. The 
Special Committee retained the law firm of Simpson, 
Thacher & Bartlett as counsel. 
32. The Special Committee, according to an ADM 
press release, will “direct the Company’s response to mat- 
ters related to the government investigation of the high- 
fructose corn syrup, citric acid and lysine industries and 
certain related civil suits”. 
33. Professor John Coffee, a noted corporate govern- 
ance expert at Columbia University Law School, has noted 
that ADM needs more than a special committee. Instead, 
Professor Coffee stated, it needs “a team of experts to 
lead a full-blown independent investigation,” because “the 
allegations against ADM could not be more serious.” 
‘Checks, Lies and Videotape,” Fortune, October 30, 1995 
p. 109 at 112 (emphasis added). 
34. Moreover, for the reasons set forth in 
para 
28-29 above, the Special Committee is oo 
ne 
dependent. At least three of the directors on the Special 
Committee, including Mulroney who is a co-chair, have 
disabling personal and professional ties to D. Andreas. 
All have direct personal ties to D. Andreas. The members 
of the Special Committee are “outside directors” only to 
the extent that they are not employees of ADM. They do 
not conform to the Council of Institutional Investors 
definition of outside director, namely, a person whose only 
tie to the company is his or her directorship. 
35. The Special Committee has yet to render its find- 
ings and recommendations. 
Any recommendation, other 
than one to prosecute each director for breaching his or 
her fiduciary duties to ADM and its shareholders, would 
Wa 
“e 

78 
be a whitewash. Because the Special Committee is com- 
posed of interested, non-independent directors who were 
themselves involved in the alleged wrongdoing, such a 
recommendation will not be made. 
For that reason as 
well, any demand made on ADM’s Board of Directors 
would be futile. 
The Corporate Governance Committee 
36. On or about October 21, 1995, the Board, in an 
attempt to muffle institutional shareholders’ constant bar- 
rage of criticism aimed at the “family” run Board, created 
the Corporate Governance Committee, co-chaired by de- 
fendants Goldberg and Webb, to examine the Board’s 
size, director, qualifications, ethics, compensation and other 
issues, including whether the Board has sufficient inde- 
pendence from management. 
37. On or about January 15, 1996, the Corporate 
Governance Committee announced its recommendations, 
which would weaken the control of defendant D. Andreas 
and force the retirement of several directors with close 
ties to him. As reported in The Wall Street Journal on 
January 
16, 1996, “[t]he committee’s report surprised 
Wall Street because it signaled that Mr. Andreas, who has 
run the [Company] in an autocratic style rare for a pub- 
licly traded company may be bowing to shareholder pres- 
sute to overhaul the 17-member board” (emphasis added). 
38. The report made several major recommendations 
with respect to the composition of the new Board, includ- 
ing that an outside director cannot be a current employee, 
a retired senior executive, the “close” relation of a senior 
manager, or someone who has a “personal” business or 
professional relationship with the Company. 
39. Notwithstanding these changes, The Wall Street 
Journal stated that “it is far from clear how much influ- 
ence Mr. Andreas will lose over the Board, which hasn't 
seriously challenged his authority since he became chair- 
79

BACKGROUND FACTS 
41. ADM is a $12 billion corporate empire built, 
according to defendant D. Andreas, by “developing more 
expensive products from raw [agricultural] commodities 
and convincing the world it needed them.” ADM’s stra- 
tegy is straightforward: to be a major player in all busi- 
nesses it enters as well as the lowest-cost producer. ADM 
is one of the world’s largest processors of corn, wheat and 
oilseeds, as well as a major procurer, transporter, proces- 
sor and merchandiser 
of numerous 
other agricultural 
products. It has proclaimed itself the “supermarket to the 
world”. 
42. Corn refining is ADM’s most profitable activity, 
accounting for approximately 55 to 70 percent of its 
yearly operating earnings. Among other things, the Com- 
pany processes corn to produce citric acid and high fruc-

ADM’S PRICE-FIXING 
47. When ADM began its BioProducts Division in 
1989, its objective was to 
production of amino acids and other bioproducts. 
To 
accomplish that objective, the Company’s first goal was 
to develop lysine “into massive business,” as noted by 
Whitacre in an interview appearing in the September 4, 
1995 issue of Fortune (the “Fortune Interview” ). 
48. In order to develop its lysine business, ADM first 
attempted a “predatory pricing” strategy that it had pre- 
viously used successfully. The strategy was to gain custo- 
mers and market share quickly, even at the initial expense 
of profitability, by flooding the market with lysine at low 
prices with the intent that competitors would be driven 
out of the market. Once the Company had gained market 
share, it hoped to raise prices and earn profits. 
49. This strategy was 
partially successful 
and, by

54. In April, 1992, Whitacre and Wilson held meet- 
ings in Tokyo with representatives from ADM’s two big- 
gest Japanese competitors in the lysine business, Kyowa 
Hakko Kogyo and Ajinomoto. The purpose of that meet- 
ing was to determine whether their competitors were in- 
terested in entering into a conspiracy to fix prices. 
55. A few months later, in June, 1992, Wilson and 
Whitacre again met with ADM’s Japanese competitors. 
During that meeting, Wilson gave a presentation in which 
he discussed how ADM’s entry into the lysine market had 
resulted in production capacity 20% 
to 25% 
greater 
than current usage levels, and that this overcapacity had 
resulted in prices being driven down from $1.30 per pound 
to $.60 
per pound. He also explained 
how this drop in 
price resulted in a $200 million loss in revenues. He 
stated: “well, gentlemen, there’s $200 million that we're 
giving to our customers. 
In other words, the customer is 
benefitting, not the people who spent hundreds of millions 
of dollars building these plants.” Wilson then repeated 
the ADM philosophy of “the competitor is our friend, and 
the customer is our enemy.” 
56. Over the next several months the Director De- 
fendants authorized, carried out and acceded to a scheme, 
in conjunction with the Company’s competitors in the 
lysine market, including Kyowa Hakko Kogyo, Ajinomoto 
and others, to fix the worldwide price and market share 
of lysine, in violation of the federal antitrust laws. 
57. In connection therewith, Whitacre and Wilson, 
under the direction of defendants M.D. Andreas and Ran- 
dall, held numerous meetings with ADM’s competitors in 
various locations all over the world. 
Most of ADM’s 
high-level management were aware of these meetings and 
what transpired therein.

Whitacre and Wilson as

63. The next day, ADM released a terse, two-sentence 
statement that a federal grand jury in Chicago was in- 
vestigating “possible violations of federal antitrust laws 
and related crimes in the food additives industry.” The 
Company also indicated that it had been served with a 
search warrant and subpoenas for documents and testi- 
mony concerning its marketing practices. The statement 
failed, however, to disclose the magnitude of the investi- 
gation, that it was commenced because of a tip from one 
of ADM'’s senior corporate executives, Whitacre, or that 
the investigation had been on-going since 1992. 
64. According to an article published on July 10, 
1995, in The Wall Street Journal, Whitacre approached 
the FBI in 1992 after he had been asked by an ADM 
executive to participate in a pricing arrangement, 
in- 
volving the ADM products lysine and citric acid that 
he believed was collusive. 
Whitacre produced informa- 
tion indicating that ADM had engaged, and was engag- 
ing, in collusive pricing practices. The Journal reported 
that Whitacre had been an undercover operative for the 
FBI for the past three years and had secretly assisted the 
government in making hundreds of tape recordings of 
ADM’s anti-competitive and illegal volume and price- 
fixing activities. 
65. Recently, it was reported in the May 13, 1996 
edition of Fortune, the DOJ had struck a bargain with 
another ADM executive who knew about the price fixing 
of lysine and promised him immunity in exchange for his 
testimony.

IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT 
FOR THE CENTRAL DISTRICT OF ILLINOIS 
[Caption Omitted in Printing] 
DEFENDANTS’ MOTION TO DISMISS THE 
CONSOLIDATED AND AMENDED DERIVATIVE 
COMPLAINT FOR FAILURE TO MAKE DEMAND 
AND FAILURE TO STATE A CLAIM 
Defendants move the Court to enter an order dismissing 
plaintiffs’ Consolidated Amended Derivative Complaint 
pursuant to Fed. R. Civ. P. 23.1. 
1. The present action was instituted on or about July 
13, 1995, in the United States District Court for the 
Northern District of Illinois. 
A notice of intent to file a 
“Proposed Consolidated and Amended Derivative Com- 
plaint” was filed in the Northern District of Illinois on 
Sept. 29, 1995. 
2. This action was transferred to this Court on or 
about November 16, 1996. 
3. Defendants 
filed a motion to dismiss plaintiffs’ 
original consolidated complaint on December 27, 1995. 
4. At the hearing 
on defendants’ 
motion to dismiss on 
April 26, 1996, the Court dimissed plaintiffs’ action for 
failure to file a verified complaint, giving plaintiffs leave 
to refile. 
5. Plaintiffs filed their Consolidated and Amended De- 
rivative Complaint (“Amended Complaint” or “Amended 
Compl.” on May 17, 1996. In the Amended Complaint, 
plaintiffs allege that members of the board of directors 

94 
(“the Individual 
Defendants”) 
of nominal 
defendant 
Archer Daniels Midland Co. (“ADM”) breached their 
fiduciary duty to shareholders by participating in or reck- 
kssly disregarding antitrust violations in three product 
areas (lysine, high fructose corn syrup, and citric acid). 
Amended Compl. 4 40-69. 
Plaintiffs also allege that the 
Individual Defendants authorized or approved illegal pay- 
ments to ADM executives. Id. 44 70, 73. 
6. Plaintiffs request the following relief: 
(i) damages 
on behalf of “ADM and its public stockholders” against 
the Individual Defendants; 
(ii) equitable and injunctive 
relief; and (iii) costs and attorneys’ fees. Id. 
7. Plaintiffs’ Amended Complaint must be dismissed 
because plaintiffs failed to make a demand on the board 
of directors pursuant to Fed. R. Civ. P. 23.1 before 
filing this action. 
8. In addition, 
plaintiffs have failed 
to show that 
demand was excused on grounds that it would be futile. 
Plaintiffs have failed to allege particularized facts that 
would raise a reasonable doubt that a majority of ADM’s 
board was incapable of impartially considering a demand 
due to self-interest or lack of independence. 
9. Defendants request oral argument to present fully 
the issue of why plaintiffs were required to make a pre- 
suit demand on the board of directors, and why their 
failure to do so requires dismissal. 
WHEREFORE, the defendants pray that this Court dis- 
miss plaintiffs’ Consolidated and Amended 
Derivative 
Complaint for failure to comply with the demand require- 
ment set forth in Fed. R. Civ P. 23.1. 
[Counsel Listing Omitted]

91. The Director Defendants named in this Count, in

family at the expense of ADM and its shareholders. 
The Defendants’ improper behavior includes, but is not 
limited to, the following: 
(a) Upon learning of the government’s investiga- 
tion, ADM executives determined to withhold infor- 
mation from the public. 
The Director Defendants 
went along with this policy. Indeed, former director 
and officer Howard G. Buffett resigned his positions 
one week after the June 27, 1995 FBI subpoenas 
became public “because he was troubled by the [gov- 
ernment’s] investigation and the way the Company 
was handling it,” and because he had “lost confi- 
dence” in whether ADM executives were telling him 
the truth. (See paragraph 66, above.)

sibility for ADM’s response to the government's in- 
vestigation to a Special Committee of the Board com- 
posed of friends and associates of D. Andreas. (See 
paragraphs 31-34, above.) This Committee 
was more

terests than in ensuring that the thorough and im- 
partial investigation 
necessary to protect the interests 
of the Company and its shareholders was conducted

AGREED MOTION FOR AN ORDER PRELIMINARILY 
APPROVING SETTLEMENT, SCHEDULING A FINAL 
SETTLEMENT HEARING AND DIRECTING THAT 
NOTICE BE GIVEN TO THE SHAREHOLDERS OF 
ARCHER DANIELS MIDLAND COMPANY 
Plaintiffs, by their counsel, move, pursuant to Fed. R. 
Civ. P. 23.1, for an order (a) preliminarily approving the 
settlement of the derivative claims asserted in this action; 
(b) scheduling a hearing to consider final approval of 
the settlement and Plaintiffs’ counsel’s request for an 
award of fees and expenses; and (c) authorizing the form 
and manner of notice to be given to Archer Daniels Mid- 
land Company’s shareholders. 
In support of this motion, 
Plaintiffs state as follows: 
1. This action was commenced in the United States 
District Court for the Northern District of Illinois and 
the Circuit Court of Cook County, Illinois in July 1995. 
Subsequently, the cases were transferred to and consoli- 
dated in this Court. 
2. Plaintiffs’ counsel and counsel for the Defendants 
and the Company have reached a proposed settlement 
with the Company and the Defendants that includes im- 
plementation of corporate governance measures and the 
payment on behalf of the Defendants of $8 million, a 
significant portion of which will be used by the Company 
in implementing the corporate governance measures. 
A 
copy of the Stipulation of Settlement is attached as Ex- 
hibit A. 
107 
3. The Settlement was achieved following Plaintiffs’ 
counsel's substantial fact investigation, consultation with 
independent experts and certain of the Company’s major 
ee a and arm’s length negotiations be- 
een 
Plaintiffs’ counsel and counsel f 
and the Defendants. 
co 
4. The settlement will confer substantial benefits on 
the Company and its shareholders and is within the range 
of possible approval by the Court. 
WHEREFORE, Plaintiffs 
naan 
request that the Court enter 
(a) Granting preliminary approval of the settlement; 
(b) Approving the proposed notice of settlement at- 
tached hereto as Exhibit B; directing that notice 
of the settlement in the form attached as Ex- 
hibit “B-1” be mailed to Class members; and 
directing that publication notice in the form 
attached as Exhibit “B-2” be published in the 
national edition of The Wall Street Journal. 
(c) Approving the timetable for giving notice to the 
shareholders of Archer Daniels Midland Com- 
pany and holding a hearing to consider approval 
of the settlement, as set forth in the Order Pre- 
liminarily Approving Settlement and Providing 
for Notice.

STIPULATION OF SETTLEMENT 
This Stipulation 
of Settlement 
(the “Stipulation”), 
dated as of May 29, 1997, is made and entered into by 
and among (i) the Plaintiffs in the Illinois Action and 
the Delaware Action on behalf of themselves, the share- 
holders of Archer Daniels Midland Company (“ADM” or 
the “Company”) and derivatively on behalf of the nomi- 
nal defendant ADM, (ii) the Director Defendants, and 
(iii) nominal defendant ADM (collectively the “Parties”), 
as those terms are defined herein, by and through their 
counsel of record in the Actions. The Stipulation is in- 
tended by the Parties to fully, finally and forever resolve, 
discharge and settle the Settled Claims (as defined here- 
in), upon and subject to the terms and conditions hereof. 
I. THE LITIGATION 
This litigation was commenced in July 1995, with the 
filing of shareholder derivative 
actions 
in the United 
States District Court for the Northern District of Illinois, 
the Circuit Court of Cook County, Illinois and the Dela- 
ware Court of Chancery (the “Delaware Court”). 
The 
actions filed in Illinois were transferred to the United 
States District Court for the Central District of Illinois 
(the “District Court”), and Plaintiffs filed a Consolidated 
and Amended Derivative Complaint, entitled Paul Felzen, 
Truste2 of Louise Laskin Trust - 199] and Sandra Esner, 
Plaintiffs, v. Dwayne O. Andreas, et al., Defendants, and 
Archer Daniel Midland Company, Nominal Defendant, 
Case No. 2279 (the “Illinois Action”). 
In November

in

titled In Re Archer Daniels Midland Company Derivativ 
Litigation, Consolidated No. 14403 (the “Delaware a> 
tion”), to add allegations relating to events that occurred 
during 1996. Both the Illinois Action and the Delaware 
Action (the “Actions”) named as defendants Dwayne 
O 
Andreas, Ralph Bruce, John H. Daniels 
tate ory G. 
Buffett, Lowell W. Andreas, James R. Randall, Michael 
D. Andreas, H.D. Hale, O. Glenn Webb, Martin Andreas 
Margaretta Rockefeller, F. Ross Johnson, Ray A. Gold. 
berg, John K. Vanier, Shreve M. Archer, Jr. M. Brian 
Mulroney, and Robert S. Strauss, and the Delaware 
Action also named Gaylord O. Coan as a defendant (the 
Director Defendants”). 
The Actions

II. PRETRIAL PROCEEDINGS AND 
DISCOVERY IN THE LITIGATION 
A. 
Defendants’ Actions 
The Director Defendants and the Compan 
motions to dismiss the Actions. 
In their ce ae 
me the Director Defendants and the Company 
ed 
that Plaintiffs failed to make a demand upon the Siscine 

110 
of ADM as required by Delaware law before bringing 
their action, that Plaintiffs failed to state a claim for 
relief against the Director Defendants and that, under 
Article Fourteenth of ADM’s Certificate of Incorporation, 
defendants are not liable for any of the breaches of duty 
alleged in the Actions. 
Plaintiffs opposed the motions. 
The District Court has not ruled on the motion to dis- 
miss and has adjourned oral argument on the motion, 
pending a hearing by the Court at which this Stipulation 
and the settlement are to be presented. 
Similarly, the 
Delaware Court has not ruled on defendants’ motion to 
dismiss. The Parties to the Delaware Action agree to ad- 
vise the Delaware Court of the proposed settlement as 
soon as practicable and to hold in abeyance all proceed- 
ings other than those set forth in the MOU or Stipulation 
that are required to obtain Final Court Approval. 
After the announcement of the federal government’s 
investigation into the charges that ADM had violated the 
antitrust laws and the filing of these Actions and class 
action lawuits for violation of the federal securities and 
antitrust laws, ADM’s Board of Directors appointed a 
Special Committee 
consisting 
of outside 
directors 
to 
“direct the Company's response to matters related to the 
government investigation of the high fructose corn syrup, 
citric acid and lysine industries and certain related civil 
suits.” The Special Committee retained counsel to assist 
it. 
After the Illinois Action and the Delaware Action were 
filed and the allegations reviewed, the Company under- 
took certain actions in the areas of corporate governance, 
employee conduct, compliance, and succession. 
On October 21, 1995, ADM’s Board of Directors cre- 
ated the Corporate Governance Committee, consisting of 
outside directors to examine the Board’s size, director 
qualifications, 
independence 
from management, 
ethics, 
compensation and other issues. The Corporate Govern- 
ance Committee reported 
its recommendations 
to the 
Board on January 15, 1996, including recommendations 
establishing requirements for an independent Board. 
In 
the Spring of 1996, the Company announced that certain 
of its directors, including Michael D. Andreas would not 
stand for re-election. Three new directors were elected to 
the Board at the annual meeting of shareholders in Octo- 
ber, 1996. In April, 1997, the Company announced that 
Dwayne Andreas had retired from his position as CEO 
of the Company and that G. Allen Andreas, Jr. would 
become the CEO and a member of the Board. In addi-

Ill. 
DEFENDANTS’ STATEMENT AND DENIALS 
OF WRONGDOING AND LIABILITY 
Each of the Director Defendants has denied and con- 
tinues to deny each and all of the claims and contentions 
alleged by Plaintiffs in the Litigation. Each of the Direc- 
tor Defendants expressly has denied and continues to deny 
all charges of wrongdoing or liability against him or her 
arising out of the conduct, statements, acts or omissions 
alleged, or that could have been alleged, in the Litigation. 
Each of the Director Defendants further asserts that at 
all material times, he or she acted in good faith and in 
a manner he or she reasonably believed to be in the best 
interest of ADM and its shareholders. 
Despite their assertions, the Director Defendants have 
concluded that the further conduct of the Litigation would 
be protracted and expensive, and that it is desirable that 
the Litigation be fully and finally settled in the manner 
and upon the terms and conditions set forth in this Stipu- 
lation in order to limit further expense, inconvenience 
and distraction, to dispose of burdensome and protracted 
litigation, and to permit the operation of ADM’s busi- 
ness without further expensive litigation and the distrac- 
tion and diversion of ADM’s executive personnel with 
respect to matters in issue in the Litigation. 
Moreover, the Director Defendants recognize that the 
procedures and measures that are set forth in Exhibit A, 
and which the Company will implement as part of the 
Settlement, will provide substantial benefits and protec- 
tions to the Company and its shareholders.

C. 
Attorneys’ Fees and Costs 
Counsel for the Plaintiffs in the Actions may make ap- 
plication to the District Court for an award of attorney 
fees, expenses and costs. No application for an award of 
attorney fees, expenses, and/or costs shall be made in any 
other forum. The Company and the Director Defendants 
agree not to oppose an application(s) for an award of 
attorney fees, costs and expenses made to the District 
Court by Co-Lead Counsel for the Plaintiffs in the Illinois 
and Delaware Actions which does not exceed $3,920,000 
of the Settlement Proceeds, plus interest. Any payment of 
an amount awarded by the District Court as fees, costs 
and expenses shall be made solely from the Settlement 
Proceeds, and shall not be made until Final Approval, 
and will be paid to Co-Lead Counsel for Plaintiffs in the 
Actions, as directed by the District Court, within three 
(3) business days after the District Court’s order direct- 
ing disbursement of the Settlement Proceeds.

Il. 
Board Structures and Policies 
The Board of Directors shall have appropriate struc- 
tures and policies to ensure that the Board acts at all 
times in the best interests of the Company and its share- 
holders. The Company looks to outside directors to pro- 
vide an independent view of the performance of the Com- 
pany and its management. 
Accordingly, to ensure the 
highest standards of independent judgment and account- 
ability, a majority of the Board shall be comprised of 
outside directors. 
Outside and Nonmanagement Directors Defined. 
An 
“outside director” means a director who 
(a) is neither a current employee nor a former member 
of senior management of the Company or an affiliate; 
(b) does not have any business or professional rela- 
tionship with the Company, either personally or through 
a company of which the director is an officer or a con- 
trolling shareholder, that is material to the Company or 
to the director; 
(c) does not have a close family relationship, by blood, 
marriage, or otherwise with any member of senior man- 
agement 
of the Company 
or one 
of the Company's 
affiliates; 
(d) is not an officer of a company of which the Com- 
pany’s chairman or chief executive officer is also a board 
member; 
(e) is not personally receiving compensation from the 
Company in any capacity other than as a director; and 
(f) does not personally receive or is not an employee 
of a foundation, university, or other institution that re- 
ceives grants or endowments from the Company, that are 
129 
material to the Company or to either 
—_ 
the foundation, university, or er 
a 
Sag recipient and/or 
A “nonmanagement director” means 
: 
, 
not a current employee of the Company. director who is 

130 
ing officers, president, vice president(s), treasurer, secre- 
tary and the controller of the Company. 
“Affiliate” includes any person or entity which, alone 
or by contractual obligation, owns or has the power to 
vote more than twenty-five (25) percent of the equity 
interest in another, unless some other person or entity act- 
ing alone or with another by contractual obligation owns 
or has the power to vote a greater percentage of the equity 
interest. A subsidiary is considered an affiliate if it is at 
least eighty (80) percent owned by the Company and 
accounts for at least twenty-five (25) percent of the Com- 
pany’s consolidated sales or assets. 
II. Nominating Committee for 
1997 Board Elections 
The Nominating Committee for selecting the directors to 
be proposed for election at the 1997 Annual Meeting shall 
be comprised of outside directors who were first elected 
to the Board of Directors after June 25, 1995. 
Ill. 
Amendment to By-Laws 
The provisions pertaining 
to the Board’s 
structures 
and policies, set forth in Section I, will become part of 
ADM'’s By-Laws. Any changes to these provisions will be 
reported within thirty (30) days of their effective date on 
Form 8-K of the Securities and Exchange Commission 
attaching a copy of such amendments, and a copy of 
such amendments will be filed promptly with the New 
York Stock Exchange. 
IV. 
Leadership of the Board 
If the offices of the Chairman and Chief Executive 
Officer are combined in one person, or if the Chairman 
is not an outside director, then the outside directors shall 
annually elect one of their members to serve as Vice- 
Chairman. The Vice-Chairman shall preside over all meet- 
ings of nonmanagement directors and of the Governance

VI. 
Governance Committee 
The Board will create a permanent committee, consist- 
ing of the nonmanagement directors, to meet together at 
least annually to assess Board and Committee effective- 
ness and to report the results to the Board. The Govern- 
ance Committee will establish and approve performance 
criteria for evaluation of the Chief Executive Officer, 
and all nonmanagement directors should participate in 
the actual evaluation of the Chief Executive Officer. 
The performance criteria and evaluation process shall be 
disclosed to shareholders in the Company’s proxy state- 
ment for the annual shareholders’ meeting. 
VII. 
Director Remuneration 
At least one-half of the Compensation for non-employee 
directors will be paid in ADM stock. 
For a period of 
three (3) years hereafter, total compensation for each 
director shall not exceed $100,000 per annum. The valu- 
ation of the stock will occur on the last day of the Com- 
pany’s fiscal year.

TIONS AND CLAIMS ASSERTED THEREIN.

THE DERIVATIVE SETTLEMENT, YOU WILL BE 
BARRED FROM CONTESTING 
THE 
FAIRNESS,

This Notice is given pursuant to Kule 23.1 of the Fed- 
eral Rules of Civil Procedure and an Order of the United 
States District Court for the Central District of Illinois 
(the “Court”). 
The purpose of the Notice is to advise 
you that the above-entitled 
actions 
(the “Litigation”) 
are now pending in the Court and the Delaware Chancery 
135 
Court (the “Delaware Court”) and that all parties to the 
Litigation have reached a settlement (the “Settlement”) 
which would fully, finally and forever resolve the Deriva- 
tive Claims as between Plaintiffs and the Defendants, as 
defined herein, on the terms and conditions summarized 
in this Notice. 
This Notice is not intended to be and should not be 
construed as an expression of any opinion by the Court 
with respect to the truth of the allegations of the Deriva- 
tive Claims or the merits of the claims or defenses as- 
serted. 
This Notice is merely to advise you of the pend- 
ency and proposed Settlement of the Derivative Claims 
and of your rights thereunder. 
I. THE LITIGATION 
This Litigation was commenced in July 1995, with the 
filing of purported derivative actions in the United States 
District Court for the Northern District of Illinois, the Cir- 
cuit Court of Cook County, Illinois and the Delaware 
Court. The actions filed in Illinois were transferred to the 
Court, and plaintiffs filed a Consolidated and Amended 
Derivative Complaint, entitled Paul Felzen, Trustee of 
Louise Laskin Trust—199] and Sandra Esner, Plaintiffs, v. 
Dwayne O. Andreas, et al., Defendants, and Archer Dan- 
iels Midland Company, Nominal Defendant, Case No. 
2279 (the “Illinois Action”). The Plaintiffs in the Dela- 
ware Court filed an amended and consolidated complaint 
entitled In: Re Archer Daniels Midland Company Deriva- 
tive Litigation, Consolidated No. 14403 (the “Delaware 
Action”). 
Both the Illinois Action and the Delaware 
Action (the “Actions” or the “Litigation”) named as de- 
fendants Dwayne O. Andreas, Ralph Bruce, John H. 
Daniels, Howard G. Buffett, Lowell W. Andreas, James 
R. Randall, Michael D. Andreas, H.D. Hale, O. Glenn 
Webb, Martin Andreas, Margaretta Rockefeller, F. Ross 
Johnson, Ray A. Goldberg, John K. Vanier, Shreve M. 

136 
Archer, Jr., M. Brian Mulroney, and Robert S. Strauss, 
and the Delaware Action also named Gaylord O. Coan 
as a defendant (the “Director Defendants”). The Actions 
alleged, among other things, that the Director Defendants 
breached their fiduciary duties or obligations owed to the 
Company and the stockholders of the Company by par- 
ticipating 
in improper conduct 
or failing to properly 
supervise the activities of certain employees of the Com- 
pany who had engaged in improper conduct, including 
violations of federal antitrust laws and federal securities 
laws. 
Il. 
PRETRIAL PROCEEDINGS AND DISCOVERY 
IN THE LITIGATION 
A. 
Defendants’ Actions 
The Director Defendants and the Company have filed 
motions to dismiss the Actions. 
In their motions to dis- 
miss, the Director Defendants and the Company argue 
that Plaintiffs, among other things, failed to make a 
demand upon the directors of ADM as required by Dela- 
ware law before bringing their action and failed to state 
a claim for relief against the Director Defendants. 
Plain- 
tiffs opposed the motions. 
The motions to dismiss are 
being held in abeyance pending a hearing by the Court 
at which this Stipulation and the Settlement are to be 
presented. 
After the announcement of the federal government's 
investigation into the charges that ADM had violated the 
antitrust laws and the filing of these Actions, the Com- 
pany’s Board of Directors appointed a Special Committee 
consisting of outside directors to investigate the charges 
made by the federal government and undertook certain 
actions in the areas of corporate governance, employee 
conduct, compliance, and succession. 
ADM’s Board of Directors also created the Corporate 
Governance Committee, consisting of outside directors to 
137 
examine, among other things, director qualifications and 
independence from management. 
In the Spring of 1996, 
the Company announced that certain of its directors, in- 
cluding Michael D. Andreas, would not stand for re- 
election. 
In April, 1997, the Company announced that 
Dwayne Andreas had retired from his position as CEO 
of the Company and that G. Allen Andreas, Jr. would 
become the CEO and a member of the Board. 
In addi- 
tion, the Company announced the election of Ambassador 
Andrew Young to the Board and the retire 
Andreas from the Board. 
Bice 
poutns 
B. 
Discovery, 
Investigation And Research Conducted 
By Plaintiffs 
Counsel for Plaintiffs have conducted certain discovery 
and conducted an extensive investigation during the prose- 
cution of the Litigati>n. 
Co-Lead Counsel for Plaintiffs 
in the Illinois Action consulted extensively with experts in 
the areas of corporate governance and economics and 
with the Company's largest institutional investors and re- 
viewed and analyzed treatises, articles and authoritative 
reports On Corporate governance issues. 
In addition, 
all 
Plaintiffs’ Counsel reviewed and analyzed thousands of 
pages of documents produced by Defendants, reviewed 
and analyzed the Company's public filings and press 
releases, and thoroughly analyzed and evaluat 
supporting Plaintiffs’ claims. 
ated the facts

THE PROPOSED SETTLEMENT 
Based on Plaintiffs’ investigation, Plainti 
with ADM that it is in the oe interests pamee ee 
shareholders to settle the derivative claims on the terms 
of the Stipulation of Settlement. 
The following descrip- 
tion of the Proposed Settlement is only a summary, 
and 
reference is made to the text of the Stipulation of Settle- 
ment, on file with the Court, f 
i 
ee 
or a full statement of its 

138 
(i) In Settlement of the derivative claims, the Director 
Defendants will pay or cause to be paid the total sum of 
$8 Million to ADM. 
(ii) As additional consideration for Settlement of the 
Litigation, the Board of Directors has also agreed to 
adopt, upon approval by the Court of the Settlement the 
corporate governance measures and procedures which are 
set forth in Exhibit A which is attached to this Notice. 
(iii) If the proposed Settlement 
is approved by the 
Court, the Court will enter a judgment which will dis- 
miss the derivative claims as against the Director De- 
fendants with prejudice, and all shareholders of ADM 
shall be barred and enjoined from asserting any Settled 
Claims, as this term is defined in the Stipulation of Settle-

duties relating to the allegations in this Litigation) against 
the Director Defendants or ADM and each and all share- 
holders shall conclusively be deemed to have released any 
and all such Settled Claims as against the Director De- 
fendants and ADM. 
(iv) All the claims that the Company may have against 
Michael Andreas, Terrance Wilson, Mark Whitacre, Rein- 
hard Richter, Sidney Hulse and Marty Allison, are re- 
served to the Company and are not released by the Stip- 
ulation of Settlement, nor are they Settled Claims. 
(v) Legal counsel for the Plaintiffs in the Actions may 
make application to the Court for an award of attorney 
fees, expenses and costs. The Company and the Director 
Defendants agree not to oppose an application(s) for an 
award of attorney fees, costs and expenses which does not 
exceed 49% of the Settlement Proceeds deposited in the 
Escrow Account, plus interest.

ERRATA SHEET 
The Notice unintentionally omits the following infor- 
mation: 
The only claims which are being settled in the 
District Court are those which have been made in the 
Illinois Action. Upon approval of the Settlement by the 
District Court, Co-Lead Counsel in the Delaware Action 
will apply to dismiss that Action based on the Settlement 
and will not apply to the Delaware Court for fees and 
expenses.

OBJECTION TO PROPOSED SETTLEMENT 
OF DERIVATIVE ACTIONS 
COME NOW, Charles W. Pauly, Jr. and Elizabeth D. 
Pauly, joint tenants and file this Objection to Proposed 
Settlement of Derivative Actions and would show to the 
Court as follows: 
1. If the Plaintiffs’ case has merit, then the financial 
settlement is grossly inadequate to compensate the Plain- 
tiffs for the injuries suffered as a result of the actions~by 
the Defendants. 
2. If the Plaintiffs’ case is without merit, then the case 
is being setiled for nuisance value which compensates 
Plaintiffs’ attorneys, but does nothing to enhance share- 
holder value. 
In fact, it only demonstrates the company 
is a “soft” target for future frivolous litigation. 
3. Plaintiffs should be put to trial and demonstrate the 
merits of their claim. 
If Plaintiffs are successful, then 
damages will be substantially higher than the nominal 
consideration being received in the settlement. 
If Plaintiffs 
are unsuccessful, 
the suit was without merit and the 
Plaintiffs and their counsel should receive nothing. 
WHEREFORE, premises considered, movant requests 
the Court deny the proposed settlement as being unfair 
and inadequate to the shareholders of Archer Daniels 
Midland Company.

Ref Paul Felzen, Trustee v. A.D.M. 
Case no, 95-2279 
Dear Judge Baker, 
As an A.D.M. shareholder of record (1042 shares, 
which are in my account with Dean Witter), I received 
notice of a proposed settlement of the above case. 
At least one aspect of the proposed settlement deserves 
your close scrutiny: attorney fees, costs and expenses not 
to exceed 49 pct. of the settlement proceeds. 
I have to say that I am in the shipping business, as a 
chartering broker, where my firm works, sometimes for 
weeks, setting up charters, for 1.25 pct. of the freight 
charges on an ocean shipment. 
Usually the freights are 
$300,000 or $400,000, yielding us say $3000 to $5000 
per charter. Real estate brokers get 10 or maybe 15 pct. 
commissions, and that is, I believe often split between 
brokers. 49 pct is beyond any normal conception, and I 
urge you to substantially reduce the percentage of the 
settlement fees to the attorneys. 
It crosses my mind that there are law firms in both 
Philadelphia and Chicago, and maybe in Delaware, in- 
volved in this case. When I am trying to arrange a 
charter, the market dictates that I involve as few brokers 
and thus as few commissions payable as possible, and 

156 
i 
w firms should be encouraged to act simi- 
See possible. You hold the possibility of such 
encouragement in your hands. 
‘The irony of a suit brought on behalf of shareholders, 
the results of which are eaten away at 49% for attorneys 
fees will, I am sure, not have escaped you. 
Very truly yours, 
/s/ Peter D. Scott 
Peter D. ScoTT

pendence such that pre-suit demand was futile.

16. The focus of the motions to dismiss in the Dela- 
ware Action was directed to three primary issues: 
pre- 
suit demand pursuant to Delaware Chancery Rule 23.1, 
whether plaintiffs stated a claim for breach of fiduciary 
duty pursuant to Chancery Rule 12(b)(6), and whether 
ADM’s adoption of exculpatory provisions in its Certifi- 
cate of Incorporation required dismissal of the complaint 
pursuant to 8 Del. Ch. § 102(b)(7). Thus, the Director 
Defendants and the Company argued that: 
the Delaware 
plaintiffs had not met their burden of establishing demand 
futility; the Delaware plaintiffs had not stated a claim for 
relief; and pursuant to Delaware law, 8 Del. Ch. § 102 
(b)(7), the defendants could not be held liable, as a 
matter of law, for any of the breaches of duty alleged in 
the Amended and Consolidated Derivative Complaint. 
17. While opposition to the motions to dismiss was 
being finalized, Delaware co-lead counsel were asked to 
attend a meeting with Robert Roseman, Esq. and Terry 
Rose Saunders, Esq., co-lead counsel in the Illinois Action 
(collectively 
“Illinois co-lead counsel”). 
For the first 
time, Delaware co-lead counsel learned that settlement 
negotiations had been held between the parties in the 
Illinois Action, to the exclusion of the Delaware plaintiffs. 
18. On April 30, 1997, Delaware co-lead counsel met 
with Illinois co-lead counsel in New York. At that meet- 
ing, Mr. Roseman advised us that settlement negotiations 
in the Illinois Action had been ongoing since December 
1996. and that the parties had reached an agreement in 
principle. 
Mr. Roseman summarized the terms of the 
agreement and requested that Delaware co-lead counsel 
seriously consider joining in the agreement since, in his

are both distinterested and independent with respect to that 
issue.

family relationship” for purposes of exclusion 
of that 
person on the board as an outside director.

The Benefits Of The Proposed Settlement

will confer upon ADM substantial benefits, particularly in

benefit ADM shareholders. 
It is respectfully submitted 
that the proposed settlement is an excellent result because 
it addresses and resolves the very issues raised in both the 
Illinois Action 
and Delaware Action 
regarding board 
supervision, monitoring, reporting, and independence, as 
well as provides for a monetary fund (paid for on behalf 
of the Director Defendants by their insurance carriers ) 
to finance (among other things) implementation of many 
of the corporate compliance procedures and mechanisms. 
Thus, the proposed settlement confers substantial benefits, 
at no cost to the Company.

and the Company in their motions to dismiss.

tainty as to whether derivative claims will survive dis- 
missal. There is never a guarantee that plaintiffs will be 
successful in convincing the Delaware Court that demand 
is futile. or that the conduct of defendants, as pled, is not 
shielded by the exculpatory provision contained in ADM’s 
Certificate of Incorporation.

37. Courts have repeatedly held that it is in the public 
interest to have experienced and able counsel enforce the 
regulations pertaining to the duties of officers and directors 
of public companies. 
Vigorous private enforcement of 
state corporation laws can only occur if private plaintiffs 
can obtain parity representation with that available to 
large institutional interests. 
If this imporant public policy 
is to be carried out, the courts must award fees which will 
adequately compensate private plaintiffs’ counsel, taking 
into account the enormous risks undertaken with a clear 
view of the economics of a contingent derivative action.

43. The bases for the principal claims in the Delaware 
Action relate to a lack of a corporate compliance system 
to monitor and oversee employee conduct, as well as a 
lack of independence of a majority of ADM’s board mem- 
bers because of their personal and business inter-relation- 
ships. 
The proposed settlement addresses and resolves 
those claims. Among other things, it requires that the 
Board be comprised of a majority of outside directors, 
it defines what constitutes an outside director, and it re- 
quires that a procedure for corporate compliance be insti- 
tuted in the form of corporate information gathering and 
reporting regarding compliance with ADM’s policies and 
procedures, as well as compliance with all federal and 
state laws. In addition, the proposed settlement creates a 
Settlement Fund of $8 million dollars from which pay- 
ments are to be made to pay for implementing these new 
corporate procedures and practices, including payments 
for outside independent counsel, as well as to pay plain- 
tiffs’ counsel in the derivative actions. 
Most impressive 
is that the monies paid to the Settlement Fund come from 
insurance carriers on behalf of the Director Defendants, 
and not from the Company. 
44. To suggest, as the Pauly Objection does, that a 
trial on the merits is the better alternative than this pro- 
posed settlement, lacks the appreciation of the significant 
legal obstacles Delaware Counsel face in overcoming the 
motions to dismiss, 
as well as the substantial 
benefits 
obtained through the proposed settlement.

that the issue of cumulative voting, as set forth in the

173 
for vote by all shareholders. Since the proposed settle- 
ment requires that a majority of the board be outside 
directors, as defined therein, it is further submitted that 
concerns regarding a non-independent board ha 
addressed and resolved.
