: Based on the facts and circumstances of this case, the 
significant risks and burdens taced by plaintiffs if the 
Delaware Action were to proceed, and the substantial 
procedures regarding corporate compliance which the Di- 
rector Defendants and ADM have agreed to implement, 
Delaware Counsel believe that the proposed settlement is 
fair, reasonable and extremely advantageous to the share- 
holders, is fully supported by the record in this case, and 
should be approved. Delaware plaintiffs also respectfully 
request that Delaware Cour.sels’ application for an award 
of attorneys’ fees and reimbursement of expenses in an 
amount of 3343 % of the Settlement Fund ($2,664,000) 
plus interest be granted with an equal allocation between 
Delaware and Illinois plaintiffs’ counsel.

I. INTRODUCTION AND SYNOPSIS 
1. Iam a Professor at Rutgers University—Faculty of 
Management in the Economics-Finance area and have 
been at this institution since 1969. 
2. The purpose of my Affidavit is to provide corporate 
governance and shareholder market valuation perspectives 
relevant 
to 
the 
Archer 
Daniels 
Midland 
Company 
(“ADM” or the “Company”), including the economic 
benefits accruing to ADM and its shareholders as a re- 
sult of the settlement 
of the derivative 
actions 
(the 
“Settlement”). The discussion of the settlement builds 
upon and, indeed, is integrated within relevant economic 
concepts and empirics. Based on economic-financial prin- 
ciples applied to the Settlement, I conclude that the Settle- 
ment offers features that will have important long-term 
benefits for the Company and its shareholders. 
175 
3. The organization of the Affidavit 
is as follows: 
Section II discusses my professional qualifications as an 
expert in the area of financial economics and corporate 
governance. Section III discusses the nature of the 
ration, and the potential conflict between the shareholders 
and incumbent management. Section IV discusses the roles 
of the board of directors in corporate governance. 
A 
synopsis of some of the relevant literature dealing with 
the importance of effective corporate governance is pro- 
vided. A number of interesting empirical observations 
including the importance of independent directors 
are 
noted. 
Section V provides perspectives on both the old 
structure at ADM. 
It is noted that the old governance 
structure at ADM was characterized by cronyism and pas- 
sivity. As a result of the settlement, there will be an 
emphasis on independent directors and an information 
flow from an in-place corporate reporting system. 
This 
will result in a much more vibrant corporate governance 
structure at the Company. 
Section VI provides an eco- 
nomic understanding of some of the proposed provisions 
of the settlement and their value-enhancing aspects to the 
Company and its shareholders. 
Section VII constitutes 
the Summary and Conclusions.

Ill. 
THE NATURE OF THE CORPORATIO N: 
AND CORPORATE CONTROL MECHANISMS 
9. The modern corporation is a particular business 
form that has certain economic advantages in organizing 
production, marketing and finance and, hence, 
in maxi- 
mizing the economic welfare of the firm’s shareholders 
‘10. The normative goal of the corporation is to maxi- 
mize the price of equity shares for its present sharehold- 
ers, This normative corporate objective obviates the need 
to take into account the particular consumption tastes and 
risk preferences of the individual investors or of the insti- 
tutional common equity shareholders of a given company. 
11. Indeed, one economic advanta 
corporation is that decision making ak hs 0 Boat ge 
corporate managers without the need to specifically elicit 
the risk and investment preferences of each or any 
of 
the many diverse shareholders of the firm. Thus Pn gn 
fulness of the shareholder maximization rule is that it 
provides a general structure for rational corporate deci- 
sion making without incurring the burden of the economic 
costs of shareholder polling. Indeed, one Nobel Memorial 
Prize in Economic Sciences awardee, Professor Ronald 
H. Coase of the University of Chicago—School of Law 
gives further insights with regard to why there is a cor- 
porate form of business organization. Coase argues (see 
Ronald H. Coase, “The Nature of the Firm,” Economica 

178 
November, 1937) that the corporate firm arises because 
of the savings in transaction costs, including monitoring 
costs, in operatting in a market where information is 
imperfect.* 
12. One important characteristic of the large publicly 
traded corporation is the separation of equity ownership 
(i.e., risk bearing) and decision making (i.e., resource 
allocation strategy and tactics). A corollary is that cor- 
porate executive management may make decisions that 
are not in the best interests of the shareholders. Further- 
more, the executive management team may continue year 
after year to act in this manner despite their poor per- 
formance, because there is relatively little most investors 
can do to correct this agency problem. 
13. One possible way 
that the interests 
of share- 
holders can be protected is through a board of directors, 
who recognize their fiduciary responsibilities 
to share- 
holders. and have or are provided systematic, directed, 
and relevant information flow through an in-place cor- 
porate reporting system from all levels of the corporation. 
IV. 
THE ROLES OF THE BOARD OF DIRECTORS 
IN CORPORATE GOVERNANCE 
14. As mentioned, the monitoring of management de- 
cisiors by a board of directors—particularly outside di- 
rectors who have access 
to systematic, directed, and 
relevant information flow through an in-place corporate 
reporting system from all levels of the corporation—is an 
important mechanism for protecting the interests of the 
1 Coase answers the question as to why corporations as an organi- 
zation form exist and continue to persist. Coase’s answer is “that 
firms increase efficiency by minimizing the costs of making and 
monitoring agreements among employees, suppliers and customers, 
thereby, in turn, lowering the costs of contracting for goods and 
services.” 
See LL. Gordon Crovitz, “Ronald Coase, Practical Econo- 
mists: A Nobel Prize for Privatization, Property Rights and Con- 
tracts,” Barron’s, October 28, 1991.

15. Chancellor William 
T. Allen of the Delaware 
Chancery Court, and a leading judicial scholar on cor- 
porate law, has discussed a board’s duty to insure that 
each director receives information sufficient to satisfy his/ 
her responsibility and that systems be in place to achieve 
this goal. Specifically in the Caremark decision, Chancel- 
lor Allen held “it is important that the board exercise a 
good faith judgment that the corporation’s information 
and reporting system is in concept and design adequate 
to assure the board that appropriate information 
will 
come to its attention in a timely manner as a matter of 
ordinary operations, so that it may satisfy its responsi- 
bility. 
_ This is important because, as Chancellor Allen 
notes, “I am of the view that a director’s obligation in- 
cludes a duty to attempt in good faith to assure that a 
corporate information and reporting system, which the 
board concludes is adequate, exists and that failure to do 
so under some circumstances may render a director liable 
for losses caused by non-compliance with applicable legal 
standar 
.” (See William T. Allen, In re Caremark In- 
ternational Inc. Derivative Litigation, Consolidated Civil 
Action No. 13670, September 25, 1996. ) 
16. Dennis Block and Jonathan M. Hoff 
the Caremark decision of Chancellor William T. Allen. 
They note that board directors do have oversight responsi- 
bilities, and that the corporate directors have a duty of care 
to act on an informed basis, which relates to the presence 
of information flow and from and in-place corporate re- 
porting system. 
Thus, the Caremark decision points to 
corporate directors having fiduciary duties 
to perform 
monitoring functions based on information flows gathered 
by corporate in-place reporting systems. 
(See Dennis J 
Block and Jonathan M. Hoff, “Duty of ‘Caremark’ Di. 
rectors’ Oversight Function,” N

November 21, 1996.)

17. The importance of the Caremark decision by Chan- 
cellor William T. Allen has been far-reaching. 
For ex- 
ample, Caremark has already effected “proper governance” 
in the financial services-banking area. 
In this regard, 
Mr. Serino, Deputy Chief Counsel—Office of the Comp- 
troller of the Currency, has indicated the importance of 
an internal ethics compliance program and that “institu- 
tions that adopt an effective compliance program can look 
to a reduction of fines and reduced jail time for its officials 
over those who don’t.” 
(See James Del Guerico, South- 
ern California Business, April 1, 1997.)

In commenting on Caremark, it is noted that a

stresses 
the importance

with particular

19. The substantial implications of Caremark for di- 
rectors has been pointed out by numerous authors. 
After 
all, Chancellor Allen pointed out that “a director’s ob- 
ligation includes a duty to attempt in good faith to assure 
that a corporate information and reporting system, which 
the board concludes is adequate, exists, and that failure 
to do so... may... render a director liable for losses 
caused by non-compliance with applicable legal standards.” 
It puts an important responsibility on directors to be 
accountable for an in-place, two-way compliance report- 
ing system. This means that there are to be regular reports 
to the director and board, evaluating and assessing its 
adequacy and any potential problems that might arise 
from this system.

21. Professor Eugene Fama of the University of Chi- 
cago has Suggested that external or outside directors may 
be more effective than internal or inside directors. 
Pro- 
fessor Fama argues that outside directors are concerned 
with protecting or enhancing their professional reputations 
as competent managers and typically owe less allegiance 
to incumbent management than inside directors. 
Hence, 
outside directors are more likely than inside directors not 
to rubber-stamp corporate strategies that might lower the 
market price of common stock. 
Thus, Professor Fama’s 
oe suggests that the monitoring of corporate per- 
ormance by outside directors may be a low cost, rela- 
tively efficient mechanism for improving corporate gov- 
ernance and firm performance. 
(See Eugene F. Fama, 
“Agency Problem and the Theory of the 
Firm,” 
of Political Economy, April, 1980.) 
veto tag 
22. An important emperical study 
i 
igati 
; 
y investigating 
the 
ee effect of outside directors versus inside Ban 
ors in effecting CEO turnover and internal change in 
corporate control is Michael Weisbach, “Outside Direc- 
tors and CEO Turnover,” J 
} 
pared A 
ournal of Financial Eco- 

182 
23. The Weisbach empirical study is based on a bi- 
varicated sample of firms that are classified as either 
insider-director dominated or outsider-director dominated. 
He found that outsider-dominated boards are more prone 
to monitor incumbent management and to use unfavor- 
able common stock return performance as a basis to re- 
move the incumbent CEO relatively quickly. 
Professor 
Weisbach also found that CEO executive management 
changes result in an increase in equity share prices for 
companies 
that 
have outsider-dominated 
boards. 
For 
insider-dominated boards, such CEO changes do not re- 
sult in additional positive shareholder value. 
24. Thus, the Weisbach and Fama studies provide an 
economic rationale and empirical support for the general 
proposition that outsider-dominated boards provide a po- 
tentially effective monitoring mechanism. 
25. Korn/Ferry International 
argues that the other 
important tasks of the board are (1) monitoring finan- 
cial performance, (2) CEO development, evaluation and 
succession, (3) monitoring the legal and ethical perform- 
ance of the corporation and (4) crisis management. 
(See 
Korn/Ferry 
International, 
Board 
Meeting 
in Session, 
“Twenty ‘Best Practices’ Improve Board Performance,” 
New York, N.Y., April, 1997.) 
26. The “Keys to Effectiveness” for a board, in the 
opinion of Korn/Ferry International, are (1) expertise, 
(2) information, 
(3) power, (4) motivation and (5) 
focus. 
27. 
In this regard, Ira Milstein of Weil, Gotshal & 
Manges has argued “that an independent, attentive board 
is the grain in the balance that leads to a corporate ad- 
vantage. 
A performing board is most likely to respond 
effectively to a world where the pace of change is accel- 
erating. An inert board is more likely to produce leader- 
ship that circles the wagons.” 
(See Ira Millstein, The 
New York Times, April 6, 1997.)

28. The conventional 
view of the responsibilities 
corporate directors, in 
the words of Chancellor William 
Allen, is “badly deficient.” 
The conventional perception is that boards should 
select senior management, create incentive compen- 
sation schemes and then step back and watch the 
organization prosper. In addition, board members 
should be available to act as advisors to the CEO 
when called upon and they should be prepared to 
act during 
a crisis: an emergency succession prob- 
a TREAD © Se DREN? SeapeaeN, See 
This view of the responsibilities of membership 
the board of directors of a public company is, ‘in 
my opinion, badly deficient. 
It ignores a most basic 
responsibility : the duty to monitor the performance 
of senior Management in an informed way. Outside 
directors 
should 
function 
as 
active 
monitors 
of 
corporate Management, not just in 
crisis, 
bu 
tinually; they should have an be 
aie 
formulation of the long-term strategic, financial, and 
organizational goals of the corporation and should 
approve plans to achieve those goals; they should as 
well engage in the periodic review of short and long- 
ror performance accord ing to plan and be prepared 
— for correction when in their judgment there 
(See William T. Allen,

29. In other words, Chancellor 
Allen’s 
vi 
corporate responsibilities of the board dhecties ichedes 
~ duty to monitor the executive management team both 
roadly and narrowly. This, in turn, requires that there 

184 
be systematic, directed, and relevant information flows 
and the availability, if need be, of a centralized reporting 
place for employees 
to report violations of company 
policy and the law. Obviously, corporate directors have 
a further need to assimilate this information and act in 
an informed way to structure and implement strategies 
and tactics designed to improve the operating results of 
the company. 
30. In summary, corporate boards, if effective, are a 
tremendously important mechanism to bolster the market 
price of the stock for the Company and its shareholders. 
If the corporate directors are effective, then the company 
will be run efficiently and the shareholders will prosper. 
This requires, as Chancellor Allen suggests, that there 
will be systematic, directed, and relevant information flow 
to the corporate directors and that they take an activist 
role in strategy formulation and monitoring. 
If, however, 
the corporate directors are not effective in formulating 
and monitoring a meaningful relevant corporate strategy, 
or in having/creating a systematic, directed, and relevant 
information flow through a corporate reporting system, 
and in choosing the best management team to implement 
the corporate strategy-tactics, the shareholders will suffer 
a significant wealth loss. Boards are usually more effec- 
tive from an economic as well as governance standpoint 
if there are a number of independent, or better yet a 
majority of independent, board members. 
However, a 
corollary of this is that independent directors become 
much more effective, if there is systematic, directed, and 
relevant information flow. Indeed, these conclusions con- 
cerning the importance of outsider-dominated boards to 
the company and its shareholders are rigorously estab- 
lished in the literature reviewed in this section. Similarly, 
the importance of systematic, directed and relevant in- 
formation flow to outside directors is crucial for outside 
directors to fulfill their heightened director responsibilities 
as detailed by Chancellor Allen.

VI. THE ARCHER DANIELS MIDLAND 
SETTLEMENT 
35. Detailed below is a general description 
f the Set- 
tlement. Specific emphasis is given to 
particular 
2 
i 
sions of Board Structure and Policies, ” 
fee 
36. As to Board Structures and Polici 
with a number of issues, including the oa 

186 
or ratio of outside directors, as well as the definition of 
what is meant by outside directors, and economic issues 
involving a conflict of interests or potential conflict of 
interests. 
37. One important provision of the Settlement is that 
a majority of the board of directors must be outside di- 
rectors. This requirement is far superior to an aspiration; 
it offers ADM no opportunity or flexibility to circumvent, 
for whatever reason, the mandate that the majority of 
directors be outside directors. 
38. The definition of outside directors is also specific, 
rather than simply being vague and ambiguous. 
It re- 
quires that any outside director (1) cannot be a present 
employee or a former senior executive; (2) does not have 
any relationship with ADM in a business or professional 
sense; (3) does not have, with any member of executive 
management, a relationship via blood or marriage; (4) 
does not have employment in a company in which the 
chairman or CEO of ADM is a board member, and 
(5) does not receive compensation from ADM in any 
capacity other than that as a director. 
39. Another important provision concerns the report- 
ing mechanisms put into place as part of the Settlement. 
Specifically, senior corporate officers will now be required 
to report on a regular basis, not less than three times a 
year, to the Audit Committee regarding compliance with 
ADM’s policies and procedures and all federal and state 
laws. Additionally, the Audit Committee will report regu- 
larly to the full Board of Directors regarding the effec- 
tiveness of the Company’s monitoring and compliances 
policies and procedures. 
40. The creation of a board having a majority of 
independent directors, coupled with enhanced board in- 
formation gathering and reporting systems, has important 
therapeutic value and value-enhancing consequences for 
the Company and its shareholders. 
These provisions will 
187 
help ensure that ADM enters the twenty-first century as a 
model of appropriate corporate 
governance 
best interests of shareholders. 
“fi 
gor sh iar 
41. Another provision of the Settlement deals with 
conflict situations involving directors. 
This provision is 
anticipatory in the sense that if a potential conflict arises 
then there is an in-place corporate governance mechanism 
to deal with this problem. 
Specifically, 
this provision 
indicates that the board of directors will help to anticipate 
whether there are any possible conflicts of interest. The 
board will use its judgment to determine on a case-by- 
case basis whether a board member should be excluded 
from voting because of a conflict of interest. In all cases 
where a board vote is required, it shall be the case that 
the majority of directors will be disinterested and in- 
dependent regarding the situation where the directors’ vote 
is required. 
42. Other important provisions of the Settlement con- 
cern the Audit and Governance Committees. The Audit 
Committee's responsibilities have been enhanced to in- 
clude monitoring and oversight—-i.e. whether legal com- 
pliance issues have been properly fulfilled. 
These issues 
of legal compliance include discrimination, corruption 
activities in foreign countries environment codes, securities 
laws, disclosure obligations as well as any anticompetition 
practices that might lead to criminal or civil prosecution. 
43. The composition of the Audit Committee 
been structured in a manner consistent with the ies 
of Proper” corporate governance. 
Specifically, the mem- 
bers will consist entirely of outside directors who became 
board members after June 25, 1995. It will have its own 
— — sieges which will be hired by this Com- 
ittee and 
will 
have full 
i 
i 
macs nn 
authority with regard to issues 
_ 44. Another equally important provision is 
tion of a Governance Committee. 
The ann eth

Committee will consist of all nonmanagement directors. 
Its task will be to set performance standards and criteria 
to evaluate 
the actual performance 
of ADM!’s 
Chief

The standards and criteria used to 
evaluate the actual performance of the CEO will be pub- 
lished in the proxy statement for the annual meeting of 
shareholders. 
45. These 
provisions 
will 
result 
in the Settlement 
achieving 
substantial improvements 
in the governance 
structure and thus in the accountability of ADM’s CEO 
and executive management. 
This will likely lead to sig- 
nificantly improved operating cost and economic profit- 
cash flow performance at ADM and its associated busi- 
ness units. Ergo, it will result in a corporate strategy that 
will increase the market price of ADM’s common stock. 
Alternatively stated, the Settlement will confer substantial 
economic benefits upon the Company and its shareho} iers 
by better aligning the economic interests of the Com- 
pany’s Board of Directors and the Company's executive 
management with those of the Company’s common share- 
holders. 
This will establish a pragmatic basis on which 
the financial markets can be confident that the ADM 
executive management team will undertake strategy deci- 
sions for the benefit of the Company and its common 
shareholders. 
Hence, there will be a higher market price 
particularly in the long run for ADM common stock as 
the benefits from improved corporate governance and a 
value enhancing corporate strategy are made more appar- 
ent to investors through the information flows to the finan- 
cial markets and the reality of these benefits 
is made 
apparent to all investors. 
46. 
Interestingly, in my 1991 Sears Affidavit sutmit- 
ted to examine the impact of substantially similar corpo- 
rate governance changes to Sears, Roebuck, my economic 
assessment of the impact of the changed governance struc- 
ture was that there were substantial long-run benefits that 
would flow to the company and the Sears shareholders 
189 
from the Sears Settlement. This assessment was on target 
and, in retrospect, predictive of what occurred 
to the 
stock price. 
Alternatively stated, in the Sears litigation, 
I maintained that there would be substantial economic 
benefits conferred to the shareholders in the long run as 
a result of this Settlement. 
As the actual time series of 
common stock prices of Sears has unfolded, my economic 
benefit assessment has been uncannily accurate. 
47. 
In the Sears case, some of the essential corporate 
goveranance changes were similar to those here. 
For 
example, there was an emphasis on adding new and addi- 
tional outside directors and that at least 75% of the 
Board had to be comprised of outside directors and that 
the key committees were made to be comprised of inde- 
pendent, outside directors. 
In fact, the foundation of this 
Settlement was modeled on the Sears Settlement that was 
structured with my assistance. The one important change 
in the Settlement has been the addition of the corporate 
information system dimension that is a result of Chancel- 
lor Allen’s decision in Caremark. 
Specifically, the Settle- 
ment has the additional substantial benefit of a systematic 
directed, and relevant information flow and in-place cor- 
porate reporting system enhancing the effectiveness of the 
outside board members 
and the corpor ate 
governan 
structure at ADM.

es 
48. In my Sears affidavit, I opined that there was 
substantial long-term economic benefits that would accrue 
to the Sears and the shareholders as a result of the Settle- 
ment. This economic conclusion is even stronger in the 
instant case because of the additional benefits obtained 
here in light of the Caremark decision.

I. INTRODUCTION 
1. Terry Rose Saunders is a principal in the firm 
Law Offices of Terry Rose Saunders “| Robert M. obo 
man is a partner in the firm of Spector & Roseman, P.C. 
Ms. Saunders and Mr. Roseman 
(“Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead 
Counsel”) have been actively involved in the prosecution 
of this litigation, are fully familiar with the proceedings 
and, if called upon, are competent to testify to the mat- 
ters set forth in this Declaration. 
2. We submit this Declaration in support of 
(a) 
the 
proposesd settlement (the “Settlement”) of this al 
action between plaintiffs, Paul Felzen, Trustee of Louise 
Laskin Trust—1991 and Sandra Esner, as shareholders 
of Archer Daniels Midland Company, nominal defendant 
Archer Daniels Midland Company (“ADM”) 
and the 
Seventeen individual defendants who were directors of 
ADM when the events that led to this litigation occurred 
(“the Director Defendants”), and (b) plaintiffs’ counsel's 
joint application for an award of attorneys’ 
imbursement of expenses. 
saad 
ial 

196 
3. The parties have entered into this Settlement in 
good faith and after extended arms-length negotiations by 
experienced and informed counsel. This Declaration, the 
Affidavit of John C. Coffee (“Coffee Affidavit”) and the 
Declaration of R. Alan Miller 
(“Miller Declaration”) 
provide factual support for the conclusion of plaintiffs 
and their counsel that the Settlement is fair, reasonable 
and adequate. 
4. After two years of litigation, including a hard- 
fought motion to dismiss and six months of settlement 
negotiations, the parties have agreed to a Settlement that 
will result in benefits for ADM and its shareholders that 
go beyond those which plaintiffs could have achieved 
through litigation. The Settlement will result in substan- 
tial changes in the composition and functioning of ADM’s 
Board of Directors and key Committees. These structural 
changes are designed to prevent recurrence of ADM’s 
recent problems and change the public perception of 
ADM. 
5. Specifically, the corporate governance measures re- 
quire ADM to maintain a Board of Directors without ties 
to management that will be expected to exercise inde- 
pendent judgment and monitor corporate conduct effec- 
tively. These measures also require the Audit Committee 
to take specific steps to ensure that ADM complies with 
the law. Moreover, the Settlement creates an $8 million 
fund to be paid from the Directors and Officers Liability 
Insurance (the “Settlement Proceeds”) for the Company 
to pay for these benefits, including independent outside 
legal counsel to assist the Audit Committee in its legal 
compliance tasks, and to pay any award of attorneys’ 
fees and reimbursement of expenses made by this Court. 
6. The Settlement is an outstanding result for ADM 
and its share holders. 
Not only did plaintiffs face legal 
and factual obstacles to ultimate success on the merits, 
but even if successful, they faced the prospect of no re- 
197 
covery. 
Any amount plaintiffs could 
to 
from the defendants would pale in oie opie 
hundreds of millions of dollars ADM has had to spend as 
the result of its antitrust problems. 
This Settlement as- 
sures that ADM will promptly adopt measures that ap- 
proximate the “state of the art” in American corporate 
governance today and will help guard against the signifi- 
cant financial liability that could result from having cor- 
porate officers violate criminal or civil laws in the future. 
7. This Declaration also 
provides factual support 
the request of Plaintiffs’ pl for an award of he 
neys’ fees of 40 percent ($3,200,000) of the Settlement 
Proceeds, and reimbursement of expenses of $76,501.69 
to be paid from the Settlement Proceeds. 
As explained 
in the Miller Declaration, this amount is a small per- 
centage (2 percent) of the total value of the benefit ob- 
tained for ADM and its shareholders 
(conservatively 
$158 million). 
It is also less than the amount of attor-

HISTORY OF THE LITIGATION 
A. 
Background Events. 
9. Two years ago, agents of the Federal Bureau of 
Investigation and the Antitrust Division of the Depart- 
ment of Justice arrived at the homes of numerous ADM 
executives and managers and ADM’s corporate 
head- 
quarters. 
The government sought documents to support 

198 
its ongoing, but previously undisclosed, investigation into 
tom on ADM was fixing prices of citric acid, high- 
fructose corn syrup (“HFCS”) and lysine. Two weeks 
later, The Wall Street Journal reported that the FBI had 
been investigating ADM since 1992, when Mark Whit- 
acre, a senior executive of ADM, approached the FBI 
concerning price fixing activities in which he claimed to 
be involved at ADM. Early reports stated that the gov- 
ernment had videotaped Michael Andreas, the Company's 
Vice-Chairman 
and son 
of Dwayne Andreas, ADM’s 
Chairman and Chief Executive 
Officer, and Terrance 
Wilson, President of the ADM Corn Processing Division 
and Group Vice President, meeting with competitors to 
discuss plans to limit the production of lysine. 
These 
press reports further disclosed that Howard Buffett had 
resigned from ADM’s Board of Directors in early July 
“because he was troubled by the investigation and the 
way the Company was handling it”, and because he had 
“lost confidence in whether he was being accurately in- 
formed by other [ADM] executives about the details of 
the antitrust matter.” 
This news fueled concern about 
whether ADM, long criticized for being run as a “family 
fiefdom” of Dwayne Andreas, would respond appropri- 
ately to the investigation. 
10. Shortly after the government investigation was dis- 
closed, purchasers of ADM’s lysine, high-fructose corn 
syrup and citric acid brought civil antitrust class — 
alleging that ADM and others had violated the federa 
antitrust laws by agreeing to fix prices and allocate or 
limit production. 
In addition, ADM shareholders com- 
menced class actions against ADM and certain officers 
for violating the federal securities laws by misrepresenting 
the Company's earnings, profitability, results of opera- 
tions and business prospects in connection with the sale 
of ADM’s common stock.

approximately 
15% from 
. 
per sha 
Pan oe 1995, the pric, Se es 
the FBI raids, to $14.28 
per share on July 10, 1995. 
199 
11. Whitacre had also alleged that ADM rewarded top 
executives with foreign pay deposited in overseas 
ac- 
counts. 
According 
to Whitacre, 
these 
so-called 
slush 
funds were part of a Company policy and were common 
knowledge to senior executives. 
B. 
Plaintiffs 
Commence This Action. 
12. The plaintiffs 
in this action 
are current 
share- 
holders of ADM who also held ADM stock in June 1995. 
Plaintiff Paul Felzen, Trustee of Louise Laskin Trust— 
1991, filed his complaint on July 11, 1995 in the United 
States District Court for the Northern District of Ilinois. 
Plaintiff Sandra Esner filed her complaint on the same 
date in the Circuit Court of Cook County, Illinois. Deidra 
Graulich filed a second federal court complaint on July 
12, 1995, in the Northern District of Hlinois. On August 
?, 1995, defendants removed the Esner Action 
to the 
United States District Court for the Northern 
District 
of Illinois. 
13. The derivative complaints alleged 
that the Di- 
rector Defendants breached their fiduciary duties to the 
Company and its stockholders by causing ADM to violate 
federal antitrust and securities laws and failing to moni- 
tor ADM’s operations and employees to prevent violations 
of the law. On August 4, 1995, the defendants moved 
to transfer the Felzen and Graulich actions to the Central 
District of Illinois. 
Defendants added the Esner action 
after it was removed. 
Plaintiffs opposed the transfer. 
14. 
After conducting 
significant 
additional 
research 
and analysis of publicly available information about ADM 
and its directors, plaintiffs Felzen and Esner filed a Pro- 
posed Consolidated and Amended Derivative Complaint 
(the “First Consolidated Complaint”) on September 29, 
1995. 
and named 
as defendants Dwayne O. Andreas, 
Ralph Bruce, John H. Daniels, Howard G. Buffett, Lowell 
W. Andreas, James R. Randall, Michael D. Andreas, 

200 
H.D. Hale, O. Glenn Webb, Martin Andreas, Margaretta 
Rockefeller, F. Ross Johnson, Ray A. Goldberg, John K. 
Vanier, Shreve M. Archer, Jr., M. Brian Mulroney and 
Robert S. Strauss (the “Director Defendants”). The First 
Consolidated Complaint 
alleged that the Director De- 
fendants (1) breached their fiduciary duties by partici- 
pating in illegal price-fixing or not properly monitoring 
ADM'’s personnel and operations to prevent illegal price- 
fixing and illegal foreign payments and (2) grossly mis- 
managed ADM. 
15. The First Consolidated Complaint 
also alleged 
that, during the time period of the antitrust conspiracy, 
Dwayne Andreas, ADM’s Chairman and Chief Executive 
Officer, dominated and controlled the Board. Many ADM 
Board members were Andreas family members or friends, 
Company officers, close relatives of Company officers, or 
had ties with the Andreas family that raised serious ques- 
tions as to whether the Board had performed 
its role 
properly or had been properly diligent. 
Plaintiffs alleged 
that these relationships made it highly unlikely that the 
Board would take effective action to investigate and rem- 
edy wrongdoing by Company executives. On October 17, 
1995, plaintiff Deidra Graulich voluntarily dismissed the 
Graulich Action without prejudice. 
16. Defendants moved to dismiss the First Consoli- 
dated Complaint or, in the alternative, to stay the action 
on October 31. 1995. 
On that same date, the United 
States District Court for the Northern District of Illinois 
ordered 
that the litigation, along with pending share- 
holder securities class actions, be transferred to the United 
States District Court for the Central District of Illinois. 
The litigation was subsequently transferred. 
17. Defendants refiled their motion to dismiss or, in 
the alternative, to stay the action in the Central District 
Court on December 27, 1995. 
Defendants argued that 
plaintiffs had failed to make a demand upon the directors 
201 
of ADM as required by Delaware law before bringing 
their action, that plaintiffs failed to state a claim for 
relief against the defendants and that, Article XIV of 
ADM'’s Certificate of Incorporation shielded the defend- 
ants from liability for the misconduct plaintiffs alleged 
because it involved only a breach of their duty of care. 
18. Shortly after the transfer order, plaintiffs served 
a document request on defendants. 
At a status hearing 
before the Court on February 26, 1996, the Court ruled 
that plaintiffs 
in the class and derivative 
shareholder 
actions were entitled to discovery of documents defend- 
ants had produced to the government or that the govern- 
ment had seized and that plaintifis could request addi- 
tional documents. 
Plaintiffs counsel reviewed and cata- 
logued the tens of thousands of documents defendants 
produced but many categories of documents 
plaintiffs 
needed to prove their case were not produced. 
When 
plaintiffs sought additional limited discovery, defendants 
objected and filed a motion for a protective order to 
forestall any further discovery. 
19. This 
litigation 
quickly 
focused 
on 
the 
issues 
Jefendants raised in their motions to dismiss and, in par- 
ticular, whether plaintiffs were required to make demand 
on ADM'’s Board before bringing this action and whether 
defendants were shielded from liability under Delaware 
law because they had acted in good faith. 
Defendants’ 
motions to dismiss the First Consolidated Complaint and 
‘or a protective order, both of which had been fully 
briefed, were set for hearing on April 26, 1996. At that 
hearing, the Court granted defendants’ motion to dismiss 
without prejudice and gave plaintiffs leave to amend. 
C. 
The Consolidated and Amended Derivative Com- 
plaint 
20. Plaintiffs filed a Consolidated and Amended De- 
rivative Complaint (the “Complaint”) on May 17, 1996. 

202 
By that time, Mark Whitacre had disclosed many of the 
details of the price-fixing activities in which he claimed 
that he and others at ADM had been involved. Whitacre 
disclosed meetings that he, Michael Andreas and Terrance 
Wilson attended with ADM’s competitors in the lysine 
business where prices and market allocation were 
dis- 
cussed. 
Whitacre stated publicly that illegal price-fixing 
was “fairly common” at ADM and had been going on 
since the early 1990's. 
Whitacre referred to comments 
made by the Company's President and its General Coun- 
sel, indicating that they were aware of price-fixing at the 
Company. 
Whitacre’s accounts portrayed a corporation 
rife with criminal conduct at the highest levels and impli- 
cated one director, M.D. Andreas, directly and two others, 
Dwayne Andreas and James Randall, indirectly. 
More- 
over, ADM continued to receive low marks from cor- 
porate governance experts and institutional shareholders, 
such as the California Public Employees’ Retirement Sys- 
tem (“CalPERS”), for its lack of both responsiveness to 
shareholders and Board independence. 
21. The Complaint contained the results of counsel's 
review and analysis of the additional publicly available 
information on ADM and the Director Defendants. 
This 
case was unusual in that senior officials of the Company, 
with close ties to ADM’s Board, were being investigated 
for criminal conduct and had exposed the Company to 
hundreds of millions of dollars 
in potential 
fines and 
damages. 
The Complaint incorporated this information 
in support of allegations that Michael Andreas, Dwayne 
Andreas and James Randall participated or assisted in 
the wrongdoing and that all directors were responsible for 
the wrongdoing at ADM because they had neither ful- 
filled their duties to prevent, detect and seek redress for 
criminal conduct nor exercised independent judgment in 
their oversight responsibilities. 
22. With leave of the Court, plaintiffs filed a Supple- 
ment to the Complaint in November 1996 and an Amend- 
203 
ment and Supplement 
to the Complaint 
in December 
1996. 
The Supplement 
updated 
the Complaint. 
The 
Amendment and Supplement to the Complaint alleged 
that the Director Defendants—other than Howard Buffett 
—had further breached their fiduciary oversight duties by 
failing to cause ADM to cooperate with the government 
in its investigation and minimalize criminal liability by 
demonstrating that ADM intended 
to remedy, prevent 
reoccurrence of, and seek redress for wrongdoing. 
D. 
Defendants’ Further Motions to Dismiss and Objec- 
tions to Discovery. 
23. Defendants moved to dismiss the Complaint on 
June 7, 1996. This motion was fully briefed. Defendants 
argued that plaintiffs had failed to make a pre-suit de- 
mand on ADM’s Board of Directors as required by F. R. 
Civ. P. 23.1 and that demand was not excused in this 
case. 
Although agreeing that the relevant standard was 
whether the allegations of the complaint create a reason- 
able doubt that the Board could have exercised its inde- 
pendent and disinterested business judgment in responding 
to demand, the parties differed as to whether a majority 
of the Board could exercise such judgment. 
24. Defendants also argued that the Complaint failed 
to state a claim for relief against 14 of the 17 Director 
Defendants because factual support necessary 
to show 
their misconduct was not alleged. 
In February, 
1997, 
defendants filed a Supplemental Memorandum in Support 
of the Motion to Dismiss, arguing that plaintiffs’ new 
allegations relating to the Board's failure to cooperate 
with the government should be dismissed because plain- 
tiffs had failed to make demand on the Board and the new 
allegations failed to state a claim for relief. 
Defendants 
also argued 
that ADM’s 
Certificate 
of Incorporation 
shielded the Director Defendants from liability for the 
new allegations as well. 

204 
25. While the motion to dismiss was pending, plain- 
tiffs renewed their requests for documents. 
Defendants 
again objected 
to producing 
the requested documents 
because they had moved to dismiss the Complaint. 
Plain- 
tiffs filed a motion to compel production of documents, 
and this motion was fully briefed. 
26. The Court has not ruled on the motion to dis- 
miss or the motion to compel and has adjourned argu- 
ment on both motions pending the hearing on the Settle- 
ment. 
27. 
In late October, 1996, plaintiffs sought discovery 
relating to ADM’s wrongdoing from the Department of 
Justice, but the government refused to produce any of the 
evidence it possessed. 
E. 
ADM'’s Response to the Government's Investigation 
and Civil Litigation. 
28. 
After 
this 
lawsuit 
was 
commenced, 
ADM 
re- 
sponded to charges of price-fixing in ADM products and 
criticism of its Board. 
(a) ADM’s Board appointed a Special Committee of 
outside directors to oversee the Company's response to 
the government's investigation and related civil litigation. 
In April, 1996, ADM announced that it had agreed to 
pay $25 million to settle the lysine civil antitrust case. 
On September 27, 1996, ADM announced that it had 
-agreet to pay $35 million to settle the citric acid civil 
antitrust case. ADM also announced that it had agreed 
to pay $30 million to settle the federal securities class 
action brought by ADM stock purchasers. 
(b) In October 1995, ADM’s Board created the Cor- 
porate Governance Committee to review and make recom- 
mendations on corporate governance 
matters, 
such as 
Board structure and director qualifications. 
The Com- 
205 
mittee reported its recommendations to the Board on Jan- 
uary 15, 1996. Subsequently, the Board reduced the num- 
ber of directors, several directors, including Michael An- 
dreas, did not stand for re-election, and the Board nom- 
inated three new outside directors for the 1996 term. 
Michael Andreas also took a leave of absence from the 
Company. 
| 
29. On October 15, 1996, ADM entered into a plea 
agreement with the United States Department of Justice 
in which ADM agreed to plead guilty and pay a $100 
million criminal fine for fixing prices in the lysine market 
from June, 1992 through June 27, 1995 and for fixing 
prices in the citric acid market from January, 
1993 
through June 27, 1995. 
As part of the plea agreement, 
ADM agreed to cooperate with the government in current 
federal investigations of antitrust schemes. 
In return, the 
government agreed not to bring criminal charges against 
any current director, officer or employee of ADM (other 
than Michael D. Andreas and Terrance S. Wilson) for 
any antitrust violations covered in the ongoing investiga- 
tions. 
There has been no evidence that any director of 
ADM, other than Michael Andreas, was aware of any 
price-fixing activities, or that the ADM Board systemati- 
cally failed to exercise its oversight responsibilities. 
Il. 
SETTLEMENT NEGOTIATIONS AND 
THE SETTLEMENT 
A. 
Negotiation Of The Settlement And The Role Of 
Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel In Implementing Cor- 
porate Governance Reforms At ADM. 
30. By the Fall of 1996, Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel 
had concluded that ADM and its shareholders would be 
best served by a settlement that would change ADM’s 
corporate governance and legal compliance rather than 
by protracted and uncertain litigation aimed at a mone- 
tary recovery for ADM. 
As of this time, the Company 

206 
had agreed to pay $190 million in criminal fines and in 
settlement of civil antitrust and securities class actions. 
ADM had made certain changes to the Board’s member- 
ship after receiving the report of the Corporate Govern- 
ance Committee. 
Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel also recog- 
nized that, even 
if plaintiffs prevailed on the issue of 
demand futility—which was not certain—they would have 
difficulty demonstrating the defendants’ willful or system- 
atic failure to oversee the Company's operations necessary 
to establish liability under Delaware law. Moreover, Plain- 
tiffs faced the likelihood the Company would create 
a 
special litigation committee to investigate director liabil- 
ity for the claims in this litigation—an action that would 
result in delay and could result in a decision to dismiss 
the litigation. 
Finally, Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel took 
into account the primary interest of many ADM share- 
holders, including large institutional shareholders, which 
was to force changes in the way ADM’s Board—and 
therefore ADM—functioned. With this background, Plain- 
tiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel contacted counsel for defendants 
and ADM in September 1996 to initiate settlement dis- 
cussions. 
Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead counsel were told that the 
defendants were not prepared to discuss settlement at that 
time. 
31. 
After the Company entered into its plea agreement 
with the government and shortly before the November 
hearing on defendants’ motion to dismiss and plaintiffs’ 
motion to compel, defendants’ counsel contacted Plaintiffs’ 
Co-Lead Counsel to suggest continuing the hearing date 
to allow the parties to explore a possible settlement. 
Ac- 
cordingly, Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel began to prepare a 
settlement 
proposal to present 
to defendants. 
At the 
same time, however, Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel pressed 
forward with the litigation and filed two additional plead- 
ings. 
32. At the outset, 
Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel pre- 
sented corporate governance measures that institutional 
207 
shareholders had requested and organizations and pro- 
fessionals who specialized in corporate governance had 
recommended, and other demands to ensure an environ- 
ment at ADM that promoted Board independence and 
compliance with the law. Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel were 
aware that, at ADM’s October 1996 shareholder meet- 
ing, approximately 42% 
of ADM’s 
shareholders 
had 
voted in favor of an amendment to ADM’s by-laws to 
require a more restrictive definition of an outside director. 
ADM's Board had recommended a vote against the pro- 
posal. Also, 47% of the shareholders had voted in favor 
of a proposal requiring confidential voting, which ADM’s 
Board also opposed. 
Both these proposals had been of- 
fered by large institutional shareholders. Although ADM’s 
Board had nominated three new independent directors 
who were elected at the 1996 annual shareholders meet- 
ing, the Board had not implemented many of the recom- 
mendations of its Governance Committee; and ADM’s 
Audit, Nominating and Compensation Committees were 
not comprised exclusively of outside directors. 
33. 
Plaintiffs 
also sought 
a monetary 
contribution 
to the Company from the Director Defendants as part of 
the Settlement. 
Plaintiffs’ 
Co-Lead 
Counsel 
received 
ADM'’s Directors and Officers Liability Insurance in effect 
when the litigation was commenced. The policy contained 
a $10 million policy limit and an exclusion for all claims. 
including derivative actions, arising from antitrust 
ac- 
tions or proceedings. 
_ 
34. Immediately prior to the December 11, 1996 hear- 
ing on pending motions, Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel met 
with defendants’ counsel to present plaintiffs’ settlement 
demands. 
This framework 
Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel 
established for a settlement was how best to strengthen 
the independence of the Board, prevent reoccurrence of 
the kind of problems ADM had experienced, and dem- 
onstrate to shareholders and the public that ADM was 
committed to good corporate governance. 
To this end 
plaintiffs made a number of demands that included: 

208 
—Adopting the definition of outside director that 42% 
of ADM'’s shareholders had voted for; 
—Establishing 
procedures 
for ensuring 
that 
future 
Board candidates were independent; 
—Creating new Board Committees of outside directors 
with authority to investigate allegations of past mis- 
conduct, review current compliance and ensure future 
compliance with the law; 
—Retaining outside independent counsel to assist these 
two new Committees; 
—Requiring confidential voting; 
—Retaining claims agaiast Michael Andreas; 
—The resignation of Dwayne Andreas as CEO and 
Chairman; and 
—Some form of restitution to the Company by the 
Director Defendants for amounts expended to resolve 
criminal charges and civil litigation. 
35. 
After two meetings in December 
1996, one 
in 
Chicago and one in Washington, 
at which the parties 
negotiated plaintiffs’ demands, plaintiffs submitted a writ- 
ten settlement proposal to defendants that contained the 
corporate sovernance measures presented and discussed at 
meetings between counsel, including the retention of in- 
dependent counsel to assist Board Committees, a require- 
ment that claims against Micheel Andreas not be released 
and provision for some recoupment of monies to the Com- 
pany. depending on the outcome of negotiations between 
defendants and the insurance carrier. 
36. 
In connection with the negotiations, Plaintiffs’ Co- 
Lead Counsel met separately with ADM’s largest institu- 
tional shareholders. namely Kayla Gaylin, the general 
counsel of CalPERS, John Lukomnick, the Deputy Comp- 
troller for Pensions of New York City, who has responsi- 
209 
bility for the City’s pension fund investments, Bart Naylor 
of the Teamsters Pension Fund and Edward Durkin of 
the Carpenters Pension Fund, to solicit their views and 
feview plaintiffs’ proposals. 
In addition, Plaintiffs’ Co- 
Lead Counsel spoke with Ann Hanson and Ann Yeager 
of the Council of Institutional Investors. 
These views, 
Which primarily related to the definition of an outside 
director, were taken into account and, to the extent feasi- 
ble, incorporated in plaintiffs’ proposals. 
Plaintiffs’ Co- 
Lead Counsel recognized that not all of these views could 
be implemented through the settlement of this litigation 
and continued to focus on institutionalizing measures and 
Procedures that would provide the framework for an in- 
— Board and an exemplary 
legal compliance 
ogram.

of Law 
at Columbia 
University Law 
School, to make sure that the corporate governance meas- 
ures being discussed were meaningful and of value to the 
Company. 
Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel 
also consulted 
with R. Alan Miller, President of the Philadelphia Invest- 
ment Banking Company, who provided 
an economic 
ale of the settlement’s value to ADM and its share- 
38. During negotiations, defendants’ counsel reviewed 
With Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel the in-depth and far- 
reaching study that ADM’s Corporate Governance Com-

B. 
The Stipulation of Settlement. 
44. The Stipulation of Settlement negotiated by Plain- 
tiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel provides that the Board will adopt 
the following new corporate governance measures and 
procedures no later than 60 days after this Court's ap- 
proval of the Settlement and the dismissals of this litiga- 
tion and the Delaware action become final: 
—a new definition of an outside director that will be- 
come part of ADM’s By-Laws; 
—a limit on the number of management directors, other 
than the Chief Executive Officer, to two ADM employees; 
—a Nominating Committee comprised only of outside 
directors and, for the 1997 Board election, comprised 
exclusively of outside directors elected to the Board after 
the events that let [sic] to this lawsuit occurred; 
—an outside director, either the Chairman if he quali- 
fies or an elected Vice-Chairman, 
to preside over all meet- 
ings of non-management directors and the Governance 
Committee; 
—an Audit Committee comprised only of outside di- 
rectors and, for three years, comprised of outside directors 
who were elected to the Board after the events that led 
to this lawsuit occurred; 
—a requirement that the Audit Committee engage out- 
side independent counsel (to be paid from the settlement 
proceeds) for at least three years to advise it in connec- 
tion with its oversight and legal compliance responsibili- 
ties, including determining whether the Company is vio- 
lating any antitrust law, promulgating compliance policies 
and ensuring that employees adhere to these policies; 
—a Governance Committee of non-management direc- 
tors to assess Board and Committee effectiveness and 
evaluate the Chief Executive Officer;

in ADM stock with no increase in total compensation 
to 
a director 
for three years;

C. 
Evaluation Of The Proposed Settlement. 
48. Based on their factual and legal investigation of 
the claims and defenses in this litigation, the thorough 
briefing of two motions to dismiss, intense and protracted 
settlement negotiations, extensive consultation with ex- 
perts and ADM’s institutional investors, and their experi- 
ence and familiarity with other similar actions, Plaintiffs’ 
Co-Lead Counsel have concluded that the Settlement is 
fair, reasonable and adequate and in the best interests of 
ADM and its shareholders. The Settlement provides sub- 
stantial benefits, a significant portion of which could not 
have been achieved through litigation, and avoids further, 
protracted, difficult and costly litigation with a risk of no 
recovery. 
49. In analyzing the advantages and disadvantages of 
the settlement terms, Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel at all 
times took into account the views and interests of ADM’s 
shareholders, the benefit to the Corporation of the corpo- 
rate governance measures, the interest of the Company in 
avoiding protracted and expensive litigation directed at 
ADM’s past problems, and the risks of the litigation. 
50. At the outset, plaintiffs had to overcome defend- 
ants’ argument 
that plaintiffs cannot 
assert derivative 
claims because they failed to make pre-suit demand or 
demonstrate that demand was not required. 
Although 
plaintiffs believe they have a strong argument that a ma- 
jority of ADM’s Directors lacked the capacity to respond 
in good faith to a demand because they were interested 
in the litigation or were dominated by someone who had 
an interest, the outcome of this motion was not certain. 
51. Once plaintiffs overcame this motion, they would 
face a number of obstacles in attempting to establish 
defendants’ liability for the derivative claims. Defendants 
have argued that the conduct about which plaintiffs com- 
plain provides no basis for derivative liability because 
defendants acted reasonably and in good faith. 
Under 
52. Although ADM has been the subject of a federal 
criminal investigation and other civil litigation, none of 
these proceedings established findings or disclosed infor- 
mation that could be used to prove that the defendants 
in this action, other than Michael Andreas, breached their, 
fiduciary duties to the Company. Plaintiffs 
also would 
have had difficulty in obtaining evidence to support their 
claims because most—if not all—fact witnesses would be 
defendants or ADM employees or agents. 
53. 
Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Counsel took into account that 
the Company's director and officer liability insurance was 
limited and contained exclusions and that the Company 
could not reasonably expect to recover from the defend- 
ants anywhere near the more than $200 million that the 
antitrust violations had cost it. Therefore, a substantial 
monetary recovery was unlikely. 
54. Based on these factors, Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead Coun- 
sel have concluded that the Settlement is fair, reasonable 
and adequate and in the best interests of ADM and its 
shareholders. 

216 
IV. 
PLAINTIFFS’ COUNSEL’S REQUEST FOR 
ATTORNEYS’ FEES AND EXPENSES 
55. 
Plaintiffs’ counsel jointly request, as compensation 
for their substantial efforts and the results achieved, an 
aggregate fee award of $3,200,000, plus interest from 
June 2, 1997. Plaintiffs’ counsel also seek $76,501.69 for 
reimbursement of expenses necessarily incurred in the 
prosecution of this litigation. The entire amount of this 
fee request is to be paid out of the Settlement Proceeds, 
the $8 million fund that the Director Defendants created 
to pay for corporate governance measures and attorneys’ 
fees and expenses. No fees or expenses will be paid from 
ADM general corporate funds. 
56. As noted, ADM’s Corporate Governance Com- 
mittee recommended a number of measures to ADM’s 
Board in early 1996. 
At the time Plaintiffs’ Co-Lead 
Counsel entered into negotiations with defendants’ coun- 
sel, however, ADM had not adopted many of these meas- 
ures. 
Based on shareholder demands and expert advice, 
plaintiffs insisted not only that ADM implement these 
measures, but also that ADM further tighten the qualifi- 
cations for outside directors, amend its By-Laws to insti- 
tutionalize the new definition of an outside director, and 
change the qualifications for membership on key Com- 
mittees. 
57. As part of the Settlement, ADM’s Board agreed 
to adopt the corporate governance measures within 60 
days. ADM’s Board also agreed to a further restructur- 
ing of the Board and Committees that 
disqualified cer- 
tain Director Defendants from continuing to serve on the 
Audit, Nominating and Compensation Committees, cre- 
ated the Corporate Governance Committee, required con- 
fidential shareholder voting and required that director 
compensation be paid partially in stock. 
58. Moreover, ADM has agreed that a new Audit 
Committee, assisted by outside independent counsel to be 
217 
funded from Settlement Proceeds, will both (a) deter- 
mine whether the Company is engaging in anti-competi- 
tive activities and (b) promulgate policies and proce- 
dures regarding compliance with all laws and the Com- 
pany’s procedures for educating employees and circulating 
these policies. 
59. 
Plaintiffs’ 
counsel’s 
initiatives and perseverence 
were a substantial factor in ADM’s adoption of the meas- 
ures set forth in Exhibit A to the Stipulation of Settlement 
and were responsible for ADM’s Board adopting and im- 
plementing these measures promptly. 
60. 
Plaintiffs’ counsel were aware in the early stages 
of settlement discussions that they might not be compen- 
sated even if they were successful in settling this litigation 
because of the limited amount of insurance coverage and 
the exclusion of coverage for antitrust-related litigation. 
Plaintiffs’ counsel, however, did not allow this risk to 
impede their efforts to resolve this litigation for the bene- 
fit of ADM and its shareholders. 
It was only after Plain- 
tiffs’ counsel had spent months negotiating the settlement 
that they learned that the carrier had agreed to pay $8 
million. 
61. As set forth in the Miller Declaration, the eco- 
nomic value of this Settlement is $158 million. This value 
reflects the likelihood that the Settlement will avoid recur- 
rence of the misconduct that led to this litigation, and its 
costs to ADM, and the effect the corporate governance 
measures have on ADM’s market value. The total long- 
term economic value of this Settlement to ADM and its 
shareholders 
should be considered 
in determining the 
appropriate fee award in this case. 
62. The notice mailed to ADM shareholders stated 
that the Company and the Director Defendants agreed 
not to oppose an application for an award of attorney 
fees and expenses up to 49% of the Settlement Proceeds. 
Only three objections to the fee request have been sub- 
mitted.

a

would be no compensation. Moreover, Plaintiffs’ counsel

67. 
Plaintiffs’ counsel achieved this outstanding Settle- 
ment quickly and efficiently, without unnecessary attorney 
time and expense. 
68. 
Plaintiffs’ counsel are requesting attorneys’ fees of 
$3,200,000, plus interest, an amount that is forty percent 
(40%) of the Settlement Proceeds and less than the 
Company and the defendants have agreed not to oppose. 
We respectfully submit that Plaintiffs’ counsel’s fee re- 
quest is fair, reasonable and justified and that the Court 
should grant Plaintiffs’ counsel’s joint application for an 
award of attorneys’ fees and expenses. 
V. OBJECTIONS TO THE SETTLEMENT 
69. Two objections to the settlement have been filed, 
and three individual shareholders have objected—one in a 
letter to the Court—to an award of fees and expenses to 
plaintiffs’ counsel of 49% of the Settlement Proceeds. 
The Notice advised shareholders that the Director De- 
fendants and the Company agreed not to oppose applica- 
tions for fees and expenses that did not exceed 49% of 
the Settlement Proceeds ($3,920,000). 
70. One objection to the Settlement has been made by 
John J. Gilbert and Martin Glotzer because the Settle- 
ment does not require that ADM adopt cumulative voting. 
These objectors do not explain why cumulative voting 
should be part of this settlement. 
In fact, this objection 
suggests a change that is inconsistent with the Settle- 
ment’s objective of strengthening Board independence and 
having directors who will serve in the best interests of all 
shareholders. 
This objection should be denied. 
71. The second objection to the Settlement has been 
filed by Charles W. Pauly. Mr. Pauly contends that the 
221 
plaintiffs should be required to try their case because, if 
the claims have merit, the Settlement is inadequate, and 
if the case is without merit, the case should not be set- 
tled. Mr. Pauly not only overlooks the substantial non- 
monetary benefits from the Settlement, but he also fails 
to appreciate that plaintiffs would be unlikely to recover 
large sums of money from the Director Defendants even 
if they prevailed. He also apparently does not understand 
that this litigation was brought on behalf of ADM and 
that ADM is benefitting from the Settlement fund, not 
contributing to it. This objection should be denied. 
72. The three shareholders who object to fees, Mitchell 
Berns, John P. Chiara, and Peter D. Scott, state that they 
believe 49% is too high a percentage. All three objectors 
focus only on the $8 million fund and do not consider 
that the value of the Settlement is much greater, that 
ADM is not contributing to the fund but rather will be 
receiving more than half of it, and that plaintiffs’ efforts 
caused the fund to be created. The Director Defendants 
and the Company agreed that reasonable attorneys’ fees 
and expenses should be paid from the Settlement Proceeds 
and that an award of 49% ($3,920,000 plus interest) 
was acceptable to them. 
Plaintiffs’ counsel are requesting 
a fee of $3,200,000, plus interest, which represents 40% 
of the Settlement Proceeds, plus their expenses, which is 
less than the Director Defendants and the Company 
agreed not to oppose. In a letter to the Court, Mr. Chiara 
suggests further that there may be a conflict between 
plaintiffs and their counsel because the defendants do not 
object to the fee. In this case, however, there is no con- 
flict because Plaintiffs’ counsel’s fees are not paid from 
any recovery that would otherwise go to the individual 
plaintiffs. 
Further, the Company is represented by inde- 
pendent counse!, who have reviewed the fairness to ADM 
of all the terms of the Settlement, including the agreement 
not to oppose an award of fees and expenses of un to 
$3,920,000. These objections should be denied. 

222 
CONCLUSION 
73. 
Plaintiffs’ 
Counsel 
believe 
that the Settlement, 
which will promptly bring about significant and highly 
valuable corporate governance reforms for the benefit of 
ADM and its current and future shareholders, is far more 
beneficial than continuing to litigate for an uncertain 
outcome and the possibility of no recovery for ADM. 
Plaintiffs’ Counsel believe that the proposed Settlement is 
fair, reasonable, and adequate and should be approved, 
along with plaintiffs’ counsel’s request for an award of 
attorneys’ fees and expenses. 
We jointly declare under penalty of perjury that the 
foregoing is true and correct and that this Declaration is 
executed this 30 day of June, 1997.

AFFIDAVIT OF JOHN C. COFFEE, JR. 
John C. Coffee, Jr., being duly sworn, deposes and 
says: 
1. I am the Adolf A. Berle Professor of Law at Colum- 
bia Univerity Law School and have taught corporate and 
securities law for over twenty years (at Columbia and at 
the law schools of Stanford University, the University of 
Michigan, the University of Virginia and Georgetown 
University). 
Prior to that, I was a practicing corporate 
lawyer with the firm of Cravath, Swaine & Moore in New 
York City from 1970 to 1976. 
2. As a law professor, one of my principal areas of 
specialization has been the field of corporate governance. 
I am a member both of the Legal Advisory Committee to 
the Board of Directors of the New York Stock Exchange, 
Inc. and of the Legal Advisory Board of the National 
Association of Securities Dealers (“NASD”). 
I served 
(for over a dozen years) as a Reporter to the American 
Law Institute for its restatement on corporate fiduciary 
duties: 
See A.L.I., PRINCIPLES OF CORPORATE 
GOVERNANCE: 
Analysis and Recommedations (1992) 
(hereinafter, “ALI Principles”). 
I am also a co-author 
of Choper, Coffee and Gilson CASES AND MATE- 
RIALS ON CORPORATIONS (3rd ed. 1995); Jennings, 
Marsh & Coffee, CASES AND MATERIALS ON SE- 
CURITIES REGULATION (4th ed. 1992), and Klein 
225 
and Coffee, BUSINESS ORGANIZATION AND FI- 
NANCE: Legal and Economic Principles (Sth ed. 
1993), all widely used law school casebooks or textbooks 
that cover the subject matter of corporate governance. 
Finally, I have also recently served as a member of the 
SEC’s Advisory Committee on the Capital Formation and 
Regulatory Processes, am a member of the Executive Com- 
mittee of the Securities Regulation Institute, and write a 
regular column on corporate and securities law for both 
the New York Law Jouranl and the Natonal Law Jour- 
nal. A fuller listing of my activities and writing is set 
forth on my resume, which is attached hereto as Exhibit 
A. 
3. Another area in which I have specialized and 
which is applicable to this case is that of “white collar 
crime”, with a special focus on organizational 
misconduct. 
In that regard, I have served as a consultant to the United 
States Sentencing Commission in connection with its for- 
mulation of sentencing guidelines for organizational of- 
fenders and served as a Reporter to the American Bar 
Association for its Standards on Sentencing Alternatives 
and Procedures (which standards covered organizational 
sentencing). 
4. In this action, I have been retained by the plain- 
tiffs’ attorneys to appraise the adequacy and significance 
of the corporate governance provisions set forth in Ex- 
pr Raye ga sag ® anmry 
dated May 29, 
1997 
(hereinafter, the “Corporate Governance Provi- 
sions”). 
In considering these provisions, I have com- 
pared them with (1) the standards set forth in the ALI 
Principles; (2) the Corporate Directors’ Handbook (the 
“Handbook”) prepared by the Business Law Section of 
the American Bar Association;! (3) certain statements 
of the Council of Institutional Investors, including its 
Shareholder Bill of Rights and its definition of “independ- 
1 See Section on Business Law, Corporate Directors’ Handbook, 
49 Bus. Law. 1243 (1994). 

226 
ent director;”? (4) certain proposed guidelines drafted 
by Calpers, the nation’s largest public pension fund,* 
(5) the guidelines of the National Association of Corpo- 
rate Directors;* and 
(6) the 1994 General Motors 
Guidelines on Significant Corporate Governance Issues 
(hereinafter, the “GM Guidelines”).* 
Both the Hand- 
book and the GM Guidelines have been described as 
“very helpful” by the Chief Justice of the Delaware Su- 
preme Court in determining director independence and 
“aspirational corporate 
practices,” and he has recom- 
mended that they “should be consulted by boards and 
their advisers.” ® 
5. In general, I believe the Corporate Governance 
Provisions represent more or less the “state of the art” in 
American corporate governance today. This is not to say 
that every conceivable reform provision has been adopted, 
but virtually every reform about whose desirability there 
today exists a significant consensus among commentators 
and institutional investors has been incorporated. In addi- 
tion, beyond these corporate governance provisions, there 
are special legal compliance provisions that I believe are 
of special value to a corporation with Archer Daniels 
Midland’s (“ADM”) history. 
In this affidavit, I under- 
take to review these separate provisions, first individually 
and then in terms of their aggregate impact. 
2 This statement is attached hereto 
as Exhibit B. 
3 See Adam Bryant, “Calpers Draws a Blueprint for Its Concept 
of An Ideal Board”, New York Times, June 17, 1997 at D-1. 
4See John A. Byrne, “The National Association of Corporate 
Directors’ new guidelines won't tolerate inattentive, passive unin- 
formed board members,” Business Week, November 25, 1996 at 
p. 100. 
5 See General Motors Guidelines, reprinted in Corporate Govern- 
ance Advisor, May-June, 1994, at 3-4. 
6See E. Norman Veasey, The Defining Tension in Corporate 
Governance in America, 52 Bus. Law. 398, 399 (1997). 
governance. 
lines. Its definition appears to be modeled after the Coun- 
cil of Institutional Investors’ “Definition of an Independ- 
ent Director” (attached hereto as Exhibit B). I believe 
this definition is particularly meaningful and relevant to 
ADM'’s case because it will effectively preclude the count- 
ing of business consultants or advisers to ADM as outside 
directors. 
7 See “Cash Compensation Drops for Outside Directors, annual 
Korn/Ferry Study Reveals,” Business Wire, May 7, 1997. 

228 
8. The Corporate Governance Provisions also effec- 
tively endorse the “lead director” concept. 
This idea 
emanates from an important article by Jay Lorsch and 
Martin Lipton,’ which recommended 
the appointment 
of a “lead director” to oversee the functioning of the 
board. 
Effectively, the existence of formally designated 
lead director creates a safety valve or check within the 
board on the influence of a dominating CEO, and it does 
so without requiring the automatic separation of the CEO 
and Chairman positions (which some feel can compro- 
mise the authority of the CEO and result in inefficiency). 
The GM Guidelines effectively endorsed 
this principle. 
Here, the “lead director” will in effect be either the Chair- 
man (if the Chairman is an outside director) or the Vice- 
Chairman (if not) (See Section IV (“Leadership of the 
Board”). 
9. While highly meritorious in my judgment, the “lead 
director” concept remains controversial. 
A 1996 survey 
of board practices finds that 79% of respondents opposed 
designating a “lead director” for their board.* 
In that 
light, the Corporate Governance Provisions take an im- 
portant, but still novel, step toward establishing a board 
capable of withstanding managerial pressures. 
The Cor- 
porate Governance Provisions cannot thus be criticized 
for simply adopting a widely prevalent model for cor- 
porate governance; rather they impose on the board con- 
trols and procedures that are resisted by most corporate 
executives today and would not be likely to have been 
adopted, absent the current litigation. 
10. Another important innovation is the Governance 
Committee (See “Section IV Governance Committee”). 
7 See Martin Lipton & Jay W. Lorsch, A Modest Proposed { 
sic) 
for Improved Corporate Governance 
48 Bus. Law. 59 (1992); see 
also, Jay W. Lorsch & Martin Lipton, On the Leading Edge: The 
Lead Director Concept, Harv. Bus. Rev., Jan.-Feb., 1993 at 79. 
§ See the IRCC Monitor: Board Practices Survey, Corporate Gov- 
ernance Advisor, May/June 1996 at 25-26. 
229 
The critical idea underlying this committee is the need 
for an at least annual review of the board’s performance 
and that of the Chief Executive Officer (in the latter 
case the review is to be by all nonmanagement 
directors). 
Again this requirement is consistent with the philosophy 
and approach of the GM Guidelines, and it is to be 
backstopped and enforced by annual disclosures about 
“the performance criteria and evaluation process in the 
Company's proxy statement.” 
Because the idea of an 
annual board review of the CEO's performance has been 
widely resisted by corporate executives,’ 
its adoption 
seems unlikely, but for this litigation. 
11. Other meaningful changes introduced by the Cor- 
porate Governance Provisions include the adoption of 
confidential voting and the addition of new outside direc- 
tors to both the Audit and Nominating Committees. As 
a result of these changes, no member of management will 
continue on the Nominating Committee under the Cor- 
porate Governance Provisions. 
12. Collectively, the combination of the lead director 
provision, the annual board review, and the requirements 
that (a) the Audit Committee be composed “solely of out- 
side directors” for the next three years (see “Section 
V. 
Independent Review Responsibilities of the Audit Com- 
mittee”) and (b) the Nominating Committee for the 1997 
board election be composed “of outside directors who 
were first elected to the Board of Directors after June 25, 
1996,” represent in my judgment significant steps to facil- 
itate board independence and to end the apparent man- 
agerial domination 
of the ADM board. Although 
it is 
never possible to predict that these changes will of neces- 
sity be succesful, there is frankly little more than [sic] can 
be done from a corporate governance perspective in terms 
of board structure 
or procedures. 
¥ In the same IRRC survey discussed above, 62.9% of respondents 
disfavored any policy whereby outside directors would hold periodic 
meetings without the presence of the CEO. /d. at 26-26. 

230 
Il. 
Law Compliance 
13. ADM 
is incorporated in Delaware, and under 
Delaware corporate law the ADM board of directors owes 
a duty of care to ADM under which it must supervise 
and monitor the corporation’s performance, including for 
possible violations of law. 
See, In re Caremark Interna- 
tional Inc. Derivative Litigation, 1996 Del. Ch. LEXIS 
125 (Del. Ch. Sept. 25, 1996). 
14. The Corporate Governance Provisions take impor- 
tant steps in addressing and rectifying any legal compli- 
ance deficiencies by expressly assigning to ADM’s Audit 
Committee new oversight responsibilities for law com- 
pliance, including issues of antitrust, discrimination, for- 
eign corrupt 
practices 
act enforcement, environmental 
conduct and federal securities laws requirements. 
This 
assignment is not simply a general precatory instruction, 
but rather an order (a) to determine whether ADM is 
engaged in anti-competitive activities and (b) to promul- 
gate policies and procedures regarding 
(i) compliance 
with the law, (ii) ADM’s procedures for educating em- 
ployees about compliance with the law; and (iii) ADM’s 
procedures for circulating these policies. The Audit Com- 
mittee is further charged with authority to ensure adher- 
ence to these policies and procedures. Additional specific 
instructions require: 
(1) the retention of outside independent counsel (for 
at least a three year period) to advise and assist the 
Audit Committee; 
(2) the appointment to the Audit Committee of only 
outside directors, and, for three years, only outside direc- 
tors who were first elected to the board after June 25, 
1995. 
15. 
It is important to recognize that, to the extent 
these requirements introduce new compliance policies and 
procedures by newly appointed Committee members and 
231 
advisers, they will help guard against the significant finan- 
cial liability that could result from having corporate offi- 
cers violate the federal criminal and civil laws in the 
future."° Thus, there is a corporate benefit from the in- 
stallation of these new oversight responsibilities. 
CONCLUSION 
16. Ultimately, it is impossible to predict that ADM 
will not violate the law again. No system of safeguards 
is foolproof; nor can every contingency be anticipated. 
But the Corporate Governance Provisions do that which 
can feasibly be done: establish a framework for effective 
monitoring and facilitate the evolution (which will not 
necessarily occur overnight) of an active and independent 
board. Such therapeutics have value (as the United States 
Sentencing Commission has independently determined). 
Indeed, so long as the board structure contemplated by 
the Corporate Governance Provisions is not modified or 
abridged, I believe that it is a prudent prediction that 
ADM will soon have an effective and independent board 
that will engage in active monitoring. 
/s/ John C. Coffee, Jr. 
JOHN C. CoFFEE, Jr.
