COUNCIL OF INSTITUTIONAL INVESTORS 
DEFINITION OF INDEPENDENT DIRECTOR 
An independent director is someone whose only non- 
trivial connection to the corporation is that person’s di- 
rectorship. 
A director will not generally be considered independent 
if he or she: 
(a) has been employed by the corporation or an affiliate 
in an executive capacity; 
(b) is an employee or owner of a firm that is one of the 
corporation’s or its affiliate’s paid advisers or con- 
sultants; 
(c) is employed by a significant customer or supplier; 
(d) has a personal services contract with the corporation 
or one of its affiliates; 
(e) is employed by a foundation or university that re- 
ceives significant grants or endowments from the 
corporation or one of its affiliates; 
(f) is a relative of an executive of the corporation or one 
of its affiliates; 
(g) is part of an interlocking directorate in which the 
CEO or other executive officer of the corporation 
serves on the board of another corporation that em- 
ploys 
the director. 
241 
EXPLANATORY NOTES 
An independent director is someone whose only non- 
trivial connection to the corporation is that person’s di- 
rectorship. 
NOTES: Independent directors do not invariably 
share a single set of qualities that are not shared by 
cil of Institutional Investors believe that the prom- 
ulgation of a narrowly drawn definition of an inde- 
pendent director (coupled with a policy specifying 
standard), is in the corporation's 
and all sharehold- 
ers’ on-going financial interest because: 
—a genuine quality of independence is critical to a 
properly functioning board, 
—certain clearly definable relationships pose a suffi- 
cient threat to a director’s unqualified independence 
in a sufficient number of cases that they warrant ad- 
vance identification, 
—the effect created by a conflict of interest on an 
individual director is almost impossible to detect, 
either by shareholders or other board members, and, 
—while an across-the-board 
application of any defi- 
nition to a large number of people will inevitably 
miscategorize 
a few of them, this is sufficiently small 
that it is far outweighted by the significant benefits. 
Stated most simply, an independent director is a per- 
son whose directorship constitutes his or her only con- 
nection to the corporation. The definition approved by 
members of the Council contains this basic formulation. 
It then adds to it a list of the relationships members 
believe pose the greatest threat to a director’s independ- 
ence. The existence of any such relationship will remove 
a director from the independent 
category. Relationships 
not described in this list will not be considered in assess- 
ing independence.

the corporation’s 
or its affiliates paid advisers or 
consultants; 
The following notes are supplied to give added clarity 
and guidance in interpreting the specified relationship. 
(a) has been employed by the corporation or an affiliate 
in an executive capacity 
NOTES: 
The term “executive capacity” includes the 
chief executive, operating, financial, legal, and ac- 
counting officers of a company. 
This includes the 
president, treasurer, secretary, controller, and any 
vice-president who is in charge of a principal business 
unit, division, or function (such as sales, administra- 
tion, or finance) or performs a major policymaking 
function for the corporation. 
An “affiliate” relationship is established if one entity 
either alone or pursuant to an arrangement with one 
or more other persons, owns or has the power to 
vote more than 25 percent of the equity interest in 
another, unless some other person, either alone or 
pursuant to an arrangement with one or more other 
persons, owns or has the power to vote a greater per- 
centage of the equity interest. 
For these purposes, 
(c) 
NOTES: 
Advisors or consultants include, but are 
not limited 
to, law firms, accountants, 
insurance 
companies, and banks.

NOTES: A director shall be deemed to be employed 
by a significant customer or supplier if the director: 
— is employed by or has a 5 percent or greater own- 
ership interest 
in a supplier or customer where the 
sales to or by the corporation represent more than 
1% of the sales of the customer or supplier or for 
more than 1% of the sales of the corporation, 
—is employed by or has a 5 percent or greater own- 
ership interest in one of the corporation’s debtors or 
creditors where the amount owed exceeds 1% of the 
corporation’s or the third party’s assets. 
Ownership means beneficial 
or record ownership, 
not 
custodial ownership. 
(d) has a personal services contract with the corporation 
equal joint venture partners meet the definition of 
an affiliate, and officers and employees of equal joint 
venture enterprises are considered affiliated. 
Affiliates include predecessor companies. A “prede- 
cessor” of the corporation is a corporation that within 
the last ten years represented more than 80 percent 
of the corporation's sales or assets when such prede- 
cessor became part of the corporation. Recent mer- 
ger partners are also considered predecessors. A re- 
cent merger partner is a corporation that directly or 
indirectly became part of the corporation or a prede- 
cessor within the last ten years and represented more 
than 50 percent of the corporation’s or predecessor’s 
sales or assets at the time of the merger. 
A subsidiary is an affiliate if it is at least 80% owned 
by the corporation and accounts for 25% of the cor- 
poration’s consolidated sales or assets.

DECLARATION OF R. ALAN MILLER 
I, R. Alan Miller, declare 
as follows: 
Qualifications 
1. I am President of Philadelphia Investment Banking 
Company. My resume is attached as Exhibit A hereto 
to describe my educational background and experience. 
2. Philadelphia 
Investment 
Banking 
Company 
(“PIBC”) provides a wide range of corporate finance 
services to clients. These services include raising capital 
through private placements of debt and equity financings; 
financial analysis and advisory services re: public offer- 
ings of securities; merger, acquisition and divestiture serv- 
ices; evaluation and economic analysis services; consult- 
ing and litigation support; and other financial services. 
3. PIBC and its principals perform a considerable 
amount of work in the areas of evaluation of businesses 
and securities and the factors involved in such evaluation. 
These are done on a formal basis for a variety of pur- 
poses, as well as continuously on an informal basis in the 
process of performing our corporate finance functions 
such as raising capital, assisting in mergers and acquisi- 
tions, etc. The factors used in such evaluations and their 
importance and materiality to the investment community 
are areas in which I have developed expertise and had 
substantial experience over the last twenty-four years. 
Additionally, we have prepared a substantial number of 
analyses of market impact and damages in connection 
with numerous shareholder litigations. 

246 
4. Also included in Exhibit A is a list of the 23 cases 
in which I have testified at trial. Many of these matters 
have involved determinations of values of companies 
and/or securities based on actual situations and/or hypo- 
thetical assumptions, as well as the determination of ma- 
teriality. More specifically, among the cases in which I 
have testified are the following: 
A. In U.S. Banknote Securities Litigation, 1 testi- 
fied as an expert witness on behalf of the plaintiff 
class (U.S.D.C., S. District of NY, J. Cedarbaum). 
The jury returned a verdict for plaintiffs and found 
substantial damages; defendants’ 
expert had said 
there were none. 
B. In Geriatric and Medical Centers Securities Lit- 
igation, 1 testified as an expert on behalf of the 
plaintiff class (U.S.D.C., E. District of Pa., J. Ful- 
lam). The jury returned a verdict for plaintiffs and 
found substantial damages; defendants’ expert had 
said there were virtually none. 
C. In O & M Investment Partners v. Windon et al., 
I testified as an expert on behalf of the plaintiff 
(U.S.D.C., 
E. District of Pa., Judge Robert F. 
Kelly). The parties settled shortly thereafter; plain- 
tiffs received a substantial payment. 
D. In Pennsylvania Enterprises Securities Litigation, 
I testified as an expert on behalf of the plaintiff re- 
garding issues of materiality.

7. In my opinion, and based on my education, knowl- 
edge and experience, the value of the benefits to ADM 
from the settlement of the subject litigation is a minimum 
of $158 million, and is quite likely considerably larger. 
Evaluation of Terms of Settlement 
8. To my understanding, the benefits to ADM from 
the proposed resolution of the derivative litigation include 
the following: 
A. Cash settlement of $8,000,000. 
B. Adoption by the Board of Directors of the corpo- 
rate governance measures and procedures described 
in 
Exhibit B hereto, the purpose of which (from an economic 
viewpoint) is to significantly reduce or eliminate the likeli- 
hood of recurrence of the types of action which led to the 
subject litigation. 
C. Ending the very considerable expense, loss exposure 
and management distraction which would be caused if the 
litigation continued. 
9. Although $8,000,000 in cash is straightforward, the 
benefits of its use are likely to well exceed this amount. 
That is, at least 51% of the $8,000,000 cash portion of 
the settlement is to be used to fund the activities of the 
independent audit committee and its counsel for a period 
of at least three years. To the extent this activity reduces 
the likelihood of recurrence of the type of action which 
led to the litigation, it increases the value of these reform 
measures substantially, which described below, is worth 
many multiples of the $8,000,000 cash amount. 

250 
10. The benefits of avoiding the costs referred to in 
paragraph 8B above can be addressed in two ways: 
A. avoidance of such costs in the future, and qualifica- 
tion thereof, and 
B. the amount, by observation of the effect on the 
market value of the Company, of the positive impact of 
such avoidance on the Company. 
11. Attached as Exhibit C hereto is a copy of the 
“litigation” section of ADM’s 1996 Form 10-K Report, 
which summarizes the outstanding litigation at the date 
thereof. As a reader can see, the list is long and substan- 
tial. I have seen no public discussion of estimated costs 
or exposure as a result of these matters. 
However, I 
believe at least $190 million has been publicly identified 
so far as amounts paid in fines and settlement payments; 
that amount does not include legal fees. 
It appears that 
$250 million may be a very conservative estimate of the 
direct costs of management's improper actions. 
To that 
must be added legal expenses, and the substantial distrac- 
tion of management attention caused by defending and 
dealing with these matters. 
It appears that a minimum of 
$300 million would be the minimum cost of this item. 
12. A. To derive an economic value from avoiding a 
recurrence of these actions and their costs, a percentage 
probability must be assigned to the likelihood of their 
recurrence, with and without the board reforms. This is a 
commonly accepted economic analysis technique known 
as expected value analysis, or as rational expectations 
analysis.” 
1 This technique is a basic economic tool often used to measure 
the value of events with multiple possible outcomes. 
To apply it, 
one must determine the cost of an event if it does occur, then 
assign a (percentage) 
factor to indicate its likelihood of occur- 
rence. 
If (as in this case) there are two different likelihoods of 
occurrence given two different 
sets of assumptions 
(before and 
after implementation of the Corporate Governance Provisions), one 
can measure the difference between them as the cost of the event 
251 
Considering the numerous areas of business and number 
of product lines in which they occurred, the senior level 
of the management 
personnel involved (and their 
ties to 
those who dominated the board) and the degree of plan- 
ning and execution necessary to implement the actions, it 
is difficult to believe the actions would not likely continue 
absent the Board of Directors reforms. With 
the reforms 
in place and funded to ensure the independence of over- 
seers, recurrence seems highly unlikely and difficult to 
achieve. A fair estimate of the difference between likeli- 
hood of recurrence 
and not, is 50% in my opinion; 
that 
is 70 to 80% chance of recurrence without reforms versus 
20 to 30% with reforms. An observer 
can make his own 
judgment as to percentage likelihood, but 20 to 30% 
seems very conservative (high). To quantify the economic 
value, then, using the expected value methodology, multi- 
ply the cost of recurrence ($300 million minimum) times 
the differential in likelihood due to reforms (50% ) for a 
result of $150 million minimum. 
B. In determining the proper percentage likelihood of 
recurrence of the wrongdoing, I note the following from 
Professor Coffee’s Affidavit: 
In general, I believe the Corporate Governance Pro- 
visions represent more or less the “state of the art” 
in American corporate governance today... 
. In 
addition, beyond these corporate governance provi- 
sions, there are special legal compliance provisions 
it if does occur, multiplied times the difference between the likeli- 
hood factors. This expected value analysis technique is taught in 
basic economic courses. It is used constantly by participants in the 
financial markets, both explicitly and implicitly, as a regular part 
of quantifying the likely effect on companies or entities of various 
possible future events, and as a means of reducing risks of various 
events occurring to dollar values. I have used this technique as the 
basis for determining alternative values as a basis for damages in 
numerous litigation matters, including in testimony which became 
the basis for awards by fact finders. 

252 
that I believe are of special value to a corporation 
with Archer Daniels Midland’s (“ADM”) 
history. 
More importantly, the definition of “outside director” 
in the Corporate Governance Provisions is tighter 
and more restrictive than those set forth in most of 
the well-known contemporary corporate governance 
guidelines. 
In addition, he supports the creation of the “lead director” 
role and its value to the enterprise. Further: 
Collectively, the combination of the lead director pro- 
vision, the annual board review, and the requirements 
that (a) the Audit Committee be composed “solely 
of outside directors” for the next three years .. 
. 
represent in my judgment significant steps to facili- 
tate board independence 
and to end the apparent 
managerial domination of the ADM board. Although 
it is never possible to predict that these changes will 
of necessity be successful, there is frankly little more 
that can be done from a corporate governance per- 
spective in terms of board structure or procedures. 
C. Adding to the benefits of the preventive actions and 
enhancing their value is the speed with which the improve- 
ments in corporate governance 
are being implemented. 
The provisions of Exhibit A to the Stipulation of Settle- 
ment are to be implemented within 60 days. A review of 
literature suggests this is considerably faster and further 
reaching than many corporate governance improvements.

ii. Enclosed as Exhibit D is a copy of an article which 
appeared in The Journal of Finance, Vol. LI, No. 1, 
March 1996, entitled “Shareholder Activism by Institu- 
tional Investors: Evidence from CalPERS” by Michael P. 
Smith. I believe the article is instructive in that it meas- 
ures shareholder wealth effects of improved corporate gov- 
ernance structures and procedures, and concludes that 
“Shareholder wealth increases for firms that adopt [im- 
proved corporate governance] or settle and decreases for 
firms that resist” (from the Abstract). 
The article con- 
cludes “Overall, the evidence indicates that shareholder 
activism 
is largely successful 
in changing governance 
structure and, when successful, results in a statistically 
significant increase in shareholder wealth.” 
iii. Enclosed as Exhibit E are copies of four articles 
which describe in considerable detail the benefits of im- 
proved corporate governance and the actions of various 
organizations of investors, and of academics to study and 
promote such improvements, as well as significant informa- 
tion allowing a comparison of the ADM settlement pro- 
visions with actual practices at leading companies and 
generally acknowledged “best practices”. The articles cite 
the following notable studies and reports: 
* The General Motors study and report, which has 
become a blueprint for numerous other companies’ 
policies. 
* The Calpers study and review process which graded 
boards on various measures of effective, independ- 
ent governance structures and proocesses. 
* A study of the Investor Responsibility Research 
Centre of guidelines developed by boards at S&P 
500 companies.

A review of these articles confirms that the ADM provi- 
sions to be enacted will move it from among the worst 
(as specifically cited many times), to the head of the best 
current practices group. 
Items which are “suggested” or 
“as deemed appropriate” by other guidelines are required 
in the ADM provisions. Structures are set in writing, and 
the independent audit committee—charged with monitor- 
ing, oversieht and training and funded independently — 
appears unique based on this review. 
These observations strongly support my opinions as to 
the relative percentage likelihood of recurrence of the vio-

immediately following

of Justice was investigating alleged violations of federal 
antitrust laws? by Archer Daniels Midland Company, a 
Delaware corporation (“ADM”).

tiffs alleged (within their original as well as subsequent

of which constitute the nucleus of the Stipulation of Settle-

May 30, 1997 (d/e 60). 
The proposed settlement provides that the Defendants > 
would be “fully, finally and forever released, relinquished 
and discharged [from] all Settled Claims.”*® 
(d/e 60,

if any, against one Defendant,

gence, recklessness, violations of federal or state securities laws,

certain 
corporate

pensation, 
subject to approval by the District Court.

The remaining funds* will be utilized in support of the

3. Corporate Governance. 
If approved, the settlement includes changes in cor- 
porate governance as discussed within the agreed motion 
filed herein on May 30, 1997 (d/e 60). 
First, if the Chairman of the Board is not an “outside 
director” (the definition of which is discussed later herein), 
then the outside directors will elect an outside director 
to act as Vice-Chairman to preside over meetings of non- 
management directors and of the Governance Committee. 
Second, the Audit Committee, comprised solely of outside 
directors, will be charged with the task of overseeing 
issues of legal compliance with state or federal laws relat- 
ing to antitrust, 
discrimination, 
environmental, 
securi- 
ties and disclosure, and foreign corrupt practices act en- 
forcement. The Audit Committee also will be charged to 
ensure 
that reporting systems provide the Board with 
adequate and accurate information. 
The Audit Commit- 
tee will receive regular reports from senior management 
and likewise will report regularly to the Board as a whole. 
Outside independent legal counsel will be retained (and 
selected) by the Audit Committee. 
Third, the Governance Committee will establish and 
approve performance criteria for evaluation of the Chief 
Executive Officer, assess effectiveness of the Board (and 
committees), 
and 
the 
Governance 
Committee's _ per- 
formance criteria and evaluation process will be disclosed 
in proxy statements 
for the annual meeting of share- 
holders. 
§ Certain of the funds may be utilized to pay costs and expenses 
necessarily incurred in providing notices or information to share- 
holders by ADM. 

262 
Fourth, total compensation to be paid to non-employee- 
directors (for a period of three (3) years) will not ex- 
ceed $100,000 per annum, and one-half of this compen- 
sation will be paid in ADM stock. 
Fifth, to the extent 
permissible, voting by shareholders will be confidential. 
Sixth, the Compensation Committee and Nominating 
Committee, as well as the Audit Committee, will be com- 
prised exclusively of outside directors. 
Seventh, a majority of the Board will be comprised 
of outside directors, and the number of direct manage- 
ment representation on the Board is limited to two indi- 
viduals plus the Chief Executive Officer. 
Furthermore, 
the term “outside director”’® was defined to mean 
a 
director who 
(a) 
is neither 
a current employee 
nor 
former member of senior management of ADM or an affil- 
iate; (b) does not have a business or professional rela- 
tionship which is material to ADM or the director; (c) 
does not have a close familial relationship with any mem- 
ber of senior management of ADM; (d) is not an officer 
of a company of which ADM’s Chairman or CEO is also 
a board member; (e) is not personally receiving compen- 
sation from ADM in any capacity other than as a director; 
and (f) does not personally receive, nor is he an em- 
ployee of a foundation, university or foundation which 
receives, grants or endowments from ADM which are 
material to ADM or the recipient.

(d) Pauly, Charles W. and Elizabeth D.—objecting

ARGUMENT 
A. 
THE STANDARDS APPLICABLE TO THE DIS- 
TRICT COURT'S REVIEW. 
The present litigation is a shareholders derivative ac- 
tion, purportedly brought on behalf of ADM, pursuant 
to Fed. R. Civ. P. 23.1. The motion presently before the 
District Court is brought because of the directive of Fed. 
R. Civ. P. 23.1 that 
{t]he action shall not be dismissed or compromised 
without the approval of the court, and notice of the 
proposed dismissal or compromise shall be given to 
shareholders or members in such manner as the court 
directs. 
While the proponents of the settlement must demon- 
strate that the settlement is fair, adequate and reasonable 
(United Founders Life Insurance Co. v. Consumers Na- 
tional Life Insurance Co., 447 F.2d 647 (7th Cir. 1971); 
Susquehanna Corp. v. Korholz, 84 F.R.D. 316 (N.D. Til. 
1979) 
), settlement of shareholders derivative actions are 
favored and a presumption in favor of approving settle- 
ments exists. Donovan v. Robbins, 752 F.2d 1170 (7th 
Cir. 1985). Whether the proposed settlement is fair rests 
in the sound discretion of the District Court. See Susque- 
hanna Corp., supra. The District Court should not, how- 
ever, decide the merits of the action and should not substi- 
tute its judgment for the business judgment of the parties 
who reached the accord. See /d. 
In its evaluation of the proposed settlement, the Dis- 
trict must 
weigh the probabilities and possibilities of victory 
or defeat as indicated by the legal or factual situa- 
tion presented and determine whether the compro- 
mise, taken as a whole, is in the best interests of the 
corporation and its shareholders. 
265 
United Founders Life Insurance Co., 447 F.2d at 655, 
quoting Winkelman v. General Motors Corp., 48 F.Supp. 
490, 493 (S.D.N.Y. 
1942). 
The District Court also 
should consider the following factors: 
(i) the strength 
of the plaintiffs’ case on the merits; (ii) the complexity, 
length and expense of continued litigation; (iii) the de- 
gree of opposition to the settlement; (iv) the presence of 
collusion in gaining a settlement; 
(v) the opinion of 
competent counsel as to the reasonableness of the settle- 
ment; and (vi) the stage of the proceedings and the 
amount of discovery completed. 
Donovan 
vy. Fitzsim- 
mons, 
778 F.2d 298 
(7th Cir. 
1985); Susquehanna 
Corp. v. Korholz, 84 F.R.D. 316 (N.D. Ill. 1979). As 
the Susquehanna Corp. court observed, 
[w]hen, in a derivative action, a settlement 
proposal 
is arrived at after arms-length negotiations ae Seda 
conducted, 
it is clear that sufficient discovery has 
been taken to allow the court and the parties to act 
intelligently, the counsel involved are competent and 
experienced, and the numbers of objectors, or the 
interest they represent, is not large, it may properly 
be presumed that the agreement is fair and adequate. 
84 F.R.D. at 321. 
The District Court is not to use the settlement hearing 
as a trial or a rehearsal of a trial on the merits of the 
litigation. 
Levin v. Mississippi River Corp., 59 F.R.D 
353 (S.D.N.Y. 1973), citing Newman vy. Stein, 464 F 2d 
689 (2nd Cir.), cert. denied 409 U.S. 
1039 (1972): 
Glicken v. Bradford, 35 F.R.D. 144 (S.D.N.Y. 1964). 
Indeed, an evidentiary hearing, while certainly permitted, 
is not required. 
Rather, it is sufficient for the District 
Court to accept memoranda in support of the settlement 
= bo 
Ah aoe A there is an adequate record for 
ing 
set 
nt. Patterson v. 
108 Chater 1s7e) 
Stovall, 528 F.2d 
The present litigation is controlled 
the law 
of t 
State of Delaware, because ADM is Be Rarer in Dele 

266 
ware, and the law of the state of incorporation is con- 
trolling with respect to the fiduciary duties owed by direc- 
tors to the corporation. See Treco, Inc. v. Land of Lin- 
coln Savings & Loan, 749 F.2d 374 (7th Cir. 1984). 
In Delaware, the business and affairs of a corporation 
are managed by or under the direction of the Board of 
Directors, and such directors are charged with fiduciary 
obligations, including the duty of loyalty and the duty 
of care. Cede & Co. v. Technicolor, Inc., 634 A.2d 345 
(Del. 1993); 8 Del. C. § 141(a). 
While directors are 
charged with fiduciary obligations, such directors fulfill 
those duties when they exercise business judgment, in 
good faith, in making decisions concerning the corporate 
entity. When a director acts in good faith, the director 
enjoys a presumption of sound business judgment that 
a court will not disturb so long as a rational business 
purpose may be attributed to the director’s decision. 
In the absence of fraud, bad faith, gross overreach- 
ing or abuse of discretion, courts will not interfere 
with the exercise of business judgment by corporate 
directors. 
Panter v. Marshall Field & Co., 646 F.2d 271, 293 (7th 
Cir. 1981) (applying Delaware law); Cf. Cede & Co., 
634 A.2d 345, supra. 
In the present litigation, the De- 
fendants are also shielded by Article XIV of the ADM 
Certificate of Incorporation, permitted by Delaware law, 
and which includes a provision: 
A director of the Corporation shall have no personal 
liability to the corporation or its stockholders for 
monetary damages for breach of his fiduciary duty 
as a director, provided however this Article shall 
not eliminate or limit the liability of a director (1) 
for any breach of director’s duty of loyalty to the 
Corporation or its stockholders; (2) for acts or omis- 
sions not in good faith or which involve intentional 
misconduct or a knowing violation of law; (3) for 
the unlawful payment of dividends or unlawful stock 
267 
repurchases under § 174 of the General Corporation 
Law of the State of Delaware; or (4) for any trans- 
action from which the director derived an improper 
personal benefit. This Article shall not eliminate or 
limit the liability of a director for any act or omis- 
sion occurring prior to the effective date of this 
Article. 
Thus, the Certificate of Incorporation of ADM provides 
a further hurdle for the Plaintiffs to overcome in any 
successful litigation against the Defendants for breach of 
fiduciary obligations. 
B. 
THE PRESENT LITIGATION IS SUBJECT TO 
INVOLUNTARY 
DISMISSAL 
UNDER 
DELA- 
WARE LAW. 
Shareholder derivative litigation brings to the court a 
cause of action which properly belongs to the corporation 
(Rales v. Blashand, 634 A.2d 927 (Del. 1993)), and 
because the litigation is brought purportedly on behalf 
of the corporation, any damages recovered are paid to the 
corporation. 
See Sternberg 
v. O'Neil, 550 A.2d 
1105 
(Del. 
1988); Zapata Corporation 
v. Maldonado, 430 
A.2d 779 (Del. 1981). 
Furthermore, the directors. not 
the shareholders, manage the business and affairs of the 
corporation. 
Aronson 
v. Lewis, 473 A.2d 805 
(Del. 
1984). 
Arising from the principle. 
that derivative 
litigation 
belongs to the corporation, is the requirement that the 
shareholders make demand upon the Board prior to filing 
shareholder litigation. See Spiegel v. Buntrock, 571 A.2d 
767 (Del. 1990). This requirement of a pre-suit demand 
is designed 
to assure that the stockholder affords the corpora- 
tion the opportunity to address an alleged wrong 
without litigation, to decide whether to invest the 
resources of the corporation in litigation, and to 
control any litigation which does occur. 

268 
Id, 571 A.2d at 773. 
In Aronson v. Lewis, 473 A.2d, 
805 (Del. 1984), the Supreme Court of Delaware stated 
By its very nature the derivative action impinges on 
the managerial freedom of directors. 
Hence, the 
demand requirement. 
. 
. exists at the threshold, first 
to insure that a stockholder exhausts is [sic] intracor- 
porate remedies, and then to provide a safeguard 
against strike suits. Thus, by promoting this form 
of alternate dispute resolution, rather than immediate 
recourse to litigation, the demand requirement is a 
recognition of the fundamental precept that directors 
manage the business and affairs of corporations. 
473 A.2d at 811-12 (footnote omitted ). 
The requirement of making a pre-suit demand is strictly 
enforced by the courts. Where a putative derivative plain- 
tiff seeks to institute an action on a corporation’s behalf 
without demand on the Board, the plaintiff must allege, 
with particularity, specific facts demonstrating why such 
demand should be excused. 
See Fed. R. Civ. 23.1; Del. 
Ch. Rule 23.1: Stepak v. Dean, 434 A.2d 388, 390 (Del. 
Ch. 1981). 
The failure to make a demand or to plead 
legally sufficient facts demonstrating that demand should 
be excused mandates dismissal of the complaint. 
Po- 
gostin v. Rice, 480 A.2d 619 (Del. 1984); Aronson v. 
Lewis, 473 A.2d 805 (Del. 1984). 
In the present litigation, the Plaintiffs did not make a 
pre-suit demand, and it is the position of ADM that the 
Plaintiffs failed to meet their heavy burden in pleading 
specific facts which would demonstrate that a majority of 
the Board was either (1) so self-interested in the chal- 
lenged conduct or (2) so dominated and controlled by 
the alleged corporate wrongdoer(s) to reverse the custo- 
mary presumption of independence. See Levine v. Smith, 
$91 A.2d 194 (Del. 1991); Pogostin v. Rice, 480 A.2d 
619 (Del. 1984). 
As is set forth within ADM’s motion to dismiss the 
complaint (d/e 29) and as is explained within ADM’s 
269 
memorandum in support of that motion (d/e 31), the 
Plaintiffs face a substantial burden in attempting to over- 
come ADM’s motion to dismiss the litigation in its en- 
tirety for failure to make the requisite demand upon the 
Board of Directors, and the Plaintiffs’ failure to plead 
particularized 
facts sufficient to excuse this demand. Fur- 
thermore, the Certificate of Incorporation 
of ADM in- 
cludes a substantial shield for the Defendants from lia- 
bility 
for monetary 
damages 
allegedly 
arising 
from 
breaches of the duty of care. Thus, the present litigation 
is similar to shareholder derivative actions in general as 
being “notoriously difficult and unpredictable.” Schimmel 
v. Goldman, 57 F.R.D. 481, 487 (S.D.N.Y. 1973); Cf. 
Zimmerman 
vy. Bell, 800 F.2d 386 (4th Cir. 1986); 
Maher v. Zapata Corp. 714 F.2d 436 (Sth Cir. 1983); 
Republic National Life Insurance 
Co. 
v. Beasley, 
73 
F.R.D. 658 (S.D.N.Y. 1977).

C. THE PROPOSED SETTLEMENT 
IS IN THE 
BEST 
INTEREST 
OF ADM 
AND 
ITS 
SHAREHOLDERS. 
The proposed settlement presently before the District 
Court for approval involves various elements. That por- 
tion of the proposed settlement which involves payment 
of $8 Million by the Defendants, or on their behalf, and 
that aspect which provides that certain claims are re- 
served to ADM, are straightforward. Those elements of 
the proposed 
settlement 
involving 
issues of corporate 
governance are, in reality, the linchpin of the proposed 
Pp ge nr pre grey therapeutics” for the benefit 
, 
corpora 
may, 
themselves, su 
iva- 
tive settlement. 
Bell Atlantic Corp. v. sed :F34 
1034 (3rd Cir. 1993); Granada Investment, Inc. v. DWG 
Corp., 962 F.2d 1203 (6th Cir. 1992); Zimmerman v 
Bell, 800 F.2d 386 (4th Cir. 1986); Maher v. Zapata 
Corp. 714 F.2d 436 (Sth Cir. 1983); Levin v. Mississippi 
River Corp., 59 F.R.D. 353 (S.D.N.Y.), affirmed 486 
F.2d 1398 (2nd Cir.), cert. denied, 414 US. 
1112 

270 
(1973); and Cf. Mills v. Electric Auto-Lite Co., 396 
U.S. 375 (1970) (non-monetary recovery on merits does 
not preclude award of fees). The corporate therapeutics 
involved in the presently proposed settlement are set forth 
clearly within the Stipulation of Settlement (d/e 60), and 
need not be repeated here. 
Suffice to say that these 
corporate therapeutics include substantial changes in the 
manner in which issues are presented and resolved by the 
ADM Board. ADM presents in support of its position 
on this issue the Affidavit of Professor Michael P. Dooley, 
the William S. Potter Professor of Law at the University 
of Virginia. 
In Bell Atlantic Corp., the court assessed whether a 
settlement which mandated structural changes in the cor- 
porate governance of the corporation was a fair settlement 
of a shareholder derivative action. The court noted the 
difficulty 
in measuring the value 
in the non-monetary 
benefit, and an objector to the settlement charged that 
these corporate governance changes were duplicative of 
changes already made and that the change upon which 
the settlement was premised amounted to little more than 
a promise to obey the law. The United States Court of 
Appeals for the Third Circuit rejected these objections 
and held that the changes in corporate governance brought 
about by the settlement were broader and more inclusive 
than changes to which the company had agreed in an 
action with the State Attorney General, and the court 
noted that the agreement not only ensured that the cor- 
© 
poration would follow the law, but provided specific mech- 
anisms to ensure employees of the corporation and its 
subsidiaries behaved appropriately. See Bell Atlantic Cor- 
poration, 2 F.3d at 1312. The Bell Atlantic Corp. court 
also noted the improbability of the plaintiff's success on 
the merits and the fact that fewer than thirty (30) of the 
corporate entity’s 1.1 million shareholders objected to the 
proposed settlement. 
In Zimmerman, a shareholder derivative action arising 
out of the alleged mishandling of a takeover attempt, the 
271 
court considered a settlement which required the corpora- 
tion to adopt a detailed statement of procedures to be 
followed in the event of a future tender offer or takeover 
bid. 
Two out of “tens of thousands” of shareholders 
objected to the proposed settlement. 
The Zimmerman 
court noted 
Influencing the future conduct of management may 
serve the interests 
of the corporation 
as fully as a 
recovery for past misconduct, and a settlement may 
be accepted “even though no direct monetary bene- 
fits are paid by the defendants to the corporation.” 
Here, the Statement of Policy provides guidelines for 
future management responses to tender offers and 
takeover bids, the very situation that prompted these 
various complair.'s. 
800 F.2d at 390, quoting Maher v. Zapata Corp., 714 
F.2d 436, 466 (Sth Cir. 1983). The Zimmerman court 
noted that the majority of directors were outside directors, 
that the Board obtained outside advice from an invest- 
ment banker, and that settlement of shareholder deriva- 
tive actions are favored because of the complexity of the 
litigation, and the Court of Appeals held that the district 
court did not abuse its discretion in approving the settle- 
ment. 
In Granada Investments, a minority shareholder brought 
a derivative action against a corporation and its directors, 
which included controlling shareholders. 
The Granada 
Investments plaintiff alleged breaches of fiduciary duties 
and securities law violations stemming from claims of 
self-dealing on the part of controlling shareholders. 
The 
district court approved a settlement which called for the 
court to designate three new directors with no familial 
relationship to any party. The settlement called for these 
new directors to serve on the audit and compensation 
committees and created a special committee to review 
and approve incentive and bonus compensation, 
inter- 

272 
corporate transactions more than $500,000, as well as 
other transactions which could potentially involve self- 
dealing. The United States court [sic] of Appeals for the 
Sixth Circuit, in Granada Investment, noted: 
Admittedly, the fairness of a settlement is more diffi- 
cult to determine when non-pecuniary benefits are 
provided to the corporation. 
Nonetheless, [the cor- 
poration] was released from all liability based on 
claims that might have been asserted by [the plain- 
tiff]. In addition, the settlement called for changes 
in corporate governance. 
Against the background of 
a Posner-dominated board, the settlement provided 
for three new strategically placed directors and for 
a new special committee with oversight powers. 
962 F.2d at 1206; Cf. Levin, 59 F.R.D. at 367, fn. 38 
(“the fact that the form of the settlement decided upon 
is somewhat unusual in that it includes some benefits 
which cannot be evaluated in financial terms, does not 
militate against its acceptance; the parties are permitted 
great freedom in shaping the form of settlement considera- 
tion”). 
Finally, in In Re Caremark International, Inc. Deriva- 
tive Litigation, 1996 WL 549894 (Del. Ch.), 
the court 
approved settlement of derivative claims following indict- 
ment and a guilty plea by the corporate entity to federal 
charges stemming from its unlawful payment of medical 
referral fees. 
Prior to the settlement with the derivative 
plaintiffs, the corporation’s board had taken several steps 
aimed at increasing management supervision, modifying 
company policies concerning referral fees and strengthen- 
ing its internal audit plan. 
In settling the derivative 
claims, the company and its directors agreed to additional 
governance changes, but the director defendants paid no 
money to the company. The Caremark court stated 
In light of the fact that the Caremark board already 
has a functioning commitiee overseeing corporate 
compliance, the changes in corporate practice that 
273 
are presented as consideration for the settlement do 
not impress one as very significant. 
Nevertheless, 
that consideration appears fully adequate to support 
dismissal of the derivative claims of director fault as- 
serted, because those claims find no substantial evi- 
dentiary support in the record and quite likely were 
susceptible to a motion to dismiss in all events. 
1996 WL 549894 at page 12. 
In the present case, the corporate therapeutic benefits 
which are received are of a type which could not have 
been attained by the Plaintiffs had they been successful 
in the litigation. 
Furthermore, the monies paid by the 
Defendants, or on their behalf, constitute funds which 
most probably would not be recovered by the Plaintiffs 
should the litigation continue. Thus, the monetary bene- 
fits received, taken together with the corporate therapeutic 
benefits, constitute more than substantial consideration 
for the proposed settlement to be deemed fair, adequate, 
and reasonable. 
D. 
THE OBJECTIONS TO SETTLEMENT SHOULD 
BE OVERRULED. 
As set forth previously, the objections of Messrs. Berns, 
Chiara and Scott are directed toward the request for attor- 
ney’s fees which may be presented herein by the Plaintiffs. 
ADM takes no position as to these objections. The ob- 
jections of Mr. and Mrs. Pauly, and the objections of 
John Gilbert and Martin Glotzer, are directed more to the 
substance of the settlement, and ADM responds thereto. 
Mr. and Mrs. Pauly object because they assert that if 
the litigation brought by the Plaintiffs had merit, the set- 
tlement is insufficient in comparison to the monies which 
ADM might receive at the conclusion of the litigation, 
and that if the litigation is without merit, the Plaintiffs 
should receive nothing. 
Simply because the monetary 
amount proposed in a settlement is a fraction of what 

274 
could possibly be recovered does not render the proposed 
settlement inadequate or unfair. 
Susquehanna Corp. v. 
Korholz, 84 F.R.D. 316, 322 (N.D. Ill. 1979); City of 
Detroit v. Grinnell Corp., 495 F.2d 448 (2nd Cir. 1974). 
Indeed, a proposed settlement is a compromise. Even if 
the cause of action asserted by the Plaintiffs was meritori- 
ous and would be successful, there is no guarantee that 
the monies recovered would be equal to or exceed the 
funds received in settlement or would exceed the value of 
the corporate therapeutics involved in the presently pro- 
posed settlement. 
Thus, the objections or Mr. and Mrs. 
Pauly are no different from potential objections to the 
settlement of any litigation where liability is contested, 
and the District Court should overrule those objections. 
Mr. Gilbert and Mr. Glotzer present an objection, as- 
serting that the corporate therapeutics, while perhaps ben- 
eficial, “do not go far enough”. 
In essence, Mr. Gilbert 
and Mr. Glotzer ask the District Court to impose an addi- 
tional term into the settlement, requiring cumulative vot- 
ing. Mr. Gilbert and Mr. Glotzer state that approximately 
twenty-five percent (25%) of the shareholders voting in 
the most recent election voted for a proposal which would 
require cumulative voting, but they fail to acknowledge 
fully that the vast majority of shareholders voting on the 
issue voted against such a proposal. 
Furthermore, 
the 
term which these individuals propose to include in the 
settlement has not been the subject of negotiation, nor 
has it been the subject of the notice sent to the share- 
holders. 
Quite simply, this term might cause the number 
of objectors to increase, something which the District 
Court cannot divine. 
It is not up to the District Court 
to reopen and enter into negotiations in the hope of im- 
proving or changing the settlement in order to meet ob- 
jections. Levin v. Mississippi River Corp., 59 F.R.D. 353, 
361 (S.D.N.Y. 1973). The settlement which is presently 
before the District Court is a matter which was the sub- 
ject of intense negotiations and compromise. 
The addi- 
tional term which is requested by Messrs. Gilbert and 
275 
Glotzer was not the subject of those negotiations, and is 
not properly brought within the settlement proposed. 
For these reasons, the District Court should overrule 
the objections to the proposed settlement and approve the 
settlement in the form presented by the parties. 
CONCLUSION 
The Settlement presently before the District Court is 
beneficial to ADM and its shareholders. Furthermore, the 
settlement terminates the risk, uncertainty, and distraction 
which accrues from all shareholder derivative litigation. 
Finally, the settlement provides benefit to the sharehold- 
ers of ADM which benefit might not otherwise be avail- 
able from litigation, and the corporate therapeutics which 
are included within the settlement most certainly would 
not be available solely as a result of continued litigati 
even if successful. 
— 
The objections, filed to date which are directed to the 
substance of the settlement, as opposed to the potential 
claim for attorney's fees, lack merit in their entirety. One 
objection would effectively bar settlement in any litiga- 
tion requiring court approval where liability is contested. 
The other objection involves a request by certain share- 
holders to require cumulative voting, which request was 
opposed by a majority of the shareholders of ADM during 
the last election where this issue was raised. 
If cumula- 
je a. is tor ee at ADM, such is more

democratic voti

AFFIDAVIT OF MICHAEL P. DOOLEY 
Michael P. Dooley, being duly sworn, deposes and 
says: 
1. I am the William S. Potter Professor of Law at the 
University of Virginia. 
I have taught Corporation Law 
and related subjects since 1968 with a special emphasis on 
corporate governance. Among the courses I have taught 
are Corporate Governance and Finance and Courts and 
Corporate Governance, the latter in the Law School’s 
LL.M. Judge’s Program. I am the Reporter for the Amer- 
ican Bar Association’s Model Business Corporation Act 
and a member of the ABA’s Committee on Corporate 
Laws and Corporate Practice Committee. 
I have also 
served as a member of the ABA’s liaison committee for 
the American Law Institute’s Corporate Governance Proj- 
ect and as a member of the Legal Advisory Committee to 
the New York Stock Exchange. I have been a consultant 
to or expert witness for a number of publicly held corpo- 
rations on a variety of corporate governance matters and 
have served in similar capacities for the Attorney General 
of the Commonwealth of Virginia and the Virginia Gen- 
eral Assembly. My recent publications in this area include 
“Two Models of Corporate Governance,” 47 Bus. Law. 
461 (1992), a book, Fundamentals of Corporation Law 
(Foundation Press, 1995), and a monograph, A Practical 
Guide for Corporate Directors (National Association of 
Corporate Directors, 1996). A copy of my resume is 
attached and incorporated by reference. 
2. In December, 1995 and January, 1996 I acted as 
a consultant to the Governance Committee of the Board 
277 
of Directors 
of Archer 
Daniels 
Midland 
Company 
(ADM). The Committee, consisting of five independent 
directors, was created by the Board at its October, 1995 
meeting following the Annual Meeting of Shareholders. 
The Committee was appointed in response to a number 
of issues that had been raised by shareholders in litigation, 
in direct communications to the Board before and during 
the Annual Meeting and in shareholder resolutions pro- 
posed for the Proxy Statement. 
In general, the issues 
raised by shareholders were related to perceptions that the 
ADM Board was insufficiently independent of manage- 
ment and lacked the necessary structures to assure effective 
oversight. These criticisms were echoed in the press. Ac- 
cordingly, the Committee was charged with conducting a 
critical review of ADM’s corporate governance structures 
and practices and making recommendations for changes 
that would reflect the best practices followed by other 
leading American corporations. 
To that end, the Com- 
mittee surveyed the corporate governance structures of 
other corporations, studied recent changes that other large 
corporations had adopted and conducted an exhaustive re- 
view of the corporate governance literature from confer- 
ences and business and academic publications. 
3. The 
Committee’s 
deliberations 
resulted 
in 
the 
attached Governance Committee Report, which was unani- 
mously approved by the Committee members for consid- 
eration by the full Board of Directors at its January 15, 
1996 meeting. 
The Report recommended far reaching 
changes in the governance practices of ADM, all of which 
were designed to increase the effectiveness of the Board’s 
independent oversight functions and to move ADM to a 
leadership 
position 
in corporate 
governance. 
Among 
other things, the Report called for a reduction in Board 
size, limitations on the number of management directors 
and a commitment to a majority of “outside directors” 
on the Board and delegation of important oversight re- 
sponsibilities to committees comprised of a majority of 
outside directors. 
The Report was initially presented to 

278 
and discussed at a pre-Boatd meeting on January 14, 
1996 (which I attended) and unanimously approved by 
the Board at its regular meeting the following day. 
4. 
It was recognized that many of the Report’s recom- 
mendations could not be implemented immediately but 
would be phased in over time. Several important changes 
were made in the months immediately following adoption 
of the Report, however. 
These included a reduction in 
Board size from 17 to 12 members as of the October 
1996 Annual Meeting, the resignation of all management 
directors except the Chief Executive Officer, the adoption 
of a mandatory retirement age for directors joining the 
board as of the 1996 Annual Meeting and the nomina- 
tion (and subsequent election) 
of three new “outside 
directors.” 
5. I have reviewed the additional changes in corporate 
governance procedures that are proposed in Exhibit “A” 
to the Settlement Agreement. 
In my opinion, the pro- 
posed Settlement will strengthen the 1996 Governance 
Committee Report’s commitment to effective and inde- 
pendent Board oversight by implementing some of the 
Report’s recommendations and enhancing others. 
6. Comparing the ways in which the ADM Board op- 
erated prior to the filing of these shareholder derivative 
suits and the manner in which it will operate upon ap- 
proval of the proposed Settlement, some of the more 
important changes are as follows: 
(i) 
Inclusion 
in the By-laws of the principles 
establishing 
the basic relationship between 
the Board and management. 
(ii) A majority of the Board will consist of di- 
rectors meeting a more restrictive and compre- 
hensive definition of “outside director” that 
meets or exceeds accepted legal standards for 
director independence. 
(v) 
(vi) 
(vii) 
legal compliance and information and re

of

7. Upon approval of the Settlement, 
the corporate 
governance policies of ADM will have been completely 
transformed 
from 
those 
that existed 
in June, 
1995. 
Whereas ADM’s Board of Directors then consisted of 17 
members, most of whom had served for many years and 
many of whom also had management positions, the new 
ADM Board consists of 13 members with 
a clear majority 
of outside directors. Whereas the Chief Executive Officer 
was then seen to dominate the Board, the office of Chief 
Executive is now clearly accountable to the Board in fact, 
as well as theory, and the CEO is the only management 
director on the Board. The decisive role in the new ADM 
Board will be played by its outside directors who will 
consitute both a majority in numbers and the exclusive 
membership of the key standing Committees of the Board. 
Through the adoption of a mandatory retirement age and 
regular self-assessment, ADM’s governance structure will 
also insure against stagnation and complacency in the 
years 
to come. The new structure also directly addresses 
what were perceived to be deficiencies in ADM’'s com- 
munications with its shareholders and other important 
constituencies. A newly created Public Policy Committee 
will be responsible for assuring that ADM meets its social 
responsibilities and for regularly communicating with in- 
stitutional investors, analysts and others regarding those 
important issues. 
8. While ADM’s proposed governance 
structure 
re- 
flects the coasensus view of best corporate practice, it goes

VERNANCE COMMITTEE REPORT TO THE 
sain 
BOARD OF DIRECTORS OF 
ARCHER DANIELS MIDLAND COMPANY 
Archer 
Daniels Midland Company has 
The Mission 
of 
Arc 
firm that produces 
m agriculture at the lowest cost possible and in 
mp be at is responsive to the ethical, legal, institu- 
tional, and social priorities of society. The gps 
purpose is to add value to raw materials to f 
|! 
world. We have succeeded in our mission, and we in- 
tend to continue to do so in a way that maximizes vad 
holder return over time. With the likely doubling 
world’s population over the next twenty-five years, Ms 
have a special responsibility both to the developing — 
and the developed world to be the “Supermrset to 
World.” 
This is the challenge that we, as a Board - 
Directors, must shoulder in leading ADM into the nex 
century. 
As we look into the next century, we see an oppor- 
tunity to establish policies aS 
governance that facilitate our corporate “yy 
following principles will guide our actions in the coming 
years. 
Succeeding 
Boards 
of Directors 
periodically 
should review these policies, and the effectiveness of our 
corporate procedures, to assure that the Company has 
the governance structures necessary to achieve our goals 
profitably and responsibly and that the Board remai 
accountable to the shareholders for its actions. 
Principal Duties of the Board. 
. 
: 
the 
The Board of Directors is to oversee, not manage, 
business and affairs of the Company. The Board he aoe 
a 
ra 
and appropriately compensate 
agement of the Company. 
The Board should develop 
287 
and review management succession plans and approve 
the major strategies and financial objectives of the Com- 
pany. The Board should provide advice and counsel to 
senior management, and select and recommend to share- 
holders an appropriate slate of candidates for the Board 
of Directors. 
The Board should periodically 
review its 
own performance, its committee structure, the quality of 
its communications with management, and its handling 
of critical issues of corporate planning and leadership 
succession in management and on the Board itself. 
Independence of the Board of Directors. 
The Board of Directors should have appropriate struc- 
tures and policies to ensure that the Board acts at all 
times in the best interests of the Company and its share- 
holders. The Company looks to outside directors to pro- 
vide an independent view of the performance of the Com- 
pany and its management. 
Accordingly, to assure the 
highest standards of independent judgment and account- 
ability, a majority of the Board should be comprised of 
outside directors. 
Outside and Nonmanagement Directors Defined. 
An “outside director” means a director who (a) is 
neither a current employee nor a former member of 
senior management of the Company; (b) does not 
have any personal business or professional relation- 
ship with the Company that is material to it or to 
the director; (c) does not have a close family rela- 
tionship with any member of senior management; 
and (d) is not receiving compensation from the Com- 
pany in any capacity other than as a director. 
A 
anagement director” means a director who is 
not a current employee of the Company. 
Management and the Board. The Chief Executive 
Officer should be a member of the Board of Di- 
rectors. In order to assure the greatest number of 

288 
outside directors on a board of manageable size, other 
direct management representation should be kept to 
a minimum and should, in no event, exceed one or 
two other management directors. 
The Board of Directors should make clear to sen- 
ior management of the Company that board member- 
ship is neither necessary to their present positions nor 
a prerequisite to a higher management position in the 
Company. Attendance of management staff at Board 
Meetings should be at the discretion of the Chairman 
but should be encouraged by the Board. The Board 
should have full and direct access to members of 
senior management and should be encouraged to re- 
quest reports directly to the Board by any member of 
senior management. Board members should use judg- 
ment in dealings with management so that they do 
not distract management from the business operations 
of the Company. 
Conflicts of Interest. 
A director’s personal finan- 
cial or family relationship may occasionally give rise 
to that director’s material personal interest on a par- 
ticular issue. The Board of Directors should deter- 
mine whether such a conflict of interest exists on a 
case-by-case basis. The Board should take appropri- 
ate steps to identify such potential conflicts and to 
assure that a majority of the directors voting on an 
issue is disinterested with respect to that issue. 
Leadership of the Board. 
Chairman of the Board. The Board of Directors 
should decide annually whether it is in the best in- 
terests of the Company that the Office of Chairman 
of the Board and Chief Executive Officer of the Com- 
pany be separate or vested in the same person. 
Lead Director. 
Vf the offices of Chairman and 
Chief Executive Officer are combined in one person, 
289 
the outside directors should annually elect one of 
their number to serve as Lead Director. 
The Lead 
Director should preside over all meetings of nonman- 
agement directors and should perform such other 
duties and functions as the outside directors may 
assign. 
The nonmanagement directors should meet 
together at least annually, without management pres- 
ent, to evaluate Company performance. 
Board Composition. 
It is important that the Board include members with 
the personal qualities and business experience that taken 
together will assure a strong Board of Directors. 
The 
principal qualities of an effective corporate director in- 
clude strength of character, an inquiring and independent 
mind, practical wisdom and mature judgment. 
Special 
emphasis should be placed on diversity of the Board 
of Directors. 
The Board should include members who 
enable the Company to maintain relationships with cus- 
tomers and other constituencies and to respond to de- 
velopments in the markets in which the Company operates. 
The Board may vary in size and composition as circ 
stances require. The Board should consist of a nr 
arcana 
of nine members and a maximum of fifteen members. 
Current nonmanagement directors younger than seventy 
and new nonmanagement directors should not be candi- 
dates for the Board of Directors 
after reaching their 
seventieth birthdays. The Board should reduce its current 
size to reflect a majority of outside directors by the next 
Annual Meeting, and any interim board member appoint- 
ment should be made in a manner 
i

consistent with these

of

Evaluation of the Chief Executive Officer. 
All nonmanagement directors should participate in the 
establishment and approval of performance criteria for 
evaluation of the CEO. The Chairman of the Board of 
Directors, if separate from the CEO, or the Lead Director, 
should preside over an annual evaluation of the perform- 
ance and compensation of the CEO. All nonmanagement 
directors should participate in the establishment and ap- 
proval of performance criteria for this evaluation and in 
the actual evaluation of the CEO. Performance in rela- 
tion to the established criteria should be used to determine 
the compensation of the CEO. 
Communication with Shareholders. 
Primary responsibility for communication with share- 
holders should rest with the CEO. The CEO should un- 
dertake shareholder communications on all matters except 
board policies, in which matters the Chairman of the 
Board may be the more appropriate person to handle the 
communications if the offices of Chairman and CEO are 
separate. Members of the Board of Directors may, from 
time to time, be called upon by the Chairman or the 
CEO to assist in such communications. 
Procedures for Meetings. 
Board members should plan to attend all board meet- 
ings and all meetings of committees to which they have 
been appointed. Agenda and materials submitted for the 
information of, or for action by, the Board should be 
submitted to each member at least ten days in advance of 
the scheduled meeting, if possible. 
Similarly, such ma- 
terials prepared for comr-ittee meetings should be dis- 
tributed to committee members at least ten days in ad- 
vance of scheduled committee meetings. The Board and 
its committees should schedule a sufficient number of 
291 
meetings to allow them to carry out their responsibilities. 
Board members should review all materials prior to sched- 
uled meeetings and should be prepared 
to contribute 
substantively to each meeting. An individual member is 
entitled to add to the agenda for board or committee 
meetings any matter that the director reasonably believes 
should be on the agenda. Board members are expected to 
maintain the confidentiality of all materials desi 
as confidential.

The Audit Committee. 
The Audit Committee of 
the Board of Directors should review the over all plan 
of independent audit of the Company, the scope of 
audit procedures, the Company’s financial statements, 
the performance of the Company’s independent ac-

of material dispute and to discuss with management 
the Company’s major financial risk exposures. 
The

formance.

The Public Policy Committee. 
After its present 
assignment is completed, the Governance Commit- 
tee should become a committee of social responsi- 
bility designated as the Public Policy Committee. 
This committee would oversee matters of legal com-

all employees regarding

Technical Advisory Council. 
The Board of Directors encourages the CEO, in his 
discretions, to create a Technical Advisory Council. The 
Council would consist of outstanding men and women 
who are not members of the Board of Directors but whose 
special expertise in matters related to the Company’s busi- 
ness may be helpful to the Company. 
Board Compensation. 
Each year the Compensation and Succession Commit- 
tee should review the status of board members’ compen- 
sation in relation to the practices of similar, large com- 
panies and make recommendations 
for the following year 
to be considered 
by the full Board. The level of compen- 
sation paid should take into account the responsibilities 
and risks of board service and should be commensurate 
with the compensation 
of directors at similar companies. 
Compensation should be directed toward tying board per- 
formance to Company performance so that board mem- 
bers have a financial incentive to act in the best long-term 
interests 
of the Company.

IN THE UNITED STATES DISTRICT COURT

OBJECTIONS OF 
CALIFORNIA PUBLIC EMPLOYEES’ RETIREMENT 
SYSTEM AND FLORIDA STATE BOARD OF 
ADMINISTRATION TO PROPOSED SETTLEMENT 
OF DERIVATIVE ACTIONS 
Preliminary Statement 
By Order dated May 30, 1997, the Court invited com- 
ments from all sharehoders of defendant Archer Daniels 
Midland Company (“ADM”) on a proposed Stipulation 
of Settlement dated May 29, 1997 (“Settlement”) that 
would, if approved, provide for an award of as much as 
$4 million in payments to the plaintiffs’ lawyers and ex- 
perts (more than half the total cash consideration of the 
Settlement, with the remainder to go toward other law- 
yers’ fees) and the adoption of substandard corporate 
governance policies that the company, in any event, had 
for the most part already agreed to adopt. 
It is not surprising that the lawyers seek to rush this 
deal through the Court;? it cannot pass careful scrutiny. 
1 The Court, urged by the lawyers who stand to benefit most (if 
not exclusively) from the proposed Settlement, has provided little 
time within which the truly interested parties—the shareholders— 
may object. Under the schedule proposed by the lawyers and ap- 
proved by the Court, the shareholders had only to July 3, 1997, to 
lodge their written objections, with the hearing on final approval 
set for only four days later (July 7). Because the notice of this 
proposed Settlement was provided to many major holders (such as 
297 
It benefits only the lawyers, and at the expense 
ADM shareholders who have already been ill-served by 
both ADM'’s board and plaintiffs’ lawyers. The Coun 
should derail this speeding (gravy) train. It should insist 
on careful consideration, 
after proper notice to share- 
holders and an adequate opportunity for them to submit 
reasoned objections and materials in support of such 
objections. Such careful scrutiny will show that what is 
Proposed as a victory for the ADM sharehoders is, in 
act, gorging by lawyers in return for acquiescence to 
unacceptance governance practices. 
Interests 
of the Objectors

ees’ Retirement Sys-

(“SBA”) is also a large public pension fund, 
with 
assets 
having a market value of over $83.3 ballon fos the 
Florida Retirement System Trust Fund, the Local Gov. 
ernment Surplus Funds Trust Fund, and other various 
‘rust funds on behalf of 586,796 employees and 149,571 
and be allowed meaningful participaton in the hearing. 
Bell At- 
lantie Corp. v. Bolger, 2 F.3d 1304, 1314-15 (3d Cir. 1998).

Objectors CalPERS and SBA are, on information and 
belief, the largest ADM shareholders that have taken 
any public position on the proposed Settlement. 
Lawyers for the nominal plaintiffs contacted the Coun- 
cil of Institutional Investors (“Council”)—a prominent

members control more than $1 trillion in 
ing this litigation. CalPERS 
and SBA, who are both

shareholders would object to the proposal as inadequate 
and unfair 
to ADM shareholders, for the reasons stated 
in this memorandum. 
ADM has ignored these objections 
and instead has pressed forward for Court approval of 
the proposed Settlement. See Declaration 
of Ann L.

THE PROPOSED SETTLEMENT SHOULD BE 
REJECTED AS INADEQUATE AND UNFAIR 
TO THE SHAREHOLDERS OF ADM 
It is a matter of public record that the management 
of ADM as been accused of, and has admitted culpa- 
bility to, grave misconduct. See Declaration 
of Steven 
F. Benz (“Benz Decl.”), Exhiabits B-J (collecting press 
reports of ADM plea and settlement agreements).

to the corporation 
and its shareholders. Counsel 
for the

Having done little but wait, the lawyers then entered 
into what can only be characterized as a sweetheart deal 
with defendants: Defendants obtain Court-sanctioned re- 
leases for virtually all of their misconduct in return for 
(1) payment of a total of $8 million (with up to 49 per- 
cent of this—and perhaps more, if interest is included— 
earmarked for the attorneys), Settlement at 15; and (2) 
adoption of a diluted set of “corporate governance” prin- 
ciples that not only lag behind those of ADM’s corporate 
peers but improve little if at all on reforms that were 
agreed upon prior to, and independent of, the Settlement. 
Id. at 13. 
Under the settled standards of Rule 23.1 for judicial 
review of proposed settlements 
(Point I, infra), 
this 
Settlement cannot possibly pass muster. 
First, the Settle- 
ment provides virtually all of its benefits to the attorneys 
for the nominal plaintiffs. 
(Point II, infra). Second, the 
supposed jewel of the proposed Settlement—the exalted 
governance principles—are neither jewels nor the fruits 
of the lawyers’ labor. 
These principles, which are sub- 
stantially 
weaker 
than 
those 
now 
routinely 
adopted 
throughout corporate America, were for the most part 
implemented by ADM long before the Settlement was 
reached. 
(Point III, infra). 
Moreover, the proposed Settlement represents a squan- 
dered opportunity to enact truly meaningful governance 
for ADM—a company perhaps more in need of it than 
any other public company in the United States. Close to 
half of the ADM shareholders voted for rigorous and 
meaningful standards of director independence at the last 
annual meeting. 
The lawyers’ willingness to settle for 
watered-down rules, in what appears to be quid pro quo 
for enormous and unjustified fees, strongly suggests that 
the Settlement was collusive and is not in the sharehold- 
ers’ best interests. 
In any event, the unseemly rush to approval should be 
halted; if the Court does not reject it outright, the Court’s 
301 
obligation under Rule 23.1 to conduct an informed and 
independent review of the proposed Settlement, and to 
hear the objections of shareholders who have only re- 
cently been informed of this resolution, require the de- 
ferral of any final decision to approve the Settlement until 
after all interested parties have been provided with an 
appropriate opportunity to present their objections, and 
materials supporting the objections, to the Court. Since 
this opportunity has not yet been provided, and the sched- 
uled hearing (on July 7) will not provide the share- 
holders with a fair opportunity to contest this unfair pro- 
posal, the Court should defer the hearing for at least 
one month. 
I. THE COURT MUST EXERCISE INDEPENDENT 
JUDGMENT TO DETERMINE WHETHER THE 
PROPOSED SETTLEMENT IS FAIR 
Under Rule 23.1, the “action shall not be dismissed 
or compromised without the approval of the court... .” 
“Under FRCP 23.1, the court has a duty to determine 
whether the settlement reasonably protects the interests 
of the corporation and its shareholders.” 
In re Oracle 
Sec. Litig., 829 F. Supp. 1176, 1179, 1186 (N.D. Cal. 
1993), citing Mathes v. Roberts, 85 F.R.D. 710, 713 
(S.D.N.Y. 1980). In reviewing a proposed settlement of 
a derivative action, “[t]he generally accepted standard 
is that the agreement must be fair and adequate in light 
of the interests of all the parties.” 7C C. Wright, A. 
Miller & M. Kane, Federal Practice and Procedure § 1839 
at 182 (1986 & Supp. 1997). Before approving the pro- 
posed Settlement, the Court “must consider its fairness, 
reasonableness, and adequacy.” Moore’s Federal Practice 
3d § 23.1.10[2}[a] at 23.1-58 (1997). 
The Court “must also ensure that there are no fraudu- 
lent or collusive features of the settlement.” 
Jd. 
(“De- 
rivative actions pose the inherent risk that the representa- 
tive plaintiff will collude with the defendant to forego the 

302 
derivative claims in exchange for a settlement that is only 
favorable to the representative plaintiff and his or her 
attorneys.”); Wright, Miller & Kane, supra, § 1839 at 
182-84 (“Settlement proposals 
. 
. 
. that do not appear 
to have been reached as the result of arms length nego- 
tiations 
. 
. 
. will not be approved.”); id. at 191 (“the 
court should be on the lookout for signs of bad faith 
or collusion among the parties to the proposed compro- 
mise”). 
As Mr. Coffee has observed, many derivative 
actions result in settlements that “benefit[] the attorneys 
more than the shareholder.” John C. Coffee, Jr., Under- 
standing the Plaintiffs Attorney: The Implications of Eco- 
nomic Theory for Private Enofrcement of Law Through 
Class and Derivative Actions, 86 Col. L. Rev. 669, 673 
n.9 (1986). 
The burden here is entirely on the proponents of the 
Settlement. 
“The proponents of the settlement have the 
burden of proving that the settlement is in the best in- 
terests of the corporation.” Moore’s Federal Practice 3d, 
supra, § 23.1.10{2}[a] at 23.1-58. 
It is the plaintiffs’ 
attorneys who must therefore prove the fairness and ade- 
quacy of the proposed settlement. Wright, Miller & Kane, 
supra, § 1839 at 184-86 (“The burden is on the pro- 
ponents of the compromise to show that it meets this 
standard, and the trial court should state its reasons for 
deciding to approve a settlement so as to provide adequate 
information for appellate review.”). 
Held to this exacting standard, the proposal here can- 
not possibly be found fair, reasonable and adequate in its 
benefits to ADM and its shareholders. 
Il. 
THE SETTLEMENT SHOULD BE REJECTED 
BECAUSE IT PROVIDES EXCESSIVE BENE- 
FITS TO THE NOMINAL PLAINTIFFS’ AT- 
TORNEYS 
The total cash consideration achieved in the proposed 
Settlement is $8 million. 
Settlement at 12-14. Of that 
303 
$8 million, up to 49 percent (more, in fact, if interest 
is included) is earmarked for payment to the attorneys 
for nominal plaintiffs. 
Id. at 15. The remainder of the 
cash is to be used for payment to “outside independent 
counsel” employed in some vague capacity to assist the 
ADM board members in discharge of their legal responsi- 
bilities. Settlement at 5 & Exhibit A. 
presumably to remind the ADM directors of their obliga- 
tion to follow the company’s bylaws and other legal re- 
quirements. No benefit to ADM or its shareholders is 
seriously posited from these payments, nor can any legiti- 
mate benefit plausibility be put forward. 
The only real 
beneficiaries of the arrangement, stated bluntly, are the 
lawyers. It is axiomatic that settlements provided exces- 
sive benefits to the lawyers cannot be approved under 
Rule 23.1. 
E.g., Rosenbaum 
v. MacAllister, 64 F.3d 
1439, 1443-48 (10th Cir. 1995) (rejecting under Rule 
23.1 disproportionate settlement benefitting attorneys); 
In re Pittsburgh & Lake Erie R.R. Co. Sec. and Antitrust 
Litig., 543 F.2d 1058, 1070 (3d Cir. 1976) (rejecting 
settlement where disproportionate cash benefit to plain- 
tiffs’ lawyers); see also Wright, Miller & Kane, supra, 
§ 1839 at 184-85 n.26 (collecting cases under Rule 23.1 
in which settlements have been rejected for this reason). 
The results do not warrant the excessive fees sought. 
Plaintiffs seek the Court’s approval of a settlement that 
provides virtually no cash benefit to the corporation—it 
sae of tame 
s The only purported benefit, en- 
“corporate governance” principles, 
resulted not from plaintiffs’ efforts but rather from the

Meeting.

Decl.”) § 10. 
Plaintiffs’ lawyers directly acknowledged 
that the Settlement’s governance principles were selected 
precisely because they would not require ADM to change 
its board at all. Yerger Decl. 44 9, 10. 
The effort expended does not justify the excessive fees. 
The attorneys’ own summary of the proceedings to date 
proves that they have done little, if anything, to prosecute 
the case.> After the filing of defendants’ motion to dis-

despite the known interest of many of ADM’s largest 
shareholders. 
These holders individually and collectively 
through the Council offered to assist in the prosecution 
of the case (and specifically to help structure necessary 
governance reform). 
When the Council provided com-

largely ignored.

the legitimacy of the Proposed Settlement. Large payouts 
to lawyers, coupled with purely spectral, supposed “struc-

8 Courts and commentaries have recognized that “fo]ne of the 
risks flowing from shareholders’ difficulty in monitoring derivative 
litigation is that plaintiffs’ counsel and the defendants will struc- 
ture a settlement such that the plaintiffs’ attorneys’ fees are dis- 
proportionate to any relief obtained for the corporation. 
Plaintiffs’ 
attorneys and the defendants may settle in a manner adverse to 
the interests of the plaintiffs by exchanging a low settlement for 
high fees.” Bolger, 2 F.3d at 1810. “At its worst, the settlement 
process may amount to a covert exchange for a high award of 
attorney’s fees.” Coffee, supra, n.2 at 714.

Objectors are thus confronted with the stark fact of

At the very least, objectors and other shareholders who

afforded a reasonable opportunity to contest this highly 
suspect resolution. 
Accordingly, if the Court does not reject outright this 
proposed Settlement—as it should, in light of the plainly 
excessive benefits to the attorneys—it should defer con- 
sideration of the Settlement until after all shareholders 
have been afforded a reasonable opportunity to be heard, 
with sufficient advance notice to permit them to study 
all of the relevant materials (including the lawyers’ fee 
petition) and to submit materials supporting their ob- 
jections sufficiently in advance of such hearing to permit 
the Court to consider them fully.

DECLARATION OF ANN L. YERGER 
Ann L. Yerger hereby declares pursuant to 28 U.S.C. 
§ 1746: 
1. I am over eighteen years of age and have personal 
knowledge 
of the matters stated herein. 
Council’s research service. The Council 
is located at 
1730 Rhode Island Avenue, Suite 512, Washington, D.C. 
20036. The Council is an association of more than 100 
public corporate and union pension funds, with more than 
$1 trillion in assets. I have been employed by the Council 
since January 1996. 
Prior to that I was employed for 
five years by the Investor Responsibility Research Center 
as 
ty Director of the Center’s Corporate Governance 
Service. 
3. On November 5, 1996, Jacob A. Goldberg of the 
law firm Spector & Roseman, P.C., called me at the Coun- 
cil of Institutional Investors to describe the Archer Daniels 
Midland Company derivative litigation. I discussed Coun- 
cil members’ ongoing concerns about the independence of 
ADM’s board of directors. 
4. About 
a month later, Mr. Goldberg phoned to set 
up a meeting with the Council. In response to my request 
for a written summary of the lawsuit, he mailed a letter 
dated December 15, 1996 to the Council’s 
offices. A true
