9. I immedately contacted Mr. Roseman to explain 
the Council’s concern with the proposed settlement defini- 
tion and to clarify the impact of the materiality hurdles. 
In response to my criticism of the proposed definition, Mr. 
Roseman told me that ADM refused to accept the Coun- 
cil’s more stringent definition of “outside” director. 
He 
explained that, under the Council’s definition, the company 
would be forced 
to remove certain directors from 
its 
board. 
10. I asked Mr. Roseman to provide a written analysis 
of ADM’s board using the proposed definition. Mr. Rose- 
man’s response is attached hereto as Exh. B. According 
to Mr. Roseman’s analysis, the only ADM directors who 
do not qualify as “outside” directors are company chair- 
man Dwayne Andreas, CEO G. Allen Andreas and Shreve 
Archer, father of ADM’s treasurer. 
All of the remaining 
10 board members qualify as “outside” directors under the 
proposed settlement’s definition, even though four direc- 
tors—two attorneys whose law firms provide legal services 
to the company, and two executives of companies or their 
affiliates that do business with ADM—have ongoing busi- 
ness relationships with ADM and accordingly are beholden 
to management. 
These four directors do not qualify as 
independent “outside” directors under the Council’s defi- 
nition or any other generally accepted standard of true 
independence. 
11. ADM has been a poster child for the corporate 
governance movement for years. 
The settlement of this 
derivative action offers an unprecedented opportunity to 
implement changes in ADM’s bylaws that will benefit 
ADM and its shareholders. 
This opportunity should not 
be squandered. 
12. I do not believe that the proposed settlement is 
fair, reasonable, adequate, or in the best interest of ADM 
and its shareholders. Indeed, I do not believe that the pro- 
posed settlement offers any significant benefit to ADM or 
its shareholders.

DECLARATION OF SARAH A.B. TESLIK 
Sarah A.B. Teslik hereby declares pursuant to 28 U.S.C. 
§ 1746: 
1. I am over eighteen years of age and have personal 
knowledge of the matters stated herein. 
2. I have been the Executive Director, attorney and 
principal staffer for the Council of Institutional Investors 
(the “Council”) since its formation in 1985. The Council 
is a non-profit association of corporate, public and Taft- 
Hartley pension funds that addresses investment issues. 
Council members invest over a trillion dollars on behalf 
of millions of employees and beneficiaries. 
Objectors 
California Public Employees’ Retirement System and the 
Florida State Board of Administration are members of 
the Council. 
3. The Council is a very deliberate and cautious or- 
ganization that only adopts policies on essentially unani- 
mous votes, and only after careful research and discus- 
sion. The Council has adopted fewer than a dozen cor- 
porate governance policies in all of its years of existence. 
Each of these has been so basic that every one of the 
Council’s corporate members has voted in favor of each 
of the policies put up to them for a vote, and most of the 
policies are standard operating procedure at most or many 
of the nation’s largest companies. 
4. The first and probably the most central of the Coun- 
cil’s policies is its requirement that a majority of a cor- 
317 
Poration’s directors be independent from potentially com- 
promising financial, familial or professional relationships 
to the company. 
The Council defines independence for 
these purposes in a detailed definition which, in essence, 
defines a director as independent if his or her only non- 
trivial connection to the board is his or her board seat. 
Members adopted this definition after they agreed that a 
board was much less likely to perform properly its central 
function as an independent oversight body if most of its 
individual members were not, in fact, independent. Without 
this foundation policy, members felt most other corporate 
governance standards and procedures were meaningless. 
pe — oy oe were adopted with unanimous 
votes 
on 
il 
5, 1991. Aco 
i 
icy 
i 
hereto as Exhibit “A”. 
ee 
eee 
_ 5. The other central policy of the Council is its posi- 
tion on confidential voting. 
Directors, of course, 
are 
elected by shareholders, and so the election process is the 
vital link that provides meaningful accountability to share- 
holders. When this policy was debated, members agreed 
unanimously that this link is weakened, threatened. 
or 
shattered if those who run for office count their own 
ballots. That is an election practice that members did not 
believe would be tolerated in any other part of American 
and should not be tolerated in corporate elections 
either. 
6. The Council therefore on June 23, 1989 unani- 
mously adopted a policy, attached hereto as Exhibit “B” 
supporting a confidential ballot—supporting the idea in 
other words, that neutral third parties selected by ‘the 
corporation should count shareholders’ proxies. With in- 
creasing numbers of employee and institutional service 
provider shareholders who could suffer retribution if they 
dared to cast votes against management, members be- 
lieved that this policy 
would onl 
i 
pervgder. 
cy 
y become more important 

318 
7. The Council’s majority standard for board inde- 
pendence is so universally accepted that it is the standard 
now used by nearly 85 percent (84.7 percent) of S&P 
500 companies. 
Indeed Council data indicate that only 
17 companies of the 483 member of the S&P 500 surveyed 
by the Council have fewer independent directors than 
ADM. 
8. In the proposed settlement ADM offers an inde- 
pendence standard and definition that are, in my opinion 
(a) completely meaningless because of clever modifying 
language; (b) virtually identical to changes already pro- 
posed by the company more than a year ago, and with- 
out reference to the derivative lawsuit; (c) worse (less 
protective of shareholders) than the standards already in 
place at all but 15 percent of S&P 500 companies; and 
(d) so completely dated in language as to be archaic. 
9. The proposed settlement provides that directors will 
be deemed to be “outside” even if they have personal, 
business, or professional ties to the company and/or its 
senior management, unless the relationship is deemed by 
the company to be “material to the company or to the 
director.” 
With a company the size of ADM, I cannot 
imagine a single relationship any one person could ever 
have, personal, professional, or otherwise, to ADM that 
would be material to ADM. 
This standard is therefore, 
in my opinion, a standard that virtually no director can 
fail. And yet it was Council members’ belief that even 
the smallest of financial or personal relationships can 
genuinely affect a person’s independence because of its 
potential to affect the person. 
10. The company offers this can’t-fail-to-be-met stand- 
ard despite the fact that the widely accepted Council 
standard was already submitted to it by two of its largest 
shareholders, and two of the largest Council members— 
the California Public Employees’ Retirement System and 
the Florida State Board of Administration—and received 
an exceptionally high endorsement of 41.6 percent of 
319 
voting shareholders at the company’s October 17, 1996 
annual meeting. 
11. The offer in the proposed settlement that direct 
management representation on the board will be limited 
to no more than three seats was lifted almost verbatim 
from the Governance Committee Report to the Board of 
Directors of Archer Daniels Midland Company (Jan. 15 
1996), which is attached hereto as Exhibit “C”. This 
Committee was established and acted prior to the filing 
of the lawsuit to which this proposed settlement is the 
proposed conclusion. The ADM board adopted all of the 
recommendations in the Report. 
See Exhibit “E”. 
So 
shareholders gain nothing from this proposed settlement 
they did not already have. And they did not have any- 
thing innovtaive here in any event: according to Council 
data, this company suggestion is standard operating pro- 
cedure at most S&P 500 companies anyway—86.5 percent 
have three or fewer executives on the board. 
12. Similarly the concept of a “lead director” seems 
to me to be another essential repeat of a change already 
called for in the 1996 Report, with the renaming of the 
lead director as the vice chairman. A similar repackaging 
appears to have occurred with the the settlements proposal 
to establish a governance committee to assess board and 
committee effectiveness. Under current board policies the 
nominating committee is responsible for annual director 
self-assessment and board evaluation,” while all nonman- 
agement directors handle the “establishment and perform- 
ance criteria for evaluation of the CEO.” As a result, the 
creation of this separate corporate goverance committee 
does not appear to represent any meaningful innovation 
from current company practice. 
13. The proposed settlement’s requirement that 
- 
rate nominating, compensation and audit committees om 
be composed exclusively of outside directors was also 
lifted verbatim from the 1996 Report. 

320 
14. The proposed settlement does offer a version of 
confidential voting that was not contained in the Report. 
But it is, if anything, an even more meaningless provi- 
sion, in my opinion, than the definition of independence 
used for directors. The proposed settlement indicates the 
company will grant shareholders confidential voting—but 
only, it seems, in those situations in which it is irrelevant 
—only in situations where there are no proxy contests or 
solicitations. 
In these situations the proposed settlement 
indicates there will not be confidential voting. And, al- 
though it seems nearly irrelevant under the circumstances, 
the company also does not indicate whether in those trivial 
and routine situations in which votes will be tallied con- 
fidentially, the confidentiality will last only until the polls 
are closed or whether it is permanent. Other companies 
with confidential voting policies generally, in the Council’s 
experience, make this clear—it is a distinction that mat- 
ters a great deal, especially to individual employee share- 
holders. 
15. I have compared the governance provisions of the 
proposed settlement with the provisions already accepted 
by the board in the 1996 Report. 
This comparison is 
summarized in Exhibit “D” hereto. 
In my opinion, the 
few items in the settlement that cannot be found in the 
Report are trivial and provide no benefit to shareholders. 
On balance, the proposed settlement is no improvement 
over the governance provisions already accepted in the 
Report. I am unable, therefore, to identify new, positive, 
and meaningful corporate governance changes offered in 
the proposed settlement. 
This is particularly surprising 
and disappointing since I personally, numerous Council 
members individually, and other members of Council staff 
repeatedly made offers to ADM directors, consultants and 
other representatives to provide information on independ- 
ent directors and other core member and Council policies 
that could have formed the basis of a very limited and 
conservative but meaningful set of governance principles. 
vides no value to ADM or to its shareholders. However, 
truly independent board could add significant value.

WHY INVESTORS PUSH FOR 
STRONG CORPORATE BOARDS 
Manager's Journal 
By Jennifer H. van Heeckeren 
Trustees and staff of the California Public Employees’ 
Retirement System are developing guidelines to grade 
companies in Calpers’s portfolio on the independence of 
their boards. Calpers cannot be ignored: 
Its $80 billion 
in equities make it one of the nation’s largest shareholders 
and a force to be reckoned with in the governance arena. 
A study I conducted with Diane Del Guercio at the 
University of Oregon shows that Calpers has the power 
to provoke significant change, such as takeover attempts, 
turnover, restructurings and asset sales. 
CEOs would be wise to embrace the idea of strongly 
independent boards. 
For if investors can’t get boards to 
work well, they may turn to other measures—-sitting on 
boards themselves, lobbying for direct government regu- 
lation or supporting a return of corporate raiders. 
The growing power and independence of boards reflects 
a reversal of a trend that dates to the early years of this 
century. A seminal event occurred in 1914, when repre- 
sentatives of J.P. Morgan resigned from 30 company 
boards in a single day. They and other institutional in- 
vestors had held sway in boardrooms for more than two 
decades, leaving CEOs with little real decision-making 
power. But as they came under critical public and politi- 
cal scrutiny, they beat a hasty retreat. 
Family owners 
were also losing influence, as founders aged and owner- 
ship tilted toward outsiders. 
So a new group of pro- 
fessional CEOs, bred in new management schools, took 
the reins of corporate America. 

324 
stock they held. They have turned to boards—sharehold- 
ers’ elected representatives—as management watchdogs. 
What do these investors 
want? The bottom 
line is 
long-term returns. The most active investors are pension 
funds with mind-boggling obligations. 
Calpers will need 
to be able to pay out $30 billion each year to its bene- 
ficiaries by 2020. 
Institutional investors rely on strong, 
independent boards to create shareholder value. 
They 
believe strong boards will help companies correct mistakes, 
recover from crises, and find, support and reward out- 
standing CEOs. 
Numerous recent examples, along with academic re- 
search, support the notion that boards can have a signifi- 
cant impact on stock prices. 
Recent research by Mc- 
Kinsey in conjunction with Institutional Investor found 
that many large private money managers—especially 
those 
looking for long term value—are willing to pay a higher 
stock price for companies with good, independent board 
governance. We surveyed 50 money managers represent- 
ing about $850 billion in assets, and found that they 
were willing 
to pay an 11% good-governance premium on 
average. 
How should CEOs respond to the demand for strong, 
independent boards? Well-performing companies have the 
option of doing nothing for now—but only until there is a 
period of poor results, a complex acquisition, or a crisis. 
CEOs could search for ways to thwart investors, as they 
did in the 1980s. But underlying forces make this strategy 
unpromising. Institutional 
ownership will probably con- 
tinue 
to increase. Public opinion is suspicious 
of CEOs, 
whose pay has dramatically increased while companies 
more sympathetic—not 
the “greedy raiders” of the °80s, 
but institutions like Calpers, which pays the pensions of 
firemen, police officers and school librarians. Politicians 
aren't likely to attack these investors on behalf of CEOs. 
rege 
Pizo 
Fe Hitt 
Wily a! | 
z 
energy on substantive 
business issues. CEOs will 
t 
that investors are much more patient and under- 
in 
times if they trust the board. And while 
may rubber-stamp the CEO’s decisions when 
often its first response to a crisis is to fire 
a strong board with substantive 
knowl- 
Strategy and company performance will tend to 
management.

ner, contributed to this article.

move this Court for an Order dismissing the joint appeal

The petition herein for a writ of certiorari to the United 
States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is granted 
limited to Question 1 presented by the petition. The 
brief of the petitioner is to be filed with the Clerk and 
served upon opposing counsel on or before 3 p.m., Tues- 
day, November 10, 1998. The brief of respondents 
is to 
be filed with the Clerk and served upon opposing counsel 
on or before 3 p.m., Tuesday, December 8, 1998. 
A 
reply brief, if any, is to be filed with the Clerk and served 
upon opposing counsel on or before 3 p.m., Tuesday, 
December 29, 1998. 
Rule 29.2 does not apply. Justice 
O’Connor took no part in the consideration or decision of 
this petition.
